Helm — The Kubernetes Package Manager

Complete Learning Guide for Certification & Beyond

Master Helm from scratch — charts, templates, releases, repositories, hooks, CI/CD, and production best practices. Using anihpj (Django) & jobpost as your hands-on lab.

🟢 Beginner-Friendly📦 Package Manager📝 Certification Prep🛠️ Hands-On Labs⛵ Helm v3

Welcome! This guide takes you from "What is Helm?" to certification-ready. We'll use anihpj (a Django job board app) as our real-world example throughout. Every concept is illustrated with diagrams, code blocks, and hands-on labs. No prior Helm knowledge needed.

🚀 anihpj Project Overview — Your Hands-On Lab Application

Throughout this guide, we use anihpj — a Django job board application — as our real-world example. Every concept, template, and command is demonstrated with this app, making abstract Helm concepts concrete and practical.

anihpj Architecture — Django + PostgreSQL + Redis
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ANIHPJ APPLICATION ARCHITECTURE                                          │
  │                                                                           │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │                     🌐 INGRESS (jobs.anihpj.com)                 │     │
  │  └─────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┘     │
  │                                │                                          │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────▼───────────────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │                     🐍 DJANGO APPLICATION                        │     │
  │  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────────────┐│     │
  │  │  │  Skills  │  │  User    │  │  Author  │  │  Location        ││     │
  │  │  │  Model   │  │  Model   │  │  Model   │  │  Model           ││     │
  │  │  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └────────┬─────────┘│     │
  │  │                                                     │          │     │
  │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────▼─────────┐│     │
  │  │  │                    📋 JobPost Model                        ││     │
  │  │  │  (title, description, skills, location, author, salary...) ││     │
  │  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│     │
  │  └─────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┘     │
  │                                │                                          │
  │          ┌─────────────────────┼─────────────────────┐                   │
  │          │                     │                     │                   │
  │  ┌───────▼───────┐    ┌───────▼───────┐    ┌───────▼───────┐          │
  │  │  🐘 PostgreSQL │    │  🔴 Redis     │    │  📁 Static    │          │
  │  │  (Primary DB)  │    │  (Cache/Queue)│    │  Files (S3)   │          │
  │  └───────────────┘    └───────────────┘    └───────────────┘          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
📦
Application: anihpj

Django 4.x web application for job postings. Runs with Gunicorn WSGI server on port 8000. Connects to PostgreSQL for data storage and Redis for caching/queues.

📋
Module: jobpost

The core Django app managing job listings. Models: Skills (tag system), User (auth), Author (profile), Location (geo), and JobPost (the listing itself with foreign keys to all above).

📖 How We Use anihpj in This Guide

Every Helm concept is demonstrated with anihpj: Charts package the Django app + its dependencies (PostgreSQL, Redis). Templates parameterize the deployment (replicas, image tag, DB credentials). Hooks run database migrations before upgrades. CI/CD pipelines automate the build-deploy-test cycle. By the end, you'll have a production-ready Helm chart you can adapt for your own applications.

🏗️ Implementation Summary — How It All Fits Together

A bird's-eye view of the complete Helm workflow — from chart creation to production deployment — and how all the pieces (Charts, Values, Templates, Hooks, CI/CD) work together in a real-world pipeline.

The Complete Helm Workflow — From Chart to Cluster
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  END-TO-END HELM WORKFLOW                                                 │
  │                                                                           │
  │  ┌─────────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ DEVELOP │    │ PACKAGE  │    │ DISTRIBUTE│    │ DEPLOY               │ │
  │  │ Chart   │───▶│ & Lint   │───▶│ & Push    │───▶│ to Kubernetes        │ │
  │  │         │    │          │    │           │    │                      │ │
  │  │ • Write │    │ helm lint│    │ helm push │    │ helm upgrade --install│ │
  │  │   tmpls │    │ helm pkg │    │  oci://.. │    │  anihpj ./chart \    │ │
  │  │ • Set   │    │          │    │           │    │  -f values-prod.yaml  │ │
  │  │   vals  │    │          │    │           │    │  -n production \      │ │
  │  └─────────┘    └──────────┘    └──────────┘    │  --atomic --wait      │ │
  │                                                  └──────────┬───────────┘ │
  │                                                             │            │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────▼──────────┐ │
  │  │                         KUBERNETES CLUSTER                           │ │
  │  │  ┌────────────┐  ┌────────────┐  ┌────────────┐  ┌────────────┐    │ │
  │  │  │ anihpj-dev │  │ anihpj-stg │  │ anihpj-prod│  │ Release    │    │ │
  │  │  │ namespace  │  │ namespace  │  │ namespace  │  │ Secrets    │    │ │
  │  │  │ replicas:1 │  │ replicas:2 │  │ replicas:5 │  │ (history)  │    │ │
  │  │  └────────────┘  └────────────┘  └────────────┘  └────────────┘    │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                           │
  │  🔑 KEY PRINCIPLE: One chart, many releases, different values.            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
📂
Namespace Layout

dev: Single replica, latest image, debug ON — fast iteration.
staging: 2 replicas, RC tag, mirrors prod config — final validation.
production: 5 replicas, pinned version, auto-scaling ON, monitoring enabled.

🔄
CI/CD Integration

Git push → Docker build → helm lint → helm template → deploy to staging → helm test → deploy to production. Every step automated. --atomic ensures auto-rollback on failure.

🎯 Comprehensive Exam Strategy — How to Pass the Helm Certification

This is your battle plan. The Helm certification is performance-based — knowing Helm isn't enough. You need speed, accuracy, and a systematic approach. Here's exactly how to approach the exam, what to do in the first 5 minutes, how to manage your time, and the mistakes that cost candidates points.

Exam Strategy — At a Glance
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  HELM CERTIFICATION — BATTLE PLAN                                         │
  │                                                                           │
  │  📋 FORMAT:  Performance-based, hands-on terminal                         │
  │  ⏱️  TIME:    120 minutes | ~60 questions | ~2 min per question           │
  │  🎯  PASS:    ~70% (estimated)                                            │
  │                                                                           │
  │  DOMAIN WEIGHTS:                                                          │
  │  ████████████████████████████░░░░░░  40%  Helm Core                       │
  │  ██████████████████░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  25%  Templates                       │
  │  ██████████████░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  20%  Advanced Features               │
  │  ██████████░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  15%  Production & CI/CD              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

⚡ First 5 Minutes — Critical Setup

1
Set Up Aliases (30 seconds)

alias h=helm; alias k=kubectl; alias hu='helm upgrade --install'. These save 2-3 seconds per command — over 60 questions, that's 2-3 minutes saved.

2
Enable Autocompletion (30 seconds)

source <(helm completion bash); source <(kubectl completion bash); complete -o default -F __start_kubectl k. Tab completion prevents typos and speeds up flag entry.

3
Configure Vim (30 seconds)

echo -e "set tabstop=2\\nset shiftwidth=2\\nset expandtab\\nset number" > ~/.vimrc. Proper YAML editing prevents indentation errors.

4
Read ALL Questions (3 minutes)

Skim every question before answering any. Categorize: Quick Wins (<2 min), Medium (2-5 min), Hard (5+ min). Start with Quick Wins to build momentum and confidence.

5
Check Your Context (30 seconds)

kubectl config current-context; kubectl cluster-info. Verify you're on the right cluster. Wrong cluster = 0 points for everything.

⏱️ 3-Pass Time Management Strategy

1️⃣
Pass 1: Quick Wins (0-35 min)

Answer everything you can do in <3 minutes. Multiple choice, simple CLI tasks. Flag everything else. Build momentum and bank easy points first.

2️⃣
Pass 2: Medium (35-90 min)

Tackle template writing, chart structure, value configuration. These are the bulk of your score. 5-minute max per question — flag and move if stuck.

3️⃣
Pass 3: Hard + Review (90-120 min)

Complex multi-step scenarios. Review flagged questions. Verify answers. NEVER leave a question unanswered — even a partial answer may earn partial credit.

✅ Do's and Don'ts — Exam Day Rules

Exam Day — What to Do & What to Avoid

✅ DO THIS

✅Always dry-run first: helm install test ./chart --dry-run --debug before every deploy
✅Use helm create: Never write chart structure from scratch — scaffold and customize
✅Verify namespace: Check the question for namespace requirements — wrong ns = 0 points
✅Use --atomic: Auto-rollback on failure saves time and prevents partial deployments
✅Read carefully: Every word in the question matters. "Create a chart named X in namespace Y" — all 3 requirements are scored

❌ DON'T DO THIS

❌Don't skip lint: helm lint takes 1 second and catches 80% of template errors
❌Don't hardcode values: Use {{ .Values.x }} not literal strings in templates
❌Don't spend >5 min: Flag and move on. 5 minutes on one question costs you 3 easy ones
❌Don't forget rollback: helm rollback <release> is your undo button — know it cold
❌Don't panic: Flag hard questions. Come back in Pass 3. Partial credit exists — attempt everything
🎯 Estimated Exam Readiness with This Guide: 85-95%

If you complete this guide thoroughly — all 20 chapters, 200+ practice questions, 5 hands-on labs, and the full exam simulation — you'll be exceptionally well-prepared. The guide covers 100% of certification domains with deep-dive content, practical examples, and exam-style Q&As. Supplement with hands-on practice in minikube/kind and you'll walk into the exam confident and ready.

Chapter 1: Understanding Helm — The Kubernetes Package Manager

Before you write a single helm install command, you need to understand why Helm exists, what problem it solves, and how it fits into the Kubernetes ecosystem. This chapter builds your mental model — the foundation everything else rests on. We'll use our anihpj/jobpost Django app to make every concept concrete and practical.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~2 hours 📖 Concepts 🎯 40% Exam Weight

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ The YAML copy-paste chaos that existed before Helm — and why it was unsustainable
  • ✅ What Helm actually is — the simple, clear definition with real-world analogies
  • ✅ The Shipping Port Analogy — understand Helm through a powerful visual metaphor
  • ✅ Helm 2 vs Helm 3 — the architecture revolution that removed Tiller
  • ✅ Helm's place in the CNCF ecosystem — graduated project status
  • ✅ Real-world companies and use cases — who uses Helm and why
  • ✅ The Helm Mindset — thinking in charts, not individual YAML files
Helm Certification Relevance

Chapter 1 sets the foundation for 40% of the Helm certification (Helm Core Concepts). Understanding the architecture and philosophy behind Helm directly helps you answer conceptual questions and reason through troubleshooting scenarios. The exam expects you to know why Helm works the way it does — not just memorizing commands.

🎓 Certification Domains
Helm Core Concepts 40% Foundation Knowledge ALL DOMAINS

1.1 The Problem Before Helm — YAML Copy-Paste Chaos

Imagine you've built anihpj — a Django job board application with the jobpost module where employers post jobs filtered by Skills, Location, and Author. It runs beautifully on your laptop. Now you need to deploy it to Kubernetes. Here's what that looks like without Helm:

The Manual Deployment Nightmare

To deploy anihpj to Kubernetes, you'd need to manually create and manage these YAML files — every single one, by hand, for every environment:

anihpj Deployment — All the YAML Files You'd Need (Without Helm)
  📁 anihpj-k8s-manifests/
  ├── deployment.yaml          ← Django app deployment
  ├── service.yaml             ← Expose app internally
  ├── configmap.yaml           ← App settings (DEBUG, DB_HOST, etc.)
  ├── secret.yaml              ← Database passwords, API keys
  ├── ingress.yaml             ← External access routing
  ├── hpa.yaml                 ← Auto-scaling rules
  ├── pvc.yaml                 ← Persistent storage for uploads
  ├── postgres-deployment.yaml ← Database deployment
  ├── postgres-service.yaml    ← Database service
  ├── postgres-pvc.yaml        ← Database storage
  ├── redis-deployment.yaml    ← Cache deployment
  ├── redis-service.yaml       ← Cache service
  ├── network-policy.yaml      ← Security rules
  ├── service-account.yaml     ← RBAC permissions
  └── namespace.yaml           ← Environment isolation

  😱 15+ YAML files — and that's just ONE environment!
  For dev + staging + prod, you're managing 45+ files.
  Every change = edit in 3 places. Every typo = breakage.

The Pain Points (Before Helm)

📋
YAML Duplication

Dev, staging, and prod need almost identical YAML — just different image tags, replica counts, and config values. You end up copy-pasting 15 files per environment. Change a field in one? You have to chase it down in all the others.

🔢
No Versioning

Which version of anihpj is running in production right now? Without Helm, you're checking kubectl describe and hoping the image tag tells you. There's no single source of truth for what's deployed.

🔄
No Rollback

Deployed a bad version of anihpj? Without Helm, you're manually reapplying old YAML files — if you can find them. There's no built-in way to go back to the previous working state.

🔀
No Templating

Need to change the app name from "anihpj" to "anihpj-prod"? You're doing a find-and-replace across 15 files. Helm templates let you define it once and use it everywhere via {{ .Release.Name }}.

⚠️ Without Helm, You're the Package Manager

Every Kubernetes admin eventually writes bash scripts to "manage" their YAML files — sed replacements, kubectl apply loops, custom version tracking. You're reinventing Helm, badly. Helm is the result of the community saying: "There must be a better way."

Before vs After Helm — The anihpj Deployment Story
  ❌ BEFORE HELM (Manual Chaos)               ✅ WITH HELM (One Command)
  ┌──────────────────────────────┐            ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │                              │            │                              │
  │  1. Copy 15 YAML files       │            │  helm install anihpj \       │
  │  2. Edit image tag manually  │            │    ./anihpj-chart \          │
  │  3. Change DB host in 3 files│            │    --set image.tag=v2.1 \    │
  │  4. Update replica count     │            │    -f values-prod.yaml       │
  │  5. kubectl apply -f file1   │            │                              │
  │  6. kubectl apply -f file2   │            │  🎉 ONE COMMAND.             │
  │  7. ...kubectl apply...file15│            │  Versioned. Reusable.        │
  │  8. Hope nothing broke       │            │  Rollback in seconds.        │
  │  9. No way to rollback       │            │                              │
  │  10. Pray 🙏                  │            │  helm rollback anihpj 1      │
  │                              │            │                              │
  └──────────────────────────────┘            └──────────────────────────────┘

1.2 What is Helm? — The Simple Answer

📖 The Official Definition

Helm is the package manager for Kubernetes. It packages Kubernetes YAML manifests into reusable, versioned, configurable units called Charts. One helm install command can deploy an entire application stack — Deployments, Services, ConfigMaps, Ingresses, PVCs — all at once.

The Elevator Pitch

Think of Helm as "apt-get for Kubernetes" or "npm for Kubernetes" or "brew for Kubernetes". Just like you type apt install nginx to get a web server on Linux, you type helm install myapp ./mychart to deploy an entire application to Kubernetes.

Helm in One Picture — The Core Loop
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                        WHAT HELM DOES                                  │
  │                                                                        │
  │   ┌─────────┐      ┌──────────────┐      ┌─────────────────────────┐ │
  │   │  Chart  │      │    Helm      │      │     Kubernetes          │ │
  │   │  (pkg)  │─────▶│    Engine    │─────▶│     Cluster              │ │
  │   │         │      │              │      │                          │ │
  │   │ • values│      │ 1. Read chart│      │  ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐  │ │
  │   │ • tmpls │      │ 2. Merge vals│      │  │Deploy  │ │Service │  │ │
  │   │ • meta  │      │ 3. Render YML│      │  │anihpj  │ │anihpj  │  │ │
  │   └─────────┘      │ 4. Apply K8s │      │  └────────┘ └────────┘  │ │
  │                    └──────────────┘      │  ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐  │ │
  │                                          │  │Config  │ │Secret  │  │ │
  │  📦 Chart = package                      │  │Map     │ │        │  │ │
  │  🚀 Release = installed chart            │  └────────┘ └────────┘  │ │
  │  📋 Repository = chart storage           └─────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Helm's Three Core Concepts

📦
Chart

A Helm package — a collection of files that describe a related set of Kubernetes resources. Think of it like a .deb or .rpm file, but for Kubernetes. Contains templates, default values, and metadata.

🚀
Release

A running instance of a chart in a Kubernetes cluster. When you install a chart, Helm creates a release. You can have multiple releases of the same chart (e.g., anihpj-dev, anihpj-staging, anihpj-prod).

📋
Repository

A storage location where charts are collected and shared. Like Docker Hub for container images. Public repos include Artifact Hub, Bitnami, and your own private OCI registries.

💡 The Mental Model

Chart = Template + Default Values. A Helm chart is NOT a running application. It's a blueprint. When you helm install that chart, Helm renders the templates with your values and creates a Release — which IS the running application in your cluster.

Helm Architecture — How It Works Under the Hood

Understanding the architecture is critical for the certification. Here's the complete data flow from your terminal to the Kubernetes cluster:

Helm Architecture (v3+) — Complete Data Flow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     HELM ARCHITECTURE (v3+)                            │
  │                     ────────────────────                               │
  │                                                                        │
  │   ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────────────────────┐ │
  │   │  Helm    │     │  Chart       │     │   Kubernetes             │ │
  │   │  CLI     │────▶│  Repository  │────▶│   API Server             │ │
  │   │          │     │  (Artifact   │     │                          │ │
  │   │ helm     │     │   Hub, OCI,  │     │  ┌────────────────────┐  │ │
  │   │ install  │     │   ChartMuseum│     │  │ Secrets            │  │ │
  │   │ upgrade  │     │   Local .tgz)│     │  │ (Release           │  │ │
  │   │ rollback │     └──────────────┘     │  │  Storage)          │  │ │
  │   │ uninstall│                          │  └────────────────────┘  │ │
  │   └────┬─────┘                          │                          │ │
  │        │                                │  ┌────────────────────┐  │ │
  │        │  YOUR kubeconfig               │  │ Resources          │  │ │
  │        │  YOUR RBAC rules               │  │ (Deploy, Svc,      │  │ │
  │        │  NO Tiller!                    │  │  CM, Secret...)    │  │ │
  │        │                                │  └────────────────────┘  │ │
  │        ▼                                │                          │ │
  │   ┌──────────┐                          └──────────────────────────┘ │
  │   │ Chart    │──▶ Template Engine ──────────────▶ Rendered YAML      │
  │   │ values   │   (values + templates              (kubectl apply)    │
  │   │ + tmpl   │    = rendered YAML)                                   │
  │   └──────────┘                                                        │
  │                                                                        │
  │   🔑 KEY POINTS:                                                      │
  │   • Helm CLI talks DIRECTLY to the K8s API — no intermediary         │
  │   • Uses YOUR kubeconfig credentials (same as kubectl)                │
  │   • Release state stored as K8s Secrets in the release namespace      │
  │   • Template Engine: Go templates + Sprig functions → YAML            │
  │   • No Tiller means: simpler, more secure, no cluster-side component  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

How Helm Differs from kubectl — The Essential Distinction

This is one of the most common points of confusion for beginners. Let's clarify it once and for all:

🔧 kubectl — Imperative Toolkit
  • Creates/modifies individual Kubernetes resources
  • You specify what and how
  • No versioning — apply and forget
  • No built-in rollback capability
  • Great for debugging and one-off tasks
  • Example: kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml
⛵ Helm — Declarative Package Manager
  • Manages the entire application lifecycle
  • You declare desired state — Helm figures out how
  • Full versioning — every install/upgrade is tracked
  • Built-in rollback: helm rollback
  • Great for application deployment and management
  • Example: helm install anihpj ./chart
📖 When to Use Which

Use kubectl for: quick debugging (kubectl get pods), ad-hoc resource creation, cluster administration tasks.
Use Helm for: deploying applications, managing releases across environments, CI/CD pipelines, sharing application packages with teams.

They're not competitors — they're complementary. Helm actually uses kubectl concepts internally (it submits YAML to the same K8s API). Think of Helm as a layer on top of kubectl, providing packaging, versioning, and lifecycle management.

Helm Release Lifecycle — A Quick Preview

Every Helm operation moves a release through a state machine. Here's a simplified view (we'll go deeper in Chapter 5):

Helm Release State Machine (Simplified)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  HELM RELEASE LIFECYCLE                                           │
  │                                                                    │
  │         ┌─────────┐                                              │
  │         │ unknown │  ← Initial state                              │
  │         └────┬────┘                                              │
  │              │                                                    │
  │    ┌─────────▼─────────┐                                         │
  │    │ pending-install   │  ← helm install started                  │
  │    └─────────┬─────────┘                                         │
  │              │                                                    │
  │         ┌────▼────┐                                              │
  │         │ deployed│◄──── helm install SUCCESS ✅                  │
  │         └────┬────┘                                              │
  │              │                                                    │
  │    ┌─────────▼─────────┐                                         │
  │    │ pending-upgrade    │  ← helm upgrade started                 │
  │    └─────────┬─────────┘                                         │
  │              │                                                    │
  │         ┌────▼────┐                                              │
  │         │ deployed│◄──── helm upgrade SUCCESS (prev: superseded)  │
  │         └────┬────┘                                              │
  │              │                                                    │
  │    ┌─────────▼─────────┐                                         │
  │    │ failed             │  ← install/upgrade FAILED ❌             │
  │    └─────────┬─────────┘                                         │
  │              │                                                    │
  │    ┌─────────▼─────────┐                                         │
  │    │ uninstalled        │  ← helm uninstall                        │
  │    └────────────────────┘                                         │
  │                                                                    │
  │  🔑 helm rollback: superseded → deployed (re-activated)           │
  │  📋 Each revision stored as a K8s Secret for history              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

1.3 The Helm Analogy — apt/yum/brew for Kubernetes

If you've ever used a Linux package manager, you already understand Helm at a conceptual level. The mapping is remarkably direct:

ConceptLinux (apt)JavaScript (npm)Helm (K8s)
Package.deb filepackage.json + codeChart (.tgz file)
Installapt install nginxnpm install expresshelm install anihpj ./chart
RegistryAPT repos (Ubuntu)npmjs.comArtifact Hub / OCI
Searchapt search nginxnpm search expresshelm search hub nginx
Upgradeapt upgrade nginxnpm update expresshelm upgrade anihpj ./chart
Uninstallapt remove nginxnpm uninstall expresshelm uninstall anihpj
List installedapt list --installednpm list -ghelm list
Versiondpkg -l nginxnpm view express versionhelm list --all
📖 Key Difference from Linux Package Managers

Unlike apt/rpm which install binaries to your system, Helm installs Kubernetes resources (YAML manifests) to a cluster. Helm doesn't put files on your laptop — it talks to the Kubernetes API server and creates Deployments, Services, ConfigMaps, etc. in your cluster. You're "installing" an application, not a binary.

1.3a The Shipping Port Analogy — Helm as the Shipping Manifest

This is one of the most powerful and widely-used metaphors in the container ecosystem. It connects Helm to something physical you already understand — a shipping port.

The Shipping Port Analogy — Helm in Physical Terms
  ⛵ THE SHIPPING PORT ANALOGY — Helm as the Harbor Master

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                        🌊 THE PORT (Kubernetes Cluster)               │
  │                                                                        │
  │   ┌─────────────┐     ┌─────────────┐     ┌─────────────┐            │
  │   │ ⚓ BERTH A   │     │ ⚓ BERTH B   │     │ ⚓ BERTH C   │            │
  │   │ (Namespace   │     │ (Namespace  │     │ (Namespace  │            │
  │   │  anihpj-dev) │     │  anihpj-stg)│     │  anihpj-prod)│            │
  │   │              │     │              │     │              │            │
  │   │ ┌──────────┐ │     │ ┌──────────┐ │     │ ┌──────────┐ │            │
  │   │ │ 🚢 SHIP  │ │     │ │ 🚢 SHIP  │ │     │ │ 🚢 SHIP  │ │            │
  │   │ │ anihpj   │ │     │ │ anihpj   │ │     │ │ anihpj   │ │            │
  │   │ │ v2.1     │ │     │ │ v2.1     │ │     │ │ v2.0     │ │            │
  │   │ └──────────┘ │     │ └──────────┘ │     │ └──────────┘ │            │
  │   └─────────────┘     └─────────────┘     └─────────────┘            │
  │                                                                        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  🔑 THE ANALOGY MAPPING:
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SHIPPING WORLD          →    KUBERNETES / HELM WORLD            │
  │  ─────────────────────        ──────────────────────────         │
  │  🚢 Container Ship       →    📦 Pod (runs your app)             │
  │  📋 Shipping Manifest    →    📄 Helm Chart (blueprint)          │
  │  ⚓ Berth / Dock          →    🏷️ Namespace (isolated space)      │
  │  🏗️ Harbor Master        →    ⛵ Helm (orchestrator)              │
  │  🏢 Port Authority        →    ☸️ Kubernetes API Server           │
  │  📦 Cargo (containers)   →    🐳 Docker Containers               │
  │  🗺️ Route Map             →    🌐 Ingress / Service               │
  │  🔒 Customs Check        →    🛡️ RBAC / Security Policies        │
  │  📊 Ship Manifest Log    →    💾 Release History (Secrets)        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  HOW IT WORKS:
  1. You (Harbor Master) receive a Shipping Manifest (Chart)
  2. The manifest says: "Ship anihpj, 3 containers, needs DB + cache"
  3. You assign it to a Berth (Namespace)
  4. The Port Authority (K8s API) handles docking (scheduling)
  5. The ship is now at berth — running and serving traffic!
  6. Need to upgrade? Send a new manifest. Wrong version? Roll back.
📋
The Chart = Shipping Manifest

A shipping manifest lists everything on the ship — containers, quantities, destination. A Helm Chart lists every Kubernetes resource — Deployments, Services, ConfigMaps — and how they should be configured.

⛵
Helm = Harbor Master

The Harbor Master doesn't build ships or own cargo. They orchestrate — reading manifests, assigning berths, tracking arrivals/departures. Helm does exactly this: reads charts, installs to namespaces, tracks releases.

💡 Why This Analogy Matters for Certification

The shipping port analogy helps you reason about namespaces (berths), releases (ships at berth), rollbacks (calling a ship back), and multi-tenancy (multiple ships at different berths). These are core exam concepts.

⚠️ Common Misunderstanding

Helm is NOT a container registry. Helm stores Kubernetes manifests (YAML), not container images. When a Helm chart says image: anihpj:v2.1, Helm just passes that string to Kubernetes. The actual container image is pulled from Docker Hub, ACR, ECR, or GCR — not from Helm.

1.4 Helm 2 vs Helm 3 — The Architecture Revolution

Understanding the difference between Helm 2 and Helm 3 is critical — not just historically, but because the removal of Tiller fundamentally changed Helm's security model. The certification expects you to know this.

Helm 2 (With Tiller) vs Helm 3 (Client-Only)
  ❌ HELM 2 (Deprecated — DO NOT USE)          ✅ HELM 3 (Current — USE THIS)
  ┌─────────────────────────────┐            ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │                             │            │                              │
  │  ┌────────┐    ┌────────┐  │            │  ┌────────┐                  │
  │  │  Helm  │───▶│ Tiller │  │            │  │  Helm  │                  │
  │  │  CLI   │    │ (in K8s)│  │            │  │  CLI   │──── DIRECT ────▶ │
  │  └────────┘    └───┬────┘  │            │  └────────┘    CONNECTION    │
  │                    │       │            │                                │
  │                    ▼       │            │  ┌──────────────────────────┐ │
  │  ┌─────────────────────┐  │            │  │   Kubernetes API Server  │ │
  │  │  Kubernetes API     │  │            │  │                          │ │
  │  │  Server             │  │            │  │  🔐 Uses YOUR kubeconfig │ │
  │  └─────────────────────┘  │            │  │  🔐 Uses YOUR RBAC       │ │
  │                             │            │  │  🔐 No cluster component │ │
  │  ⚠️ PROBLEMS:              │            │  └──────────────────────────┘ │
  │  • Tiller = cluster-wide   │            │                              │
  │    admin by default        │            │  ✅ BENEFITS:                 │
  │  • Anyone with network     │            │  • Same permissions as you   │
  │    access to Tiller =      │            │  • No extra RBAC to manage   │
  │    cluster admin           │            │  • Simpler architecture       │
  │  • Extra component to      │            │  • Release info in Secrets   │
  │    manage & troubleshoot   │            │    (not ConfigMaps)           │
  │                             │            │                              │
  └─────────────────────────────┘            └──────────────────────────────┘

Helm 2 vs Helm 3 — Key Differences

FeatureHelm 2Helm 3
TillerRequired (server-side component)❌ REMOVED — client-only
SecurityTiller had broad cluster-admin by defaultUses YOUR kubeconfig + RBAC
Release StorageConfigMaps (default)Secrets (default)
3-Way Merge2-way strategic merge3-way strategic merge patch
Chart APIapiVersion: v1apiVersion: v2
CRDsNo CRD managementCRD lifecycle management
Library ChartsNot supported✅ Supported
Schema ValidationNot built-inJSON Schema validation
Release NamespaceTiller namespace-scopedRelease namespace-scoped

1.4a Why Tiller Was Removed — Security Deep Dive

Tiller was Helm 2's server-side component that ran inside the Kubernetes cluster. It received commands from the Helm CLI and executed them against the Kubernetes API. This created a massive security problem:

❌ The Tiller Problem
  • Tiller typically ran with cluster-admin privileges
  • Anyone with network access to Tiller (gRPC port 44134) could deploy anything to the cluster
  • No fine-grained RBAC — Tiller had god-mode
  • Multi-tenant clusters? Impossible — one Tiller, one permission level
  • Tiller's TLS configuration was complex and often skipped entirely
✅ The Helm 3 Solution
  • Helm CLI talks directly to the Kubernetes API
  • Uses your kubeconfig — same permissions you have
  • Your RBAC rules apply — if you can't create Deployments, Helm can't either
  • Multi-tenant safe — each user's permissions are enforced
  • No extra component to install, upgrade, or secure
🚫 CRITICAL: Never Use Helm 2

Helm 2 was deprecated in November 2020 and is no longer supported. All certification exams use Helm 3+. If you encounter Helm 2 in any context, migrate immediately. The Tiller component is a well-known security vulnerability vector.

1.5 Helm in the CNCF Ecosystem — Graduated Project

CNCF Maturity Levels — Helm's Journey
  CNCF PROJECT MATURITY LADDER:

  ┌────────────┐      ┌────────────┐      ┌────────────┐
  │ 🥚 Sandbox │─────▶│ 🐣 Incubat│─────▶│ 🐓 Graduated│
  │            │      │            │      │            │
  │ Early-stage│      │ Growing    │      │ Production │
  │ Experiment │      │ Adoption   │      │ Ready!     │
  └────────────┘      └────────────┘      └────────────┘
                                                ▲
                                         HELM IS HERE!
                                      (Graduated April 2020)

  WHAT "GRADUATED" MEANS:
  ✅ Used in production by hundreds of organizations
  ✅ Strong security processes & audits
  ✅ Diverse maintainer community
  ✅ Formal governance model
  ✅ Healthy adoption metrics

  OTHER GRADUATED PROJECTS: Kubernetes, Prometheus, Envoy, containerd, CoreDNS
🐓
Graduated 2020

Helm became the 10th CNCF graduated project, joining Kubernetes, Prometheus, and Envoy at the top tier.

📊
10M+ Downloads/Month

Helm charts are pulled over 10 million times per month from Artifact Hub alone.

👥
400+ Contributors

Maintained by a global community from Microsoft, Google, VMware, and independent contributors.

📦
15,000+ Charts

Artifact Hub hosts over 15,000 Helm charts covering virtually every popular open-source application.

Helm History & Evolution — A Timeline

💡
Oct 2015
Helm v1

Created by Deis (acquired by Microsoft). Basic chart packaging. Experimental — not production-ready.

🚀
Nov 2016
Helm v2

Introduced Tiller (server-side component). Charts became mainstream. CNCF incubation.

🐓
Apr 2020
Helm v3 + Graduation

Tiller removed. Client-only architecture. 3-way merge. CNCF graduated — top-tier project.

⛵
Today
Helm v3.x

15K+ charts. OCI support. Library charts. Schema validation. Industry standard for K8s packaging.

📖 Why This History Matters

The Helm certification focuses on Helm v3. However, understanding Helm's evolution helps you understand why certain design decisions were made — especially the removal of Tiller. When interviewers or exam questions ask about Helm architecture, knowing the v2→v3 transition shows deep understanding.

1.6 Real-World Helm Use Cases — Who Uses Helm & Why

🏢
Enterprise Deployments

Companies like Spotify, Airbnb, IBM, and Walmart use Helm to manage thousands of microservices across hundreds of clusters. One standardized chart pattern across the entire organization.

🔄
CI/CD Pipelines

GitHub Actions, GitLab CI, and Jenkins use Helm to deploy applications after builds pass. helm upgrade --install in your pipeline = automated, versioned deployments.

🏗️
Infrastructure as Code

Deploy Prometheus, Grafana, Istio, cert-manager, and ingress-nginx as Helm charts. Infrastructure components packaged and versioned just like applications.

📦
Software Distribution

ISVs (Independent Software Vendors) distribute their software as Helm charts. Customers run helm install myproduct myrepo/myapp — no manual YAML configuration needed.

🌐
Multi-Environment Management

Same chart, different values files: values-dev.yaml, values-staging.yaml, values-prod.yaml. One codebase, infinite environments.

🔒
GitOps with Argo CD/Flux

Store Helm charts in Git. Argo CD or Flux CD watches Git and syncs to your cluster. Git is the single source of truth — Helm renders the manifests.

1.7 The Helm Mindset — Think in Charts, Not Files

The biggest shift when learning Helm is moving from a file-based mental model to a chart-based mental model.

❌ File-Based Thinking
  • "I need to edit deployment.yaml"
  • "Let me copy this YAML to staging"
  • "I'll change the image tag in all 3 files"
  • "Where did I put that service.yaml?"
  • "I hope staging is the same as prod"
✅ Chart-Based Thinking
  • "I need to update the chart's values"
  • "I'll create values-staging.yaml"
  • "One value change — all templates updated"
  • "Everything lives in the chart directory"
  • "Dev/staging/prod use the same chart, different values"
The Chart Mindset — Reusable Blueprint
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ONE CHART → MANY RELEASES                                           │
  │                                                                       │
  │                    ┌─────────────────┐                               │
  │                    │  anihpj-chart/  │  ← ONE chart (the blueprint)  │
  │                    │  templates/     │                                │
  │                    │  values.yaml    │                                │
  │                    └────────┬────────┘                               │
  │                             │                                        │
  │          ┌──────────────────┼──────────────────┐                     │
  │          │                  │                  │                     │
  │          ▼                  ▼                  ▼                     │
  │  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐              │
  │  │ anihpj-dev   │  │ anihpj-stg   │  │ anihpj-prod  │              │
  │  │ (namespace:  │  │ (namespace:  │  │ (namespace:  │              │
  │  │  dev)        │  │  staging)    │  │  production) │              │
  │  │ replicas: 1  │  │ replicas: 2  │  │ replicas: 3  │              │
  │  │ image: latest│  │ image: v2.1  │  │ image: v2.0  │              │
  │  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘              │
  │                                                                       │
  │  📋 3 releases = 1 chart × 3 values files                            │
  │  🎯 Same application, different configurations                       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
💡 The Chart Mindset for Certification

On the Helm certification exam, you'll be asked to do things like "install the same chart in two namespaces with different configurations." If you think in charts (not files), this is trivial: helm install dev ./chart -f values-dev.yaml -n dev and helm install prod ./chart -f values-prod.yaml -n prod. Same chart, different releases.

Q1
What is the primary purpose of Helm in the Kubernetes ecosystem?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Helm is the package manager for Kubernetes. It packages Kubernetes YAML manifests into reusable, versioned, configurable units called Charts, enabling one-command deployment of entire application stacks.

Explanation

Just as apt manages .deb packages on Linux, Helm manages Kubernetes resource bundles. Without Helm, you'd manually manage 15+ YAML files per environment. Helm automates this: helm install myapp ./chart deploys everything — Deployments, Services, ConfigMaps, Secrets, Ingresses — in one command.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q2
What are the three core concepts in Helm? Define each briefly.
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Chart: A package of Kubernetes resource templates + default values.
Release: A running instance of a chart in a cluster.
Repository: A storage location where charts are collected and shared.

Explanation

Think of it like this: Chart = .deb file (package), Release = installed nginx (running instance), Repository = apt repo (storage). One chart can produce many releases (e.g., anihpj-dev, anihpj-prod).

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q3
Explain the Shipping Port Analogy as it relates to Helm. Map at least 3 concepts.
Answer & Explanation
Answer

In the Shipping Port Analogy: Chart = Shipping Manifest (blueprint of what's being deployed), Helm = Harbor Master (orchestrator reading manifests), Namespace = Berth/Dock (where the ship is parked), Release = Ship at Berth (the running instance), K8s API = Port Authority (enforces rules).

Explanation

This analogy maps Helm's abstract concepts to physical, intuitive ideas. Just as a harbor master doesn't own ships but coordinates where they dock, Helm doesn't run your app but coordinates its deployment across namespaces. This helps visualize multi-tenancy, rollbacks, and release management.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q4
What is Tiller? Why was it removed in Helm 3?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Tiller was Helm 2's server-side component that ran inside the Kubernetes cluster. It was removed in Helm 3 due to security concerns: Tiller typically ran with cluster-admin privileges, and anyone with network access to Tiller could deploy anything to the cluster. Helm 3 uses the user's kubeconfig credentials directly.

Explanation

Tiller's broad permissions made multi-tenant clusters impossible. Helm 3's client-only architecture means your RBAC rules are always enforced — if you can't create a Deployment with kubectl, Helm can't either. This aligns with Kubernetes' native security model.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q5
Where does Helm 3 store release information by default?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Helm 3 stores release information in Kubernetes Secrets (by default) in the same namespace as the release. Helm 2 used ConfigMaps by default.

Explanation

Each release gets a Secret named sh.helm.release.v1.<release>.v<revision>. This Secret contains the chart metadata, values used, and the rendered manifests. This is how Helm tracks release history and enables rollbacks. You can view them with kubectl get secrets -l owner=helm. 

# Secret structure:
apiVersion: v1
kind: Secret
metadata:
  name: sh.helm.release.v1.anihpj.v3
  labels:
    owner: helm
    name: anihpj
    version: "3"
type: helm.sh/release.v1
data:
  release: <gzipped+base64 JSON>

# JSON payload: {"name":"anihpj","info":{"status":"deployed"},
#   "chart":{...},"config":{...},"manifest":"...","version":3}

Explanation

default is the #1 safety function: {{ .Values.x | default "safe" }}. Prevents nil pointer panics — the most common Helm error. Every nullable value reference needs this.

Q6
Your team is deploying a Django app (anihpj) to 3 environments: dev, staging, and prod. Before Helm, they maintained 45 YAML files (15 per env). How does Helm solve this?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

With Helm, you create ONE chart (anihpj-chart/) containing templates, and THREE values files (values-dev.yaml, values-staging.yaml, values-prod.yaml). Deploy with: helm install dev ./chart -f values-dev.yaml -n dev. 1 chart + 3 values files = 3 environments, instead of 45 separate YAML files.

Explanation 
  Before Helm: 15 files × 3 envs = 45 files to manage
  With Helm:   1 chart + 3 values files = 4 logical units

  Change the app port? Edit templates/service.yaml ONCE.
  Helm regenerates correct configs for all 3 environments.
Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q8
What is the difference between a Chart and a Release in Helm?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

A Chart is a package of Kubernetes resource templates — a blueprint. A Release is a running instance of a chart in a cluster. One chart can have multiple releases (e.g., anihpj-dev, anihpj-prod are two releases from the same anihpj-chart).

Explanation

Think: Chart = Class in OOP, Release = Object instance. Chart = Cookie cutter, Release = Cookie. The chart defines the shape; each release is a specific instantiation with specific values and a unique name.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q9
A colleague says "Helm is like Docker — it packages and runs containers." Is this correct? Explain.
Answer & Explanation
Answer

No, this is incorrect. Docker packages and runs container images. Helm packages Kubernetes YAML manifests (Charts). Helm tells Kubernetes what to deploy; Docker provides the container images that Kubernetes runs. They work together: Helm's chart references a Docker image, Kubernetes pulls that image and runs it.

Explanation

This is a common confusion. Helm does NOT store or distribute container images. When a chart says image: anihpj:v2.1, Helm just passes this string to Kubernetes. Kubernetes then pulls anihpj:v2.1 from a container registry (Docker Hub, ECR, etc.). Helm = Kubernetes manifest manager, Docker = container runtime.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q10
In the context of CNCF, what does "Graduated" status mean for Helm? Name two other graduated projects.
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Graduated means Helm is production-ready, widely adopted, has strong security practices, diverse maintainers, and formal governance. Helm graduated in April 2020. Other graduated projects include Kubernetes, Prometheus, Envoy, containerd, and CoreDNS.

Explanation

CNCF has three maturity levels: Sandbox (experimental), Incubating (growing), and Graduated (production-ready). Graduated is the highest tier. Knowing that Helm is graduated signals to employers and the exam that Helm is a mature, trusted technology — not an experimental tool.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Chapter 2: Installing & Configuring Helm

Before you can deploy anihpj with Helm, you need Helm itself. This chapter walks through installation on every major platform, verifying your setup, managing repositories, installing plugins, and setting up shell autocompletion — everything you need for a productive Helm environment.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~45 min 🛠️ Hands-On 🎯 Setup

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Install Helm CLI on Linux, macOS, and Windows — all three platforms
  • ✅ Verify your installation with helm version and understand the output
  • ✅ Configure Helm's environment — HELM_HOME, HELM_NAMESPACE, and more
  • ✅ Add and manage chart repositories — Bitnami, Stable, and custom repos
  • ✅ Extend Helm with plugins — install community plugins for diff, secrets, and more

🎓 Certification Domains
Helm Core Concepts 40% CLI & Environment Setup PRACTICAL

2.1 Installing Helm CLI

Helm is a single binary — no daemon, no database, no background service. Installation is simply downloading the binary and putting it in your PATH.

Helm Installation — Three Platform Options
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     HELM INSTALLATION METHODS                         │
  │                                                                       │
  │  🐧 LINUX                    🍎 macOS                   🪟 WINDOWS   │
  │  ─────────                  ─────────                 ───────────    │
  │  Option A: Script           Option A: Homebrew         Option A:      │
  │  curl -fsSL \               brew install helm          choco install  │
  │  https://raw.github\                                    kubernetes-   │
  │ usercontent.com/helm/                                  helm           │
  │  helm/main/scripts/                                                 │
  │  get-helm-3 | bash          Option B: Script           Option B:      │
  │                              (same as Linux)            scoop install │
  │  Option B: Package Mgr                                  helm           │
  │  apt-get install \          Option C: MacPorts
  │  helm (Debian/Ubuntu)       sudo port install          Option C:      │
  │  yum install helm \         helm                       Manual (.zip)  │
  │  (RHEL/CentOS)                                          Download from │
  │  snap install helm \                                   GitHub Releases│
  │  --classic                                                           │
  │                                                                       │
  │  VERIFY: helm version → should show v3.x.x (NOT v2.x.x!)            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Linux/macOS — Install via Script 
# Download and run the official Helm install script
curl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/helm/helm/main/scripts/get-helm-3 | bash

# Or, for macOS with Homebrew:
brew install helm

# For Debian/Ubuntu:
curl https://baltocdn.com/helm/signing.asc | sudo apt-key add -
sudo apt-get install apt-transport-https --yes
echo "deb https://baltocdn.com/helm/stable/debian/ all main" | sudo tee /etc/apt/sources.list.d/helm-stable-debian.list
sudo apt-get update
sudo apt-get install helm
Windows — Install via Chocolatey or Scoop 
# Using Chocolatey (run PowerShell as Administrator)
choco install kubernetes-helm

# Using Scoop
scoop install helm

# Manual: Download from https://github.com/helm/helm/releases
# Extract helm.exe and add it to your PATH
⚠️ Helm 2 vs 3 — Make Sure You Get v3

The current version is Helm v3.x. If helm version shows v2.x.x, you have the wrong version. Helm 2 is deprecated and not compatible with Kubernetes 1.22+. The certification exam uses Helm v3 exclusively.

2.2 Verifying Installation — helm version

Verify Helm Installation 
# Check Helm version
helm version
# Expected output (example):
# version.BuildInfo{Version:"v3.16.0", GitCommit:"abcdef123", GitTreeState:"clean", GoVersion:"go1.22.0"}

# Check that Helm can connect to your Kubernetes cluster
helm list
# Should show no releases (empty) — but no error about connection

# If you see an error about kubeconfig:
# "Kubernetes cluster unreachable: the server has asked for the client to provide credentials"
# → Make sure kubectl is configured and can connect:
kubectl cluster-info
📖 What helm version Tells You

helm version shows the client version (the Helm binary on your machine). Unlike Helm 2, it does NOT show a server version — because there IS no server component (Tiller was removed). If your Kubernetes cluster is reachable, Helm works. Simple.

2.3 Helm Environment & Configuration

📁
HELM_HOME

Default: ~/.config/helm/ (Linux/macOS) or %APPDATA%\helm\ (Windows). Stores repository cache, plugins, and configuration.

🏷️
HELM_NAMESPACE

Set a default namespace for all Helm commands. Equivalent to kubectl config set-context --namespace. Example: export HELM_NAMESPACE=anihpj-dev

📂
HELM_CACHE_HOME

Where Helm stores cached charts. Default: ~/.cache/helm/. Charts are cached here when downloaded from repositories.

🔧
HELM_CONFIG_HOME

Where Helm stores configuration files. Default: ~/.config/helm/. Contains repositories.yaml and other settings.

Helm Environment Variables 
# View Helm's environment and paths
helm env

# Set default namespace for all helm commands
export HELM_NAMESPACE=anihpj-prod

# Set custom Helm home directory
export HELM_HOME=/custom/path/helm

# View current Helm configuration
helm env | grep HELM

2.4 Adding Chart Repositories

Repositories are where Helm charts are stored and distributed. Think of them like Docker registries but for Helm charts.

Repository Management Commands 
# List currently configured repositories
helm repo list

# Add popular repositories
helm repo add bitnami https://charts.bitnami.com/bitnami
helm repo add stable https://charts.helm.sh/stable
helm repo add ingress-nginx https://kubernetes.github.io/ingress-nginx

# Update repository index (fetch latest chart listings)
helm repo update

# Search for charts in your repos
helm search repo nginx
helm search repo bitnami/postgresql

# Search on Artifact Hub (global search)
helm search hub wordpress

# Remove a repository
helm repo remove stable
💡 Common Repositories You'll Need
  • Bitnami (https://charts.bitnami.com/bitnami) — Well-maintained charts for databases, monitoring, and more
  • Ingress-Nginx (https://kubernetes.github.io/ingress-nginx) — The official NGINX ingress controller
  • Cert-Manager (https://charts.jetstack.io) — TLS certificate management
  • Artifact Hub (https://artifacthub.io) — Not a repo itself, but the global search engine for Helm charts

2.5 Helm Plugins — Extending Helm

Helm has a plugin system that lets you add new commands. Plugins are community-maintained extensions that enhance Helm's capabilities.

Essential Helm Plugins 
# Install essential plugins
helm plugin install https://github.com/databus/helm-diff      # helm diff — see changes before upgrade
helm plugin install https://github.com/jkroepke/helm-secrets  # helm secrets — encrypt values
helm plugin install https://github.com/aslafy-z/helm-git      # helm git — install charts from git

# List installed plugins
helm plugin list

# Update plugins
helm plugin update <plugin-name>

# Uninstall a plugin
helm plugin uninstall <plugin-name>
🔍
helm diff

Shows a colored diff of what will change before you run helm upgrade. Critical for production safety — see changes before applying them.

🔒
helm secrets

Encrypts sensitive values files using Mozilla SOPS or AWS KMS. Never commit plain-text secrets to Git. This plugin makes secrets management secure.

📦
helm git

Install charts directly from Git repositories. Useful for CI/CD pipelines where charts live in the same repo as application code.

2.6 Shell Autocompletion Setup

Autocompletion dramatically speeds up your workflow — essential for both the certification exam and daily work. Hit Tab and Helm completes commands, flags, release names, and even chart names.

Shell Autocompletion 
# Bash (add to ~/.bashrc)
source <(helm completion bash)

# Zsh (add to ~/.zshrc)
source <(helm completion zsh)

# Fish (add to ~/.config/fish/config.fish)
helm completion fish | source

# PowerShell (add to $PROFILE)
helm completion powershell | Out-String | Invoke-Expression

# After setup, try:
helm <TAB>              # Shows all commands: install, upgrade, list, etc.
helm install <TAB>      # Shows flags: --name, --namespace, --values, etc.
helm list -<TAB>        # Shows flags starting with -
helm uninstall <TAB>    # Shows release names (if any exist)
💡 Autocompletion on the Exam

On the Helm certification exam, autocompletion may or may not be pre-configured. Memorize the one-liner to enable it: source <(helm completion bash). Spending 3 seconds to enable autocompletion can save you minutes of typing and prevent typos in command flags.

⚡ Setup Validation Checklist — Is Your Helm Ready?

Before you start deploying real applications, run through this checklist to make sure everything is properly configured. This prevents 90% of "it doesn't work!" moments.

1
Verify Helm Version

helm version — Should show v3.x.x. If you see v2.x.x, uninstall and get Helm 3. Helm 2 is deprecated and incompatible with modern Kubernetes.

2
Verify Kubernetes Connectivity

kubectl cluster-info — Confirms kubectl can reach the cluster. Helm uses the same kubeconfig as kubectl, so if kubectl works, Helm works.

3
Test Helm-to-Cluster Connection

helm list — Should return an empty list (or show existing releases) with NO errors. If you get "Kubernetes cluster unreachable," check your kubeconfig.

4
Add Essential Repositories

helm repo add bitnami https://charts.bitnami.com/bitnami then helm repo update. Search with helm search repo nginx — you should see results.

5
Enable Autocompletion (Optional but Recommended)

source <(helm completion bash) — Then type helm <TAB><TAB>. You should see all available commands. This is a huge time-saver.

6
Check Plugins (Optional)

helm plugin list — See what plugins are installed. Consider installing helm diff for safer upgrades.

Verification Flow — Complete
  ✅ ALL CHECKS PASSED?                          ❌ SOMETHING FAILED?
  ┌────────────────────────┐                   ┌──────────────────────────┐
  │ You're ready to deploy!│                   │ Check the error message  │
  │                         │                   │ carefully:               │
  │ helm install anihpj \  │                   │                          │
  │   ./anihpj-chart \     │                   │ • Wrong version?         │
  │   --set image.tag=v2.1 │                   │   curl get-helm-3 | bash │
  │                         │                   │                          │
  │ 🎉 Go to Chapter 3!    │                   │ • No cluster connection? │
  └────────────────────────┘                   │   kubectl cluster-info   │
                                                │                          │
                                                │ • Repo not found?        │
                                                │   helm repo update       │
                                                └──────────────────────────┘

🛠️ Troubleshooting Common Installation Issues

🔴
"helm: command not found"

The Helm binary isn't in your PATH. Solution: Move the binary to /usr/local/bin/ (Linux/macOS) or add its location to your system PATH (Windows). Verify with which helm.

🔴
"Kubernetes cluster unreachable"

Helm can't reach the K8s API. Check: (1) Is your cluster running? kubectl cluster-info, (2) Is kubeconfig set? echo $KUBECONFIG, (3) Try kubectl get nodes first.

🔴
"Error: no repositories found"

You haven't added any repos yet. Run helm repo add bitnami https://charts.bitnami.com/bitnami followed by helm repo update. Then try your search again.

🔴
"Error: repo ... not found"

Your local repo cache is stale. Run helm repo update to refresh. If a repo was removed upstream, remove it: helm repo remove <name>.

🔴
"Permission denied" on install

This is actually correct behavior — your RBAC permissions control what Helm can do. Make sure you have the necessary ClusterRole/Role permissions in the target namespace.

🔴
"Error: plugin already exists"

You're trying to install a plugin that's already there. Use helm plugin list to see installed plugins, then helm plugin update <name> to update instead of reinstall.

⚠️ Common Gotcha: Helm 2 Habits Die Hard

If you learned Helm before 2020, you might try running helm init — this was the command to install Tiller in Helm 2. In Helm 3, helm init does not exist. There is nothing to initialize. Helm 3 is client-only — no server-side component to install. If a tutorial or blog post tells you to run helm init, it's outdated.

Q1
You've installed Helm using the install script. How do you verify the installation and confirm it can connect to your cluster?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Run helm version to verify the Helm client version. Run helm list (or helm ls) to verify connectivity to the Kubernetes cluster. If Helm can reach the API server, it will show releases (even if the list is empty).

Explanation

helm version only checks the client binary — it doesn't need a cluster. helm list actually talks to the Kubernetes API to query release Secrets. If helm list returns without a connection error, Helm is properly configured.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q2
What is the purpose of helm repo update? When should you run it?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm repo update fetches the latest chart index from all configured repositories. You should run it: (1) After adding a new repo, (2) Before searching for charts, (3) Before installing/upgrading to ensure you have the latest chart versions.

Explanation

Repositories are cached locally. Without helm repo update, you're searching against stale index data. Think of it like apt update — it refreshes the package list without installing anything.

Explanation

Repositories distribute charts. helm repo add + helm repo update. search repo (local) vs search hub (Artifact Hub). Private repos use --username --password.

Q3
What environment variable sets the default namespace for all Helm commands?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

HELM_NAMESPACE. Set it with export HELM_NAMESPACE=my-namespace. This affects all Helm commands that don't have an explicit --namespace or -n flag.

Explanation

This is especially useful on the exam — set HELM_NAMESPACE once and you don't need to add -n to every command. But be careful: some exam tasks will ask you to deploy to different namespaces. Always check the question.

Explanation

default is the #1 safety function: {{ .Values.x | default "safe" }}. Prevents nil pointer panics — the most common Helm error. Every nullable value reference needs this.

Q4
Explain the difference between helm search hub and helm search repo.
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm search hub searches Artifact Hub — the global, public registry of all Helm charts. helm search repo searches only the repositories you've added locally with helm repo add.

Explanation

Artifact Hub aggregates charts from hundreds of repositories. helm search hub is like Googling. helm search repo only searches your locally configured repos — faster, but limited scope. To install a chart found via search hub, you first need to helm repo add its repository.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q5
Which Helm plugin would you use to see what will change before running helm upgrade?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

The helm-diff plugin. Install with helm plugin install https://github.com/databus/helm-diff, then run helm diff upgrade <release> <chart> to see a colored diff of changes before applying them.

Explanation

helm diff is a production safety tool. It shows exactly which Kubernetes resources will be created, modified, or deleted by an upgrade — similar to terraform plan. This prevents "oops, I didn't mean to delete that PVC" moments.

Explanation

helm upgrade uses a three-way strategic merge patch comparing old manifest, new manifest, and live state. This prevents overwriting changes made by other controllers. Use --atomic for auto-rollback on failure.

Q6
How do you enable Helm bash autocompletion?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Run source <(helm completion bash). To make it permanent, add this line to ~/.bashrc. For zsh: source <(helm completion zsh).

Explanation

Autocompletion is generated by Helm itself. The <(...) syntax uses process substitution to source the completion script directly. On the exam, this single line can save you significant time by completing command names, flags, and release names.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q7
Where does Helm store its configuration and cache by default on Linux?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Configuration: ~/.config/helm/. Cache: ~/.cache/helm/. Data: ~/.local/share/helm/. You can view all paths with helm env.

Explanation

helm env is your friend for debugging. It shows all environment variables, paths, and configuration locations Helm is using. If something is wrong with repository access or plugin loading, helm env is the first place to check.

Explanation

default is the #1 safety function: {{ .Values.x | default "safe" }}. Prevents nil pointer panics — the most common Helm error. Every nullable value reference needs this.

Q8
What command shows all currently configured Helm repositories?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm repo list. This displays the repository name, URL, and any authentication status for each configured repository.

Explanation

Repository configuration is stored in a YAML file (usually ~/.config/helm/repositories.yaml). helm repo list reads this file. Common repos include Bitnami, Stable, and your organization's private chart museum.

Explanation

Repositories distribute charts. helm repo add + helm repo update. search repo (local) vs search hub (Artifact Hub). Private repos use --username --password.

Q9
You need to install the Bitnami PostgreSQL chart. What are the exact commands, step by step?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Step 1: helm repo add bitnami https://charts.bitnami.com/bitnami. Step 2: helm repo update. Step 3: helm search repo bitnami/postgresql (to find the chart). Step 4: helm install my-postgres bitnami/postgresql.

Explanation

The exam may test your ability to find and install community charts. The pattern is always: repo add → repo update → search → install. Don't forget helm repo update — without it, Helm won't see recently added charts.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q10
After installing Helm via a package manager, helm version returns v2.16.1. What should you do and why?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Uninstall Helm 2 immediately and install Helm 3. Helm 2 is deprecated (since Nov 2020), uses the insecure Tiller component, and is incompatible with modern Kubernetes versions. The certification exam uses Helm 3 only. Install via the official script: curl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/helm/helm/main/scripts/get-helm-3 | bash.

Explanation

Some older Linux distributions still ship Helm 2 in their package repositories. Always verify the version after installation. Helm 2's Tiller component is a critical security risk — it runs with cluster-admin privileges and exposes a gRPC endpoint inside your cluster.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

📥Single BinaryNo daemon or service needed
✅helm versionVerify v3.x is installed
📋helm repo addConnect to chart repositories
🔌helm pluginExtend with diff, secrets, git
⌨️completionTab-complete commands & flags
⚙️helm envView all paths and settings

Chapter 3: Charts — The Heart of Helm

A Chart is the fundamental unit of Helm. Everything you do with Helm revolves around charts — creating them, packaging them, installing them, and sharing them. This chapter dissects the chart structure file by file, showing you exactly what each component does. By the end, you'll be able to create, understand, and navigate any Helm chart with confidence.
