3.1 What is a Chart? — Structure Deep Dive

A Helm chart is a directory tree containing all the files needed to describe a Kubernetes application. Think of it as the "source code" of your deployment — templates, default configuration, metadata, and dependency declarations all living together in one organized structure.

Complete Chart Anatomy — Every File Explained
  📁 anihpj-chart/                              ← Chart root directory
  │
  ├── 📄 Chart.yaml          ← METADATA
  │   ├── apiVersion: v2     ← Chart API version (v2 = Helm 3)
  │   ├── name: anihpj       ← Chart name
  │   ├── version: 0.1.0     ← Chart version (SemVer)
  │   ├── appVersion: "2.1"  ← Application version
  │   ├── type: application  ← "application" or "library"
  │   ├── description: ...   ← Human-readable description
  │   └── dependencies: []   ← Subchart declarations
  │
  ├── 📄 values.yaml         ← DEFAULT CONFIGURATION
  │   ├── replicaCount: 2
  │   ├── image:
  │   │   ├── repository: anihpj
  │   │   ├── tag: "v2.1"
  │   │   └── pullPolicy: IfNotPresent
  │   ├── service:
  │   │   ├── type: ClusterIP
  │   │   └── port: 80
  │   ├── ingress:
  │   │   └── enabled: false
  │   └── resources: {}
  │
  ├── 📄 values-dev.yaml     ← ENVIRONMENT OVERRIDES
  ├── 📄 values-staging.yaml
  ├── 📄 values-prod.yaml
  │
  ├── 📁 templates/          ← YAML GENERATION ENGINE
  │   ├── 📄 deployment.yaml
  │   ├── 📄 service.yaml
  │   ├── 📄 configmap.yaml
  │   ├── 📄 secret.yaml
  │   ├── 📄 ingress.yaml
  │   ├── 📄 hpa.yaml
  │   ├── 📄 NOTES.txt       ← POST-INSTALL MESSAGE
  │   ├── 📄 _helpers.tpl    ← REUSABLE TEMPLATE HELPERS
  │   └── 📁 tests/
  │       └── 📄 test-connection.yaml
  │
  ├── 📁 charts/             ← SUBCHART DEPENDENCIES
  │   ├── 📦 postgresql-12.x.x.tgz
  │   └── 📦 redis-18.x.x.tgz
  │
  ├── 📄 Chart.lock          ← DEPENDENCY LOCK FILE
  └── 📄 .helmignore         ← EXCLUDE PATTERNS
📖 Chart = Blueprint, Release = Building

The chart directory contains templates, not actual Kubernetes resources. When you helm install, Helm reads the chart, injects values into templates, renders complete YAML manifests, and submits them to Kubernetes. The resulting running application is called a Release.

3.1a Chart.yaml — Metadata, Versioning & apiVersion

Chart.yaml is the identity card of your chart. It tells Helm what this chart is called, what version it is, what application it deploys, and what other charts it depends on.

Chart.yaml
apiVersion: v2              # REQUIRED: Chart API version (v2 = Helm 3+)
name: anihpj                # REQUIRED: Chart name
description: A Helm chart for the anihpj Django job board application
type: application           # REQUIRED: "application" or "library"
version: 0.1.0              # REQUIRED: Chart version (SemVer 2)
appVersion: "2.1.0"         # OPTIONAL: Version of the app being deployed
kubeVersion: ">= 1.19.0-0"  # OPTIONAL: Supported K8s versions
home: https://anihpj.example.com
sources:
  - https://github.com/anihpj/anihpj
maintainers:
  - name: DevOps Team
    email: devops@anihpj.com
keywords:
  - django
  - job-board
dependencies:
  - name: postgresql
    version: "12.x.x"
    repository: https://charts.bitnami.com/bitnami
    condition: postgresql.enabled
📌
apiVersion: v2

Always v2 for Helm 3. v1 was Helm 2. This field tells Helm which API to use for processing your chart. If you see v1, the chart is outdated.

🏷️
version vs appVersion

version = chart version (changes when templates change). appVersion = application version (software being deployed). They're independent!

🔀
type: application/library

application = standard deployable chart. library = reusable template snippets (not directly installable).

⚠️ Common Mistake: Confusing version and appVersion

Beginners often think version and appVersion must match. They don't. You can release chart version 5.2.0 that still deploys appVersion 1.0.0 if only the templates changed. version tracks the chart, appVersion tracks the app.

3.1b values.yaml — Default Configuration & Documentation

values.yaml is the single source of default configuration. Every value that can be customized lives here.

values.yaml for anihpj
replicaCount: 2
image:
  repository: anihpj/anihpj
  pullPolicy: IfNotPresent
  tag: "v2.1"
nameOverride: ""
fullnameOverride: ""
serviceAccount:
  create: true
  name: ""
service:
  type: ClusterIP
  port: 80
ingress:
  enabled: false
  hosts:
    - host: anihpj.local
      paths: [{ path: /, pathType: Prefix }]
resources: {}
autoscaling:
  enabled: false
  minReplicas: 1
  maxReplicas: 10
  targetCPUUtilizationPercentage: 80
postgresql:
  enabled: true
  auth:
    database: anihpj_db
    username: anihpj_user
    password: changeme
💡 values.yaml as Documentation

The comments in values.yaml serve as self-documentation. A well-commented values.yaml tells users exactly what each setting does and what the valid options are.

3.1c templates/ — The YAML Generation Engine

The templates/ directory is where the magic happens. Each file is a Go template that Helm renders into Kubernetes YAML. Anything in this directory (except NOTES.txt and files starting with _) gets processed and submitted to Kubernetes.

📄
deployment.yaml

Defines the Deployment — replica count, image, ports, health checks, resource limits, volumes.

🌐
service.yaml

Exposes the app internally (ClusterIP) or externally (NodePort/LoadBalancer). Maps ports and selectors.

⚙️
configmap.yaml

Non-sensitive configuration — env vars, app settings, feature flags.

🔐
secret.yaml

Sensitive data — passwords, API keys, TLS certs. Base64-encoded at rest.

🚪
ingress.yaml

External HTTP/HTTPS routing. Usually conditional (if ingress.enabled).

📈
hpa.yaml

Horizontal Pod Autoscaler — auto-scales based on CPU/memory.

📖 Special Template Files
  • NOTES.txt — Displayed to user after install/upgrade. NOT a K8s resource.
  • _helpers.tpl — Reusable template functions. NOT deployed.
  • tests/ — Test pod definitions. Run via helm test.

3.1d charts/ — Subchart Dependencies

The charts/ directory holds dependent charts — other charts your application needs. For anihpj, that might be PostgreSQL (database) and Redis (cache). When you declare dependencies in Chart.yaml and run helm dependency update, Helm downloads the dependency charts as .tgz files into charts/. The Chart.lock file records exact versions for reproducible builds.

3.1e .helmignore — Excluding Files from Packaging

Similar to .gitignore, the .helmignore file tells Helm which files to exclude when packaging a chart with helm package.

.helmignore
# Patterns to ignore when packaging
.git/
.gitignore
.idea/
*.swp
*.bak
*tmp*
.DS_Store
Thumbs.db
tests/
.github/
.gitlab-ci.yml

3.1f Chart.lock — Reproducible Dependency Builds

Chart.lock is automatically generated by helm dependency update. It records the exact versions and digests of all dependency charts — critical for reproducible deployments.

⚠️ Never Delete Chart.lock

Deleting Chart.lock means helm dependency update will re-resolve versions, potentially pulling different versions. Always commit Chart.lock to Git.

3.2 Creating Your First Chart — helm create

Create and Explore Your First Chart
# Create a new chart with default scaffolding
helm create my-first-chart

# See what was created
tree my-first-chart/

# Customize for anihpj:
helm create anihpj-chart

# Edit Chart.yaml with your app details
vim anihpj-chart/Chart.yaml

# Edit values.yaml with your defaults
vim anihpj-chart/values.yaml

# Verify the chart is valid
helm lint anihpj-chart/

# See what YAML the chart would generate
helm template anihpj-chart/

# Install it!
helm install anihpj-dev ./anihpj-chart --namespace dev --create-namespace
💡 helm create is Your Exam Friend

On the certification exam, always use helm create to scaffold charts. It gives you a working template immediately. Never start from an empty directory — it wastes precious exam time.

Bonus: helm show — Inspecting Charts Without Installing

helm show lets you peek inside any chart — local or from a repository — without installing it. Essential for understanding what a chart does before committing to it.

helm show — Chart Inspection
# Show chart metadata (Chart.yaml content)
helm show chart ./anihpj-chart
helm show chart bitnami/nginx

# Show the chart's README
helm show readme bitnami/postgresql

# Show all values with documentation
helm show values bitnami/postgresql
# This prints the entire values.yaml with comments — incredibly useful!

# Show everything (chart info + readme + values)
helm show all bitnami/nginx

Bonus: helm package — Distributing Your Chart

When you're ready to share your chart, helm package creates a versioned .tgz archive. This is what you push to chart repositories.

Packaging and Verifying Charts
# Package a chart (creates anihpj-0.1.0.tgz)
helm package ./anihpj-chart

# Package with a specific destination
helm package ./anihpj-chart -d ./releases/

# Sign a chart package (requires GPG key)
helm package ./anihpj-chart --sign --key 'devops@anihpj.com'

# Verify a signed chart
helm verify anihpj-0.1.0.tgz

# Lint before packaging (good CI practice)
helm lint ./anihpj-chart && helm package ./anihpj-chart
1
Lint

helm lint ./chart — Validate syntax and best practices.

2
Template

helm template ./chart --debug — Render YAML locally to verify output.

3
Package

helm package ./chart — Create .tgz archive.

4
Push

helm push ./chart-0.1.0.tgz oci://registry.example.com/charts — Distribute to registry.

3.3 Chart Structure for anihpj — Step by Step Walkthrough

Let's walk through creating a production-ready chart for our Django job board application. anihpj needs: Django web deployment, PostgreSQL, Redis cache, and optional ingress.

1
Scaffold

helm create anihpj-chart — creates standard skeleton.

2
Metadata

Set name: anihpj, appVersion: "2.1.0", add PostgreSQL/Redis deps in Chart.yaml.

3
Configure

Set image, service port 8000, enable ingress with host jobs.example.com in values.yaml.

4
Templates

Write deployment.yaml with Django/Gunicorn settings, env vars from ConfigMap/Secret.

5
Config

configmap.yaml for DEBUG/DB_HOST. secret.yaml for SECRET_KEY/DB_PASSWORD.

6
Dependencies

helm dependency update to download PostgreSQL + Redis into charts/.

7
Deploy

helm lint → helm template --debug → helm install anihpj-dev ./anihpj-chart -n dev --create-namespace

3.5 Semantic Versioning for Charts (SemVer 2)

SemVer: MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH
  MAJOR . MINOR . PATCH          Example:  2  .  1  .  5
    │       │       │
    │       │       └── PATCH: Backward-compatible bug fixes
    │       │            Fix typo in NOTES.txt → 2.1.5 → 2.1.6
    │       │            Update default resource limits → 2.1.6
    │       │
    │       └── MINOR: Backward-compatible new features
    │            Add new template (hpa.yaml) → 2.1.5 → 2.2.0
    │            Add new values section → 2.2.0
    │
    └── MAJOR: Incompatible / breaking changes
         Rename a values key → 2.1.5 → 3.0.0
         Remove a template → 3.0.0

  🔑 If users must change their values.yaml to upgrade, it's MAJOR.

3.6 Chart Types — Application vs Library Charts

📦 Application Chart
  • Default chart type
  • Deployable — helm install
  • Contains complete K8s resources
  • Used for: anihpj, nginx, postgresql
📚 Library Chart
  • NOT deployable directly
  • Reusable template snippets
  • Other charts import its templates
  • Used for: shared helpers/patterns
Q1
What command creates a new Helm chart with default scaffolding?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm create <chart-name>. Generates a complete chart with Chart.yaml, values.yaml, templates/, and helper files.

Explanation

The scaffolded chart deploys nginx by default. Customize Chart.yaml, values.yaml, and templates for your app.

Explanation

default is the #1 safety function: {{ .Values.x | default "safe" }}. Prevents nil pointer panics — the most common Helm error. Every nullable value reference needs this.

Q2
Difference between version and appVersion in Chart.yaml?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

version = chart version (templates/metadata). appVersion = application version (software). They're independent; version is REQUIRED, appVersion is optional.

Explanation

Chart.yaml requires: apiVersion: v2, name, version. Critical: version (chart) and appVersion (app) are INDEPENDENT. type: application (deployable) or library (template-only).

Q3
Purpose of the charts/ directory?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Holds dependency charts (subcharts). Populated by helm dependency update from Chart.yaml dependencies.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q4
Which file is displayed after successful helm install?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

templates/NOTES.txt. NOT a K8s resource — rendered text shown to user with post-install instructions.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q5
What version bump for renaming a values key?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

MAJOR (2.1.5 → 3.0.0). Renaming a key breaks existing users' values.yaml — a breaking change.

Explanation

helm install creates a new release tracked in Kubernetes Secrets. Key flags: --atomic (auto-rollback), --wait (wait for readiness), --dry-run (validate first). Always dry-run before deploying on the exam.

Q6
Application vs Library chart?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Application = deployable (helm install). Library = NOT deployable, provides reusable template snippets.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q7
Command to validate a chart without installing?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm lint <chart-path>. Checks Chart.yaml syntax, template rendering, and best practices.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q8
Purpose of .helmignore?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Excludes files from chart packages (helm package). Works like .gitignore. Common: .git/, *.swp, CI configs.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q9
Should you commit Chart.lock to git?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

YES. It pins exact dependency versions for reproducible builds. Without it, versions may drift.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q10
After helm create, what 3 files do you edit first?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

1. Chart.yaml (metadata), 2. values.yaml (defaults), 3. templates/deployment.yaml (app-specific config).

Explanation

Chart.lock pins exact dependency versions and digests. Always commit to Git for reproducible builds. Rebuild with helm dependency build (no network, faster, reads lock file).

📄Chart.yamlMetadata & identity
⚙️values.yamlDefault configuration
🔧templates/YAML generation engine
📦charts/Dependencies storage
🔒Chart.lockReproducible builds
🏷️SemVer 2MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH

📐 Chart Design Best Practices

These conventions separate professional charts from amateur ones. The certification exam tests your ability to create well-structured, maintainable charts.

📛
Chart Naming

Use lowercase letters, numbers, and hyphens only. anihpj-chart ✅ | Anihpj_Chart ❌. The chart name in Chart.yaml must match the directory name.

🏷️
Always Set appVersion

Even though appVersion is optional, always set it. It's the first thing users check to know what version of your software is deployed. Use the same tag as your container image.

📝
Comment values.yaml

Every key in values.yaml should have a YAML comment explaining what it does. The comments serve as inline documentation. Users should never have to guess what a value controls.

🎯
Sensible Defaults

Your chart should deploy a working application with helm install myapp ./chart — no extra flags needed. Defaults should be safe and functional. Users override for customization, not to fix broken defaults.

📦
One Chart, One App

A chart should deploy ONE application with its immediate dependencies. Don't bundle unrelated apps into one chart. Use subcharts for dependencies (PostgreSQL, Redis) — not for unrelated services.

🔄
Template Portability

Templates should work across Kubernetes versions. Use .Capabilities.KubeVersion for version-specific logic. Avoid hardcoding API versions that may be deprecated (e.g., extensions/v1beta1).

Chapter 4: Values & Configuration — The Control Panel of Helm

Values are how you tell Helm what to deploy. Without values, every installation would be identical — same image, same replica count, same config. Values make your charts reusable across environments, teams, and use cases. This chapter covers the complete values system: defaults, overrides, precedence rules, multiple files, and schema validation.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~1.5 hours ⚙️ Config 🎯 40% Exam Weight

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Master values.yaml as the single source of default configuration
  • ✅ Override values with --set, --values, and --set-file
  • ✅ Understand the complete value precedence chain — what overrides what
  • ✅ Manage environment-specific configurations with multiple values files
  • ✅ Validate values with JSON Schema (values.schema.json)
  • ✅ Build dev, staging, and prod configurations for anihpj
🎓 Certification Domains
Helm Core Concepts 40% Values & Configuration CORE

4.1 values.yaml — The Single Source of Configuration

values.yaml is the default configuration file in every Helm chart. It defines sensible defaults so that helm install myapp ./chart works without any additional flags. Every template accesses these values through the .Values object.

How Values Flow Into Templates
  ┌──────────────────┐
  │  values.yaml     │  ← Chart defaults (lowest priority)
  │  ─────────────   │
  │  replicaCount: 2 │
  │  image.tag: v2.1 │
  │  service.port: 80│
  └────────┬─────────┘
           │
           ▼
  ┌──────────────────┐     ┌─────────────────────────┐
  │  Template Engine │────▶│  Rendered YAML          │
  │                  │     │                         │
  │  {{ .Values.     │     │  apiVersion: apps/v1    │
  │    replicaCount  │     │  kind: Deployment       │
  │  }}              │     │  spec:                  │
  │                  │     │    replicas: 2          │  ← From values.yaml
  │  {{ .Values.     │     │    template:            │
  │    image.tag     │     │      spec:              │
  │  }}              │     │        containers:      │
  └──────────────────┘     │        - image: anihpj  │
                           │            anihpj:v2.1  │  ← From values.yaml
                           └─────────────────────────┘
📖 Values Are NOT Secrets

values.yaml is plain text — never put passwords or API keys here. Use Kubernetes Secrets or tools like helm-secrets plugin (Mozilla SOPS) or External Secrets Operator for sensitive data. Values files get stored in release history as Secrets, but the source values.yaml should never contain actual secrets.

4.2 Overriding Values — --set, --values, --set-file

Helm provides three ways to override default values. Each has different use cases:

MethodSyntaxBest ForExample
--set--set key=valueQuick overrides, CI/CD--set image.tag=v3.0
--values / -f-f file.yamlEnvironment configs-f values-prod.yaml
--set-file--set-file key=pathLarge config files--set-file config=app.conf
--set-string--set-string key=valForce string type--set-string port="80"
Overriding Values — Examples
# --set: Quick overrides (good for CI/CD pipelines)
helm install anihpj ./chart --set image.tag=v3.0 --set replicaCount=5

# --set for nested values (use dot notation)
helm install anihpj ./chart --set service.port=8080 --set ingress.enabled=true

# -f: Use a values file
helm install anihpj-prod ./chart -f values-prod.yaml

# Multiple -f flags (later files override earlier ones)
helm install anihpj ./chart -f values-base.yaml -f values-prod.yaml

# Combine --set and -f (--set wins for conflicting keys)
helm install anihpj ./chart -f values-prod.yaml --set image.tag=hotfix

# --set-string: Force string (prevents YAML type coercion)
helm install anihpj ./chart --set-string appVersion="2.0"
⚠️ Gotcha: --set Type Coercion

--set replicaCount=5 sets it as a number. --set-string replicaCount="5" sets it as a string. If your template expects a number but gets a string, it may fail. Use --set-string only when you explicitly need string values.

--set-json — Setting Complex Values (Helm 3.7+)

Need to set an entire array or nested object from the command line? --set-json accepts raw JSON — perfect for complex structures that are awkward with dot notation.

--set-json Examples
# Set an array (impossible with plain --set)
helm install anihpj ./chart \
  --set-json 'ingress.hosts=[{"host":"anihpj.local","paths":[{"path":"/","pathType":"Prefix"}]}]'

# Set resource limits as a complex object
helm install anihpj ./chart \
  --set-json 'resources={"limits":{"cpu":"500m","memory":"256Mi"},"requests":{"cpu":"100m","memory":"128Mi"}}'

# Set an array of environment variables
helm install anihpj ./chart \
  --set-json 'env=[{"name":"DEBUG","value":"false"},{"name":"LOG_LEVEL","value":"info"}]'

Troubleshooting Values — Common Issues

🔍
"nil pointer evaluating interface"

You referenced a nested value that doesn't exist. Check if the parent key exists in values.yaml. Use {{ .Values.x | default "fallback" }} as a safety net.

🔍
Wrong type error

Template expects an integer but got a string. Use --set (not --set-string) for numbers. Or add type conversion: {{ int .Values.replicaCount }}.

🔍
Values not taking effect

Check value precedence — are you using --reuse-values? Old values override new defaults. Use helm get values <release> --all to see what's actually applied.

🔍
Schema validation fails

Your values don't match values.schema.json. Run helm lint to see validation errors. Check required fields, types, and enum values.

4.3 Value Precedence — What Overrides What

This is one of the most important concepts for the certification. When multiple value sources conflict, Helm uses a strict precedence chain:

Value Precedence — Highest to Lowest Priority
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  VALUE PRECEDENCE (Highest → Lowest Priority)               │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  🔴 HIGHEST — User-supplied --set values             │   │
  │  │  helm install anihpj ./chart --set image.tag=v3.0    │   │
  │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤   │
  │  │  🟠 --set-file / --set-string                       │   │
  │  │  helm install anihpj ./chart -f override.yaml        │   │
  │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤   │
  │  │  🟡 Parent chart's values (for subcharts)            │   │
  │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤   │
  │  │  🟢 Multiple -f flags: LAST FILE WINS                │   │
  │  │  -f base.yaml -f prod.yaml  → prod.yaml overrides   │   │
  │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤   │
  │  │  🔵 values.yaml (chart's built-in defaults)          │   │
  │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤   │
  │  │  ⚪ LOWEST — Subchart's own values.yaml              │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                              │
  │  🔑 KEY RULE: --set always beats -f files.                   │
  │  🔑 KEY RULE: Multiple -f: later files override earlier.    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Precedence in Action
# Example: values.yaml has image.tag: "v2.0"
# values-prod.yaml has image.tag: "v2.1"

# What image tag is deployed?
helm install anihpj ./chart -f values-prod.yaml --set image.tag=v3.0
# Answer: v3.0 (--set wins over everything)

helm install anihpj ./chart -f values-prod.yaml
# Answer: v2.1 (-f overrides values.yaml)

helm install anihpj ./chart
# Answer: v2.0 (just the default)

4.4 Multiple Values Files — Environment-Specific Config

The real power of Helm values is using multiple files for different environments. The same chart can deploy to dev, staging, and production with drastically different configurations.

Multi-Environment Values Strategy
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ONE CHART → THREE ENVIRONMENTS                           │
  │                                                            │
  │  anihpj-chart/                                             │
  │  ├── values.yaml          ← BASE (common defaults)        │
  │  │   replicaCount: 2                                      │
  │  │   image.repository: anihpj/anihpj                      │
  │  │                                                         │
  │  ├── values-dev.yaml      ← DEV OVERRIDE                  │
  │  │   replicaCount: 1         (minimal resources)           │
  │  │   image.tag: latest                                     │
  │  │   ingress.enabled: false                                │
  │  │                                                         │
  │  ├── values-staging.yaml  ← STAGING OVERRIDE              │
  │  │   replicaCount: 2         (like prod, not public)       │
  │  │   image.tag: v2.1-rc1                                   │
  │  │                                                         │
  │  └── values-prod.yaml     ← PRODUCTION OVERRIDE           │
  │      replicaCount: 5         (high availability)           │
  │      image.tag: v2.0                                       │
  │      ingress.enabled: true                                 │
  │      resources:                                            │
  │        limits: {cpu: 1, memory: 512Mi}                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  DEPLOY:
  helm install anihpj-dev  ./chart -f values-dev.yaml -n dev
  helm install anihpj-stg  ./chart -f values-staging.yaml -n staging
  helm install anihpj-prod ./chart -f values-prod.yaml -n production

4.5 Schema Validation — values.schema.json

Helm 3 supports JSON Schema validation for values. Create a values.schema.json file in your chart, and Helm will validate user-supplied values against it before installing or upgrading. This catches configuration errors early.

values.schema.json
{
  "$schema": "https://json-schema.org/draft-07/schema#",
  "type": "object",
  "required": ["replicaCount", "image"],
  "properties": {
    "replicaCount": {
      "type": "integer",
      "minimum": 1,
      "maximum": 50,
      "description": "Number of application replicas"
    },
    "image": {
      "type": "object",
      "required": ["repository", "tag"],
      "properties": {
        "repository": { "type": "string", "minLength": 1 },
        "tag": { "type": "string", "pattern": "^v?\d+\.\d+" },
        "pullPolicy": {
          "type": "string",
          "enum": ["Always", "IfNotPresent", "Never"]
        }
      }
    },
    "service": {
      "type": "object",
      "properties": {
        "type": { "enum": ["ClusterIP", "NodePort", "LoadBalancer"] },
        "port": { "type": "integer", "minimum": 1, "maximum": 65535 }
      }
    }
  }
}
💡 Schema Validation on the Exam

On the certification exam, schema validation may appear as a task: "Add a values.schema.json that requires replicaCount to be between 1 and 10." Know the basic JSON Schema syntax: type, required, properties, minimum, maximum, enum, pattern.

4.6 anihpj: dev vs staging vs prod Values

Let's look at real values files for our Django job board:

🛠️
values-dev.yaml

replicaCount: 1
image.tag: latest
ingress.enabled: false
resources: {}
debug: true
Single replica, latest code, no ingress.

🧪
values-staging.yaml

replicaCount: 2
image.tag: v2.1-rc1
ingress.enabled: true
resources.limits.cpu: 500m
debug: false
Mirrors prod, tests release candidates.

🏭
values-prod.yaml

replicaCount: 5
image.tag: v2.0
ingress.enabled: true
resources.limits.cpu: 1
autoscaling.enabled: true
HA, stable version, auto-scaling.

Deploying anihpj to All Environments
# Deploy to all environments with appropriate values
helm install anihpj-dev  ./anihpj-chart -f values-dev.yaml -n dev --create-namespace
helm install anihpj-stg  ./anihpj-chart -f values-staging.yaml -n staging --create-namespace
helm install anihpj-prod ./anihpj-chart -f values-prod.yaml -n production --create-namespace

# Verify each deployment
helm list -n dev
helm list -n staging
helm list -n production

# Check what values are being used
helm get values anihpj-prod -n production
Q1
What command overrides the image tag to v3.0 during install?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm install myapp ./chart --set image.tag=v3.0

Explanation

--set overrides individual values inline. For nested keys, use dot notation: --set service.port=8080.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q2
What is the value precedence order (highest to lowest)?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

1. --set / --set-string → 2. -f files (later wins) → 3. Parent chart values → 4. values.yaml → 5. Subchart values.yaml

Explanation

Value precedence (highest to lowest): --set → -f files (last wins) → parent chart values → values.yaml → subchart defaults. --set always beats everything else.

Q3
You use -f base.yaml -f override.yaml --set image.tag=hotfix. Which image tag is used?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

hotfix. --set has the highest priority. Then override.yaml (last -f wins), then base.yaml.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q4
What file provides JSON Schema validation for values?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

values.schema.json — placed in the chart root. Helm validates user values against this schema before install/upgrade.

Explanation

values.schema.json validates user values at install/upgrade time. Key keywords: type, required, enum, minimum/maximum, pattern. Catches invalid config before reaching the cluster.

Q5
How do you deploy the same chart to dev and prod with different configs?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Create values-dev.yaml and values-prod.yaml. Deploy with: helm install app-dev ./chart -f values-dev.yaml -n dev and helm install app-prod ./chart -f values-prod.yaml -n prod.

Explanation

values.schema.json validates user values at install/upgrade time. Key keywords: type, required, enum, minimum/maximum, pattern. Catches invalid config before reaching the cluster.

Q6
Why shouldn't you store secrets in values.yaml?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

values.yaml is plain text and typically committed to Git. Use Kubernetes Secrets, helm-secrets plugin (SOPS), or External Secrets Operator instead.

Explanation

values.yaml provides defaults accessed via .Values. Best practices: comment keys, flat nesting, sensible defaults, never store secrets. Override with --set (highest) or -f files.

Q7
What does --set-string do differently from --set?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

--set auto-detects the type (number, boolean, string). --set-string forces the value to be a string. Use it when you need "5" not 5.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q8
How do you view the values used by a deployed release?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm get values <release> — shows all values (computed) for a release. helm get values --all shows all values including defaults.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q9
Where should you document what each value does?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

In values.yaml itself — use YAML comments. They serve as inline documentation. Also in chart README.md for complex configurations.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q10
How do you validate values without installing?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm lint checks Chart.yaml + templates. helm template --debug renders templates. helm install --dry-run simulates install. All validate values without actually deploying.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

📋values.yamlDefault configuration
🔝--setHighest priority override
📁-f filesEnvironment-specific configs
📐SchemaJSON validation
🏗️Multi-env1 chart × N configs
🔒SecretsNever in values.yaml

🔬 How Value Merging Works — Under the Hood

When you provide values from multiple sources (values.yaml, -f files, --set), Helm doesn't just overwrite — it performs a deep merge. Understanding this prevents surprising behavior.

Deep Merge vs Shallow Replace — Know the Difference
  VALUES.YAML (base):              OVERRIDE YAML (-f file):        MERGED RESULT:
  ┌─────────────────────┐         ┌─────────────────────┐         ┌─────────────────────┐
  │ image:              │         │ image:              │         │ image:              │
  │   repository: anihpj│    +    │   tag: v3.0         │    =    │   repository: anihpj│
  │   tag: v2.1         │         │   pullPolicy: Always│         │   tag: v3.0         │
  │   pullPolicy: IfNot..│        └─────────────────────┘         │   pullPolicy: Always│
  └─────────────────────┘                                         └─────────────────────┘

  🔑 Deep Merge: Nested keys are merged, not replaced!
     The override file only specified 'tag' and 'pullPolicy'.
     'repository' from the base was preserved automatically.

  ⚠️ BUT: If you override an entire parent key, ALL children are replaced:
  --set 'image={"repository":"newrepo","tag":"v4.0"}'
  This REPLACES the entire 'image' object — 'pullPolicy' is lost!
Debugging Value Merging
# See what values were ACTUALLY used (after all merging)
helm get values anihpj --all

# Compare: user-supplied only vs all values
helm get values anihpj         # Just what the user provided
helm get values anihpj --all   # User values merged with chart defaults

# See the rendered templates with values
helm template ./chart --debug 2>&1 | head -100

# Install with --dry-run to see rendered manifests
helm install test ./chart --dry-run --debug | less
⚠️ Common Values Pitfall: Incomplete Override

If your values-prod.yaml specifies image: but only sets tag, other image sub-keys (repository, pullPolicy) come from values.yaml. This is usually what you want! But if you need a completely different image object, specify ALL sub-keys or use --set-json to replace the entire object.

Chapter 5: Installing, Upgrading & Managing Releases

This is where Helm comes alive. Installing, upgrading, rolling back, and uninstalling — these are the commands you'll use every single day. A Release is an instance of a chart running in a cluster, and Helm tracks every change through its complete lifecycle. Mastering release management is 40% of the certification.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~2.5 hours 🚀 Operations 🎯 40% Exam Weight

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Install releases with helm install — names, atomic, wait, timeout
  • ✅ Upgrade releases with helm upgrade — reuse-values, three-way merge
  • ✅ Rollback to previous versions with helm rollback
  • ✅ Uninstall releases with helm uninstall — keep-history option
  • ✅ List and inspect releases with helm list and helm history
  • ✅ Understand the complete release state machine
  • ✅ Inspect releases with helm get and helm status
🎓 Certification Domains
Helm Core Concepts 40% Release Lifecycle HEAVILY TESTED

5.1 helm install — Your First Release (anihpj)

helm install deploys a chart for the first time, creating a new Release. This is the command that turns your chart blueprint into a running application.

helm install — Basic Usage
# Basic install (release name = chart name)
helm install anihpj ./anihpj-chart

# Install to a specific namespace
helm install anihpj-dev ./anihpj-chart --namespace dev --create-namespace

# Install with custom values file
helm install anihpj-prod ./anihpj-chart -f values-prod.yaml -n production

# Install with --set overrides
helm install anihpj ./chart --set image.tag=v3.0 --set replicaCount=5

# Dry-run: See what would be deployed without actually deploying
helm install anihpj ./chart --dry-run --debug

# Generate a random release name
helm install ./chart --generate-name
# Creates: anihpj-chart-1689273

5.1a Release Names — --generate-name vs Named Releases

✅ Named Releases (Recommended)
  • helm install anihpj-prod ./chart
  • Predictable, meaningful names
  • Easy to reference in scripts
  • Best for production environments
🎲 --generate-name
  • helm install ./chart --generate-name
  • Random name: chartname-timestamp
  • Useful for CI/CD transient deploys
  • Avoids name conflicts automatically

5.1b Atomic Installations — --atomic, --wait, --timeout

⚛️
--atomic

If the install fails, automatically roll back to the previous state. All-or-nothing. Critical for production.

⏳
--wait

Wait until all resources are ready before returning. Waits for Pods to be Running, Services to have endpoints.

⏱️
--timeout

How long to wait for --wait or --atomic. Default: 5 minutes. Set with --timeout 10m.

Production-Grade Install
# Production install: atomic + wait + timeout
helm install anihpj-prod ./anihpj-chart   -f values-prod.yaml   -n production   --create-namespace   --atomic   --wait   --timeout 10m

5.2 helm upgrade — Updating Deployed Releases

When you need to change a running release — new image, more replicas, updated config — you use helm upgrade.

helm upgrade Examples
# Upgrade to a new chart version
helm upgrade anihpj ./anihpj-chart

# Upgrade with new values
helm upgrade anihpj ./anihpj-chart -f values-prod.yaml

# Upgrade with --set
helm upgrade anihpj ./anihpj-chart --set image.tag=v3.0 --set replicaCount=5

# Install if not exists, upgrade if it does (idempotent!)
helm upgrade --install anihpj ./anihpj-chart -f values-prod.yaml

# Atomic upgrade: rollback on failure
helm upgrade anihpj ./anihpj-chart --atomic --wait --timeout 10m

# Dry-run to see what would change
helm upgrade anihpj ./anihpj-chart --dry-run --debug
💡 Always Use --install for CI/CD

helm upgrade --install is idempotent: it installs if the release doesn't exist, upgrades if it does. This is the standard pattern for CI/CD pipelines — one command that works regardless of current state.

5.2a --reuse-values vs --reset-values

🔄 --reuse-values
  • Keeps all previously set values
  • Only apply new chart defaults for NEW values
  • Use when: you want to keep current config
  • Example: helm upgrade anihpj ./chart --reuse-values
🔁 --reset-values
  • Resets to chart defaults
  • Discards all previously set values
  • Use when: you want a clean slate
  • Example: helm upgrade anihpj ./chart --reset-values -f new-values.yaml

5.2b Three-Way Strategic Merge Patch

Helm 3 uses a three-way merge to determine what changes during an upgrade. It compares: (1) the old manifest, (2) the new manifest, and (3) the current live state in Kubernetes. This prevents Helm from overwriting changes made by other controllers (like HPA or admission webhooks).

Three-Way Merge — How It Works
  ┌──────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────┐
  │  OLD MANIFEST    │   │  NEW MANIFEST    │   │  LIVE STATE      │
  │  (previous rev)  │   │  (what we want)  │   │  (in K8s now)    │
  │                  │   │                  │   │                  │
  │  replicas: 2     │   │  replicas: 3     │   │  replicas: 4     │
  │  image: v2.0     │   │  image: v2.1     │   │  image: v2.0     │
  └────────┬─────────┘   └────────┬─────────┘   └────────┬─────────┘
           │                      │                      │
           └──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┘
                                  │
                                  ▼
                    ┌─────────────────────────┐
                    │  THREE-WAY MERGE RESULT  │
                    │                          │
                    │  replicas: 3  (new wins) │
                    │  image: v2.1  (new wins) │
                    │                          │
                    │  If old=new but live      │
                    │  differs → keep live      │
                    └─────────────────────────┘

5.3 helm rollback — Going Back in Time

One of Helm's most powerful features: instant rollback to any previous revision. Each install/upgrade creates a new numbered revision.

Rollback Commands
# See revision history
helm history anihpj
# REVISION  UPDATED                   STATUS      CHART         DESCRIPTION
# 1         Mon Jun 8 10:00:00 2026   superseded  anihpj-0.1.0  Install complete
# 2         Mon Jun 8 10:30:00 2026   superseded  anihpj-0.1.0  Upgrade complete
# 3         Mon Jun 8 11:00:00 2026   deployed    anihpj-0.1.0  Upgrade complete

# Rollback to previous revision
helm rollback anihpj
# Rolls back to revision 2

# Rollback to a specific revision
helm rollback anihpj 1
# Rolls back to revision 1

# Check what happened
helm history anihpj
# REVISION 4 = the rollback (deployed)
⚠️ MAX HISTORY LIMIT

Helm stores only 10 revisions by default (--history-max). If you deploy frequently, increase this: helm install --history-max 50. Old revisions beyond the limit are automatically deleted. You can't rollback to deleted revisions!

5.4 helm uninstall — Cleaning Up

Uninstall Commands
# Uninstall a release
helm uninstall anihpj-dev

# Uninstall from a specific namespace
helm uninstall anihpj-stg -n staging

# Dry-run to see what would be deleted
helm uninstall anihpj --dry-run

5.4a --keep-history — Uninstall Without Deleting History

By default, helm uninstall deletes all release history. Use --keep-history to preserve the history for auditing or potential re-installation.

--keep-history Usage
# Uninstall but keep history records
helm uninstall anihpj --keep-history

# The release status becomes "uninstalled" but history remains
helm list --all
# NAME     NAMESPACE  STATUS        ...
# anihpj   default    uninstalled   ...

# You can still see the history
helm history anihpj

# Re-install with the same name
helm install anihpj ./chart  # Works because name was preserved

5.5 helm list — Viewing All Releases

helm list Options
# List releases in current namespace
helm list
helm ls          # Short form

# List all releases across all namespaces
helm list --all-namespaces
helm list -A     # Short form

# Show all releases including "uninstalled" and "failed"
helm list --all

# Show only failed releases
helm list --failed

# Show only deployed releases
helm list --deployed

# Filter by date
helm list --date  # Sort by date
helm list -r      # Sort by revision (reverse chronological)

5.6 helm history — Tracking Release Changes

View Release History
# View revision history
helm history anihpj

# Output format: yaml or json
helm history anihpj --output yaml
helm history anihpj -o json

# See max revisions setting
helm get values anihpj | grep historyMax
# Default: historyMax: 10
⚠️ History Max is Per-Release

If you have 10 revisions and upgrade, revision 1 is permanently deleted. You cannot rollback to it. Increase --history-max if you need longer rollback windows.

5.7 Release Lifecycle — Understanding the State Machine

Complete Release State Machine
  HELM RELEASE STATE MACHINE

         ┌─────────┐
         │ unknown │  ← State cannot be determined
         └────┬────┘
              │
    ┌─────────▼─────────┐
    │ pending-install   │  ← helm install started
    └─────────┬─────────┘
              │
         ┌────▼────┐
         │ deployed│◄──── helm install SUCCESS
         └────┬────┘     helm upgrade SUCCESS
              │          helm rollback SUCCESS
    ┌─────────▼─────────┐
    │ pending-upgrade    │  ← helm upgrade started
    └─────────┬─────────┘
              │
         ┌────▼────┐
         │ deployed│◄──── Previous revision → "superseded"
         └────┬────┘
              │
    ┌─────────▼─────────┐
    │ pending-rollback   │  ← helm rollback started
    └─────────┬─────────┘
              │
    ┌─────────▼─────────┐
    │ failed             │  ← install/upgrade/rollback FAILED
    └─────────┬─────────┘
              │
    ┌─────────▼─────────┐
    │ uninstalling       │  ← helm uninstall in progress
    └─────────┬─────────┘
              │
    ┌─────────▼─────────┐
    │ uninstalled        │  ← helm uninstall complete
    └────────────────────┘

5.7a States: unknown, deployed, superseded, failed, pending-install...

✅
deployed

Release is running successfully. This is the desired state for production releases.

📦
superseded

Previous revision replaced by a newer one. Still in history for rollback purposes.

❌
failed

Install/upgrade/rollback failed. Resources may be partially deployed. Requires cleanup.

⏳
pending-install

Install is in progress but hasn't completed yet. Transitional state.

🔄
pending-upgrade

Upgrade is in progress. Transitional state before deployed or failed.

🗑️
uninstalled

Release has been removed. Only visible with --all flag or --keep-history.

5.8 helm get — Inspecting Releases

helm get Subcommands
# Get all information about a release
helm get all anihpj

# Get computed values (merged from all sources)
helm get values anihpj
helm get values anihpj --all       # Include default values
helm get values anihpj --revision 2  # Specific revision

# Get the rendered Kubernetes manifests
helm get manifest anihpj
helm get manifest anihpj --revision 3

# Get the hooks that ran
helm get hooks anihpj

# Get the NOTES.txt output
helm get notes anihpj

5.9 helm status — Current Release State

Check Release Status
# Check current status
helm status anihpj

# Show status with revision info
helm status anihpj --revision 2

# Show status with detailed resource info
helm status anihpj --show-resources

# Output in different formats
helm status anihpj -o yaml
helm status anihpj -o json
Q1
What command installs a chart and creates a new release named "anihpj-prod" in the "production" namespace?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm install anihpj-prod ./anihpj-chart -n production --create-namespace

Explanation

On the exam, namespace accuracy is critical. Wrong namespace = 0 points. Always read questions carefully for namespace requirements. Use -n <ns> explicitly. Verify with kubectl config view --minify.

Q2
What does --atomic do during install or upgrade?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

If the operation fails, Helm automatically rolls back to the previous successful state. All-or-nothing.

Explanation

On the exam, namespace accuracy is critical. Wrong namespace = 0 points. Always read questions carefully for namespace requirements. Use -n <ns> explicitly. Verify with kubectl config view --minify.

Q3
How do you upgrade a release and keep all previously set values?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm upgrade anihpj ./chart --reuse-values. This keeps all existing values and only applies new chart defaults for new fields.

Explanation

--atomic auto-rolls back on failure — your safety net. Combine with --wait --timeout. Without it, failed releases stay broken. Essential for production and exam deployments.

Q4
What is the three-way merge in Helm 3?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Helm compares (1) old manifest, (2) new manifest, and (3) live state in K8s to determine what to change. Prevents overwriting changes made by other controllers.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q5
How do you rollback to revision 2 of a release?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm rollback anihpj 2. Use helm history anihpj first to see available revisions.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q6
What is the default maximum number of stored revisions?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

10 revisions. Increase with --history-max flag. Beyond the limit, oldest revisions are deleted and cannot be rolled back to.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q7
How do you uninstall a release but keep its history?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm uninstall anihpj --keep-history. The release status becomes "uninstalled" but history records remain.

Explanation

default is the #1 safety function: {{ .Values.x | default "safe" }}. Prevents nil pointer panics — the most common Helm error. Every nullable value reference needs this.

Q8
What command makes install/upgrade idempotent for CI/CD?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm upgrade --install anihpj ./chart. Installs if release doesn't exist, upgrades if it does — safe for repeated pipeline runs.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q9
What does helm get values show vs helm get values --all?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm get values = user-supplied values only. --all = user values + chart defaults merged. The difference helps debug configuration issues.

Explanation

helm get inspects deployed releases: values (user-supplied), values --all (merged), manifest (rendered YAML), hooks, notes. Essential for debugging configuration and drift.

Q10
A release is in "failed" state. How do you view what went wrong?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm status anihpj shows current state and error messages. helm history anihpj shows revision history. helm get all anihpj --revision <n> shows the failed revision's details.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

🛠️ Release Management Best Practices & Troubleshooting

🔧
Detecting Release Drift

Someone modified resources directly with kubectl? Use helm diff upgrade <release> <chart> (helm-diff plugin) or helm get manifest <release> | kubectl diff -f - to compare live state vs Helm's last known state.

🔧
Recovering Failed Releases

helm rollback <release> works even from failed state. If that fails: helm uninstall <release> --keep-history then helm install <release> ./chart --reuse-values (recreate with previous values).

🔧
Safe Production Upgrades

Always: helm diff upgrade → review → helm upgrade --dry-run → helm upgrade --atomic --wait --timeout 10m. The atomic flag is your safety net — failed upgrade = automatic rollback.

🔧
Finding Stale Releases

helm list --all --all-namespaces shows everything including failed and pending. helm list --failed -A finds broken releases. Clean up with helm uninstall or fix with helm rollback.

Release Management Decision Flowchart
  Release broken? ──YES──▶ helm rollback <name> ──SUCCESS──▶ 🎉 Done!
       │                                 │
       NO                               FAILED
       │                                 │
       ▼                                 ▼
  Everything OK?              helm uninstall --keep-history
       │                      helm install <name> ./chart
       ▼
  🎉 Production healthy!

  🔑 GOLDEN RULE: Always test with --dry-run first.
     helm upgrade <name> ./chart --dry-run --debug

🔄 Common Release Management Workflows

Here are the patterns you'll use every day — and what the certification exam tests:

The Three Most Common Workflows
  WORKFLOW 1: Fresh Deploy                         WORKFLOW 2: Update in Place
  ──────────────────────                           ────────────────────────
  helm install anihpj ./chart \                    helm upgrade anihpj ./chart \
    -f values-prod.yaml \                            --set image.tag=v3.0 \
    -n production                                    --atomic --wait
    --create-namespace
    --atomic --wait                                WORKFLOW 3: CI/CD Idempotent
                                                   ────────────────────────
  RESULT: New release created                       helm upgrade --install anihpj \
  Status: deployed                                    ./chart -f values-prod.yaml \
                                                       -n production \
                                                       --atomic --wait --timeout 10m

                                                   RESULT: Installs OR upgrades
                                                   Perfect for CI/CD pipelines!
🚀
First Deploy

helm install <name> ./chart -f values.yaml -n <ns> --create-namespace --atomic --wait

🔄
Update Config

helm upgrade <name> ./chart -f new-values.yaml --atomic --wait

⏪
Emergency Rollback

helm rollback <name> <revision> --wait

🔧
Safe Preview

helm upgrade <name> ./chart --dry-run --debug
Always preview before applying!

📥installCreate a new release
🔄upgradeUpdate existing release
⏪rollbackGo to previous revision
🗑️uninstallRemove a release
📋listView all releases
🔍statusCurrent release state

Chapter 6: Go Templating Fundamentals — The YAML Generation Engine

Templates are where Helm transforms from a simple package manager into a powerful code generator. Using Go's text/template library with Sprig's 70+ functions, you write templates that produce different Kubernetes YAML based on values, conditions, and loops. This chapter covers the foundational building blocks.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2 hours 🔧 Core 🎯 25% Exam Weight

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Master the {{ }} template syntax and delimiter rules
  • ✅ Understand all Built-in Objects: .Release, .Values, .Chart, .Files, .Capabilities
  • ✅ Access nested configuration through the .Values object
  • ✅ Use release metadata through the .Release object
  • ✅ Embed static files and detect K8s capabilities
🎓 Certification Domains
Templates 25% Built-in Objects FOUNDATION
Template Rendering Pipeline — How YAML is Generated
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TEMPLATE RENDERING PIPELINE                                  │
  │                                                                │
  │  ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────────┐             │
  │  │ values.  │   │ Template │   │ Rendered     │             │
  │  │ yaml     │──▶│ Engine   │──▶│ K8s YAML     │             │
  │  │ (config) │   │ (Go tmpl │   │ (final       │             │
  │  │          │   │  + Sprig)│   │  manifests)  │             │
  │  └──────────┘   └──────────┘   └──────┬───────┘             │
  │                                       │                      │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────▼───────────────────┐ │
  │  │  kubectl apply -f <rendered YAML>  (via K8s API)       │ │
  │  │  or helm install → renders + applies automatically     │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                │
  │  DEBUG: helm template ./chart --debug  → see rendered YAML   │
  │  DEBUG: helm install ./chart --dry-run → simulate install    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

6.1 Template Syntax — {{ }} Delimiters

Helm templates use Go's text/template syntax. Everything between {{ and }} is evaluated as a template expression. Everything outside is literal text (YAML).

Template Syntax Basics
# This is a Helm template file (e.g., deployment.yaml)
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: {{ .Release.Name }}-deployment    # ← Template expression
  labels:
    app: {{ .Chart.Name }}                # ← Chart name from Chart.yaml
spec:
  replicas: {{ .Values.replicaCount }}    # ← Value from values.yaml
  template:
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: {{ .Chart.Name }}
        image: "{{ .Values.image.repository }}:{{ .Values.image.tag }}"
📖 Whitespace Control

Use {{- to trim leading whitespace and -}} to trim trailing whitespace. {{- .Values.name -}} removes spaces on both sides — crucial for clean YAML output.

6.2 Built-in Objects — The Template Context

Helm provides five built-in objects that give templates access to all configuration and metadata:

⚙️
.Values

All values from values.yaml, -f files, and --set flags. Your primary data source. Access nested keys with dot notation.

🚀
.Release

Information about the release: Name, Namespace, Revision, IsUpgrade, IsInstall, Service.

📦
.Chart

Metadata from Chart.yaml: Name, Version, AppVersion, Description, Type, ApiVersion.

📁
.Files

Access static files bundled in the chart (not in templates/). Useful for ConfigMaps with config files.

🔍
.Capabilities

Kubernetes cluster information: Version, API versions available. Use for version-conditional templates.

📋
.Template

Information about the current template: Name and BasePath of the file being rendered.

6.3 The .Values Object — Accessing Configuration

.Values is your primary interface to user-supplied configuration. It contains everything from values.yaml plus overrides.

Accessing .Values
# Given this values.yaml:
# replicaCount: 3
# image:
#   repository: anihpj/anihpj
#   tag: v2.1
# service:
#   port: 80

replicas: {{ .Values.replicaCount }}           # → 3
image: "{{ .Values.image.repository }}:{{ .Values.image.tag }}"  # → anihpj/anihpj:v2.1
port: {{ .Values.service.port }}               # → 80

# Accessing a key that doesn't exist = RUNTIME ERROR
# Use default() for safety:
port: {{ .Values.service.port | default 8080 }}

6.4 The .Release Object — Name, Namespace, Revision

.Release Fields
metadata:
  name: {{ .Release.Name }}-anihpj       # Release name (e.g., anihpj-prod)
  namespace: {{ .Release.Namespace }}    # Target namespace
  annotations:
    revision: "{{ .Release.Revision }}"   # Current revision number
    isUpgrade: "{{ .Release.IsUpgrade }}" # true/false
    isInstall: "{{ .Release.IsInstall }}" # true/false
    service: {{ .Release.Service }}      # Always "Helm"

6.5 The .Chart Object — Metadata Access

Access Chart.yaml fields directly in templates. Useful for labels and annotations that should match chart metadata.

.Chart Usage
labels:
    app.kubernetes.io/name: {{ .Chart.Name }}
    app.kubernetes.io/version: {{ .Chart.AppVersion }}
    helm.sh/chart: {{ .Chart.Name }}-{{ .Chart.Version }}

6.6 The .Files Object — Embedding Static Files

Include files from your chart (config files, scripts, certificates) in ConfigMaps or Secrets.

.Files Examples
# Embed a file's content in a ConfigMap
data:
  nginx.conf: |
{{ .Files.Get "config/nginx.conf" | indent 4 }}

# Get file as base64 (for Secrets)
data:
  cert.pem: {{ .Files.Get "certs/cert.pem" | b64enc }}

# List all non-template files
{{ range $path, $_ := .Files.Glob "config/**" }}
  {{ $path }}: |
{{ $.Files.Get $path | indent 4 }}
{{ end }}

6.7 The .Capabilities Object — K8s Version & API Detection

Detect what your cluster supports at render time. Essential for backward-compatible charts.

.Capabilities Usage
# Check Kubernetes version
{{ if .Capabilities.KubeVersion.GitVersion }}
  # Running on Kubernetes {{ .Capabilities.KubeVersion.GitVersion }}
{{ end }}

# Check if an API version is available
{{ if .Capabilities.APIVersions.Has "networking.k8s.io/v1/Ingress" }}
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
{{ else }}
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1beta1
{{ end }}
kind: Ingress

🛡️ Go Template Gotchas & Best Practices

Common Template Pitfalls — And How to Avoid Them
  ❌ GOTCHA 1: Missing whitespace control
  ─────────────────────────────────────
  {{ .Values.name }}          → "  anihpj  " (extra whitespace!)
  {{- .Values.name -}}        → "anihpj"     (clean!)

  ❌ GOTCHA 2: Nil pointer when key doesn't exist
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────
  {{ .Values.missing }}       → CRASH: nil pointer evaluating...
  {{ .Values.missing | default "safe" }} → "safe"

  ❌ GOTCHA 3: Scope loss inside with/range
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  {{ with .Values.db }}
    name: {{ .host }}         ✅ . is now .Values.db
    release: {{ .Release.Name }} ❌ .Release not accessible!
    release: {{ $.Release.Name }} ✅ Use $ for root scope
  {{ end }}

  ❌ GOTCHA 4: Forgetting to quote strings with special chars
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  value: {{ .Values.special }}   → YAML parse error if ":" in value
  value: {{ .Values.special | quote }} → safe!
🛡️
Always use default()

Never assume a key exists. {{ .Values.x | default "fallback" }} prevents nil pointer panics. This is the #1 cause of template errors.

🛡️
Always quote dynamic strings

{{ .Values.name | quote }} wraps in double quotes. Prevents YAML parsing errors from special characters like colons, hashes, and brackets.

🛡️
Use $ for root scope

Inside with or range, $ always refers to the root context. {{ $.Release.Name }} works anywhere.

🛡️
Trim whitespace with {{- -}}

{{- eats leading whitespace, -}} eats trailing. Both: {{- .Values.x -}}. Critical for clean YAML.

🛡️
Use include, not template

include returns a string (pipeline-friendly). template writes inline (no pipelines). Always prefer include.

🛡️
nindent for block insertion

nindent N = newline + indent N spaces. Use when inserting YAML blocks: {{ include "labels" . | nindent 4 }}.

Q1
What delimiters does Helm use for template expressions?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

{{ and }}. Everything between them is evaluated as a Go template expression. {{- trims leading whitespace, -}} trims trailing.

Explanation
  {{ .Values.name }}       ← Evaluates to the value of .Values.name
  {{ .Values.name | quote }} ← Piped through the quote function
  {{- .Values.name -}}     ← Trims whitespace on both sides

Whitespace control is critical for clean YAML output. Without {{- and -}}, template expressions leave blank lines that can break YAML parsing.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q2
Name three Built-in Objects available in Helm templates.
Answer & Explanation
Answer

.Values, .Release, .Chart, .Files, .Capabilities. Each provides different contextual data to templates.

Explanation

These five objects plus .Template form the complete template context. Every Go template function (if, range, with) operates within this context. The dot (.) represents the current scope — initially the root context containing all built-in objects. Inside with or range blocks, . changes, but $ always refers to the root.

Explanation

helm template renders YAML locally without a cluster. Unlike --dry-run, it skips API validation. Use --debug for detailed output. Ideal for CI/CD and offline development.

Q3
How do you access a nested value like image.tag from values.yaml?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

{{ .Values.image.tag }}. Use dot notation to traverse nested keys. For safety: {{ .Values.image.tag | default "latest" }}.

Explanation

Given image: { repository: anihpj, tag: v2.1 } in values.yaml, .Values.image returns the entire map, and .Values.image.tag drills down to "v2.1". Dot notation chains through nested YAML structures. The default function prevents nil pointer panics when a key doesn't exist — a must-use safety pattern.

Explanation

values.yaml provides defaults accessed via .Values. Best practices: comment keys, flat nesting, sensible defaults, never store secrets. Override with --set (highest) or -f files.

Q4
How do you embed a file from the chart into a ConfigMap?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

{{ .Files.Get "path/to/file" | indent 4 }}. Place the file in your chart (not in templates/) and reference it with .Files.Get.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q5
How do you check if the cluster supports a specific Kubernetes API version?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

{{ if .Capabilities.APIVersions.Has "networking.k8s.io/v1/Ingress" }}...{{ end }}. Use this for version-conditional templates.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q6
What does .Release.IsUpgrade return?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

true if the current operation is an upgrade, false if it's a fresh install. Used to conditionally run upgrade-only logic.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q7
How do you reference the chart name from Chart.yaml in a template?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

{{ .Chart.Name }}. Also available: .Chart.Version, .Chart.AppVersion, .Chart.ApiVersion.

Explanation

Chart.yaml requires: apiVersion: v2, name, version. Critical: version (chart) and appVersion (app) are INDEPENDENT. type: application (deployable) or library (template-only).

Q8
What happens if you reference .Values.missingKey when the key doesn't exist?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Runtime error: template rendering fails with "nil pointer evaluating interface." Always use default: {{ .Values.missingKey | default "fallback" }}.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q9
How do you base64-encode a file's content for a Secret?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

{{ .Files.Get "certs/cert.pem" | b64enc }}. b64enc is a Sprig function that encodes strings to base64.

Explanation

Never store plaintext secrets in values.yaml. Use placeholders, inject at deploy time (--set or CI/CD), encode with b64enc in templates. Production: Sealed Secrets, External Secrets Operator, or Vault.

Q10
What objects would you use to generate Kubernetes-standard labels?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Use .Chart.Name, .Chart.Version, and .Release.Name for standard K8s labels: app.kubernetes.io/name, helm.sh/chart, app.kubernetes.io/instance.

Explanation

Never store plaintext secrets in values.yaml. Use placeholders, inject at deploy time (--set or CI/CD), encode with b64enc in templates. Production: Sealed Secrets, External Secrets Operator, or Vault.
