Chapter 7: Template Functions & Pipelines — The Power Tools

Go templates alone are limited. Helm supercharges them with 70+ Sprig functions plus custom Helm functions. This chapter covers string manipulation, type conversion, control structures (if/range/with), named templates, pipelines, and the tpl function — everything you need to write sophisticated, reusable templates.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~3 hours 🔧 Functions 🎯 25% Exam Weight

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Master string, type, math, and date transformation functions
  • ✅ Use default, required, and empty for safe value handling
  • ✅ Control template flow with if/else, with, and range
  • ✅ Create reusable templates with define, template, and include
  • ✅ Chain functions with pipelines |
  • ✅ Evaluate templates dynamically with tpl
  • ✅ Debug templates effectively
🎓 Certification Domains
Templates 25% Functions & Pipelines HEAVILY TESTED

7.1 String Functions

String Functions
# quote: Wrap in double quotes (safe for YAML strings with special chars)
name: {{ .Values.appName | quote }}               # → "anihpj"

# upper / lower: Case conversion
{{ "anihpj" | upper }}                            # → ANIHPJ
{{ "ANIHpj" | lower }}                            # → anihpj

# trim: Remove whitespace from both ends
{{ "  hello  " | trim }}                          # → "hello"

# replace: Replace substring (old new string)
{{ "anihpj-v1" | replace "-" "_" }}               # → "anihpj_v1"

# nospace: Remove all whitespace
{{ "a n i h p j" | nospace }}                     # → "anihpj"

# trunc: Truncate to N characters
{{ "anihpj-production-deployment" | trunc 10 }}   # → "anihpj-pro"

# indent: Indent every line by N spaces
data:
  config.yaml: |
{{ .Files.Get "config.yaml" | indent 4 }}

7.2 Type Conversion

Type Conversion Functions
# toYaml: Convert object to YAML string
{{ .Values.resources | toYaml | indent 10 }}
# Output:
#           limits:
#             cpu: 500m
#             memory: 256Mi

# fromYaml: Parse YAML string to object
{{ $config := .Files.Get "config.yaml" | fromYaml }}
port: {{ $config.server.port }}

# toJson / fromJson: Same but for JSON
{{ .Values.annotations | toJson }}
{{ $data := .Files.Get "data.json" | fromJson }}

# toString: Force-convert to string
{{ 42 | toString }}                              # → "42"

7.3 Default Values — Safety Nets

Default Value Functions
# default: Use fallback if value is empty/nil
replicas: {{ .Values.replicaCount | default 2 }}
image: {{ .Values.image.repository | default "nginx" }}

# required: FAIL if value is empty (for mandatory values)
{{ required "image.repository is required!" .Values.image.repository }}

# empty: Returns true if value is "empty" (0, "", nil, false, empty list/map)
{{ if empty .Values.tolerations }}
  # No tolerations configured
{{ end }}

# coalesce: Return first non-empty value from a list
{{ coalesce .Values.customName .Chart.Name "default-name" }}

7.4 Math Functions

Math Functions
# Basic arithmetic
{{ add 2 3 }}         # → 5
{{ sub 10 3 }}        # → 7
{{ mul 3 4 }}         # → 12
{{ div 10 2 }}        # → 5
{{ mod 10 3 }}        # → 1

# min / max: Find min or max of values
{{ min 1 5 3 9 }}     # → 1
{{ max 1 5 3 9 }}     # → 9

# Example: Calculate percentage
cpuPercent: {{ div .Values.usedCPU .Values.totalCPU | mul 100 }}

7.5 Date Functions

Date Functions
# now: Current date/time (in template rendering time)
deployedAt: {{ now | quote }}

# date: Format a date (layout reference: Mon Jan 2 15:04:05 MST 2006)
{{ date "2006-01-02" now }}

# dateModify: Add/subtract time
{{ now | dateModify "-24h" | date "2006-01-02" }}

# htmlDate: ISO 8601 format
{{ now | htmlDate }}

7.6 Control Structures — if/else, with, range

Control structures let your templates make decisions, iterate over data, and scope values.

7.6a if/else — Conditional Resource Inclusion

if/else Examples
{{ if .Values.ingress.enabled }}
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: Ingress
# ... ingress spec ...
{{ else }}
# Ingress disabled — no ingress resource created
{{ end }}

{{ if eq .Values.environment "production" }}
  replicas: 5
{{ else if eq .Values.environment "staging" }}
  replicas: 2
{{ else }}
  replicas: 1
{{ end }}

7.6b with — Scoping Values

with Examples
# with changes the scope (.) to the specified value
{{ with .Values.postgresql }}
  - name: POSTGRES_HOST
    value: {{ .host }}        # . now refers to .Values.postgresql
  - name: POSTGRES_PORT
    value: {{ .port | default 5432 | quote }}
{{ end }}

# If the value is empty/nil, the block is silently skipped
{{ with .Values.optionalConfig }}
  # This block only renders if optionalConfig is non-empty
{{ end }}

7.6c range — Iterating Over Lists & Maps

range Examples
# Iterate over a list
{{ range .Values.ingress.hosts }}
  - host: {{ .host | quote }}
    http:
      paths:
      {{ range .paths }}
        - path: {{ .path }}
          pathType: {{ .pathType }}
      {{ end }}
{{ end }}

# Iterate over a map (key, value)
{{ range $key, $value := .Values.env }}
  - name: {{ $key }}
    value: {{ $value | quote }}
{{ end }}

7.6d Variable Assignment — $var := value

Variable Assignment
# Assign a variable with :=
{{ $appName := .Chart.Name }}
{{ $releaseName := .Release.Name }}

# Use the variable
name: {{ $appName }}-{{ $releaseName }}

# Variables are scoped — use $ to access outer scope inside range
{{ $globalName := .Release.Name }}
{{ range .Values.services }}
  name: {{ $globalName }}-{{ .name }}
{{ end }}

⚖️ Comparison & Logical Operators Reference

OperatorMeaningExample
eqEqual{{ if eq .Values.env "production" }}
neNot equal{{ if ne .Values.env "dev" }}
ltLess than{{ if lt .Values.replicas 3 }}
gtGreater than{{ if gt .Values.replicas 1 }}
leLess than or equal{{ if le .Values.replicas 5 }}
geGreater than or equal{{ if ge .Values.memory 256 }}
andLogical AND{{ if and .Values.enabled .Values.ready }}
orLogical OR{{ if or .Values.debug .Values.verbose }}
notLogical NOT{{ if not .Values.disabled }}

7.7 Named Templates — define, template, include

7.7a define vs template vs include — Key Differences

DirectivePurposePipeline SupportUse Case
defineDeclare a named template blockN/ACreating reusable snippets
templateExecute a named template inline❌ No pipelineSimple inclusion
includeExecute + return as string✅ Yes, pipe to indent, quote, etc.Always use include!
Named Template Example (_helpers.tpl)
{{/* _helpers.tpl */}}
{{- define "anihpj.fullname" -}}
{{- .Release.Name }}-{{ .Chart.Name }}
{{- end -}}

{{/* Usage in deployment.yaml */}}
name: {{ include "anihpj.fullname" . }}
# include returns a string — you can pipe it:
name: {{ include "anihpj.fullname" . | trunc 63 | quote }}

7.8 Pipelines — Chaining Functions with |

Pipeline Examples
# Chain multiple functions with |
{{ .Values.appName | upper | quote }}
# → "ANIHPJ"

# Common pipeline: get → indent
data:
  config.yaml: |
{{ .Files.Get "config.yaml" | indent 4 }}

# Complex pipeline: get → fromYaml → toYaml → indent
{{ .Files.Get "config.yaml" | fromYaml | toYaml | indent 4 }}

# Default + quote pipeline
{{ .Values.image.tag | default "latest" | quote }}

7.9 The tpl Function — Template Inside Template

tpl evaluates a string as a template. This lets users provide template expressions in values.yaml that are rendered at deploy time. Powerful but use sparingly.

tpl Examples
# values.yaml:
# greeting: "Hello, {{ .Values.name }}!"
# name: "World"

# template:
{{ tpl .Values.greeting . }}
# → "Hello, World!"

7.10 Template Debugging

Debug Commands
# See rendered templates locally
helm template ./chart --debug

# Lint for syntax errors
helm lint ./chart

# Simulate install/upgrade
helm install test ./chart --dry-run --debug
helm upgrade test ./chart --dry-run --debug

# Show computed values
helm get values myrelease --all

# Show rendered manifests for a deployed release
helm get manifest myrelease

7.11 Lookup Function — Querying K8s API at Render Time

The lookup function queries the Kubernetes API during template rendering. Use it to read existing resources. Warning: This breaks Helm's declarative model — use sparingly.

lookup Examples
# Check if a Secret exists
{{ $secret := lookup "v1" "Secret" .Release.Namespace "anihpj-credentials" }}
{{ if $secret }}
  # Secret exists, use existing value
{{ else }}
  # Secret doesn't exist yet
{{ end }}

7.12 Sprig Function Library — 70+ Template Functions

📝
String

quote, upper, lower, trim, replace, nospace, trunc, indent, contains, hasPrefix, hasSuffix, repeat, substr, title

🔢
Math

add, sub, mul, div, mod, max, min, floor, ceil, round

📅
Date

now, date, dateModify, htmlDate, htmlDateInZone, dateInZone

📋
Lists

list, first, last, append, prepend, concat, reverse, uniq, has, slice, sortAlpha

🔤
Type

toString, toYaml, fromYaml, toJson, fromJson, typeOf, kindOf, kindIs

🔐
Encoding

b64enc, b64dec, sha256sum, sha1sum, md5sum, snakecase, camelcase, kebabcase

Q1
How do you provide a default value if .Values.replicas is not set?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

{{ .Values.replicas | default 3 }}. The default function returns the fallback if the value is nil, empty, or zero.

Explanation

default is the #1 safety function: {{ .Values.x | default "safe" }}. Prevents nil pointer panics — the most common Helm error. Every nullable value reference needs this.

Q2
What's the difference between template and include?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

template executes and writes inline (no pipeline support). include returns a string (supports pipelines like | indent, | quote). Always prefer include.

Explanation

include returns a string (pipeline-friendly: | indent). template writes inline (no pipelines). Always use include. This is a frequently tested exam concept.

Q3
How do you conditionally include a resource only in production?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

{{ if eq .Values.environment "production" }}...resource spec...{{ end }}. Use eq, ne, and, or, not for comparisons.

Explanation

Helm uses if for conditionals: {{ if .Values.ingress.enabled }}...{{ end }}. Wrap entire resources. Use eq, ne, lt, gt, and, or, not for comparisons. Resource-level conditionals are standard practice.

Q4
How do you iterate over a list of values in a template?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

{{ range .Values.ingress.hosts }}...{{ .host }}...{{ end }}. Inside range, . refers to the current item. For maps: {{ range $k, $v := .Values.env }}.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q5
How do you convert a values object to YAML inside a ConfigMap?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

{{ .Values.appConfig | toYaml | indent 4 }}. toYaml serializes the object, indent 4 adds proper indentation for ConfigMap data.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q6
What does tpl do?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

tpl evaluates a string as a template. {{ tpl .Values.dynamicGreeting . }} renders template expressions inside values at deploy time.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q7
What function forces a value to be required (fail if missing)?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

{{ required "Error message" .Values.mandatoryKey }}. If mandatoryKey is empty/nil, Helm fails with the error message.

Explanation

required FAILS if the value is empty. Use for mandatory config: {{ required "msg" .Values.key }}. Opposite of default. Catches missing config before deployment.

Q8
How do you chain multiple functions together?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Use the pipe | operator: {{ .Values.name | upper | quote }}. Each function's output becomes the next function's input.

Explanation

required FAILS if the value is empty. Use for mandatory config: {{ required "msg" .Values.key }}. Opposite of default. Catches missing config before deployment.

Q9
What does the lookup function do and when should you avoid it?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

lookup queries the K8s API at render time. Avoid it in most cases — it breaks Helm's declarative model and makes helm template fail (no API server available). Use values/config instead.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q10
Where do you define named templates and what file extension do they use?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

In files starting with _ (like _helpers.tpl). Files starting with underscore are NOT deployed as K8s resources — they only contain reusable named templates.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

🛡️Defaultsdefault, required, empty
🔀Flowif/else, with, range

Chapter 8: Building the anihpj Chart Templates — Real-World Practice

Now we apply everything from Chapters 6 and 7 to build the complete anihpj chart. Each template below is a real, working example you can adapt for your own Django applications.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2 hours 🛠️ Hands-On 🎯 25% Exam Weight

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Build a parameterized Deployment template for Django
  • ✅ Create Service, ConfigMap, Secret, Ingress, and HPA templates
  • ✅ Write helpful NOTES.txt for post-install guidance
  • ✅ Create reusable helper functions in _helpers.tpl

8.1 Deployment Template — Parameterized for Flexibility

templates/deployment.yaml
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: {{ include "anihpj.fullname" . }}
  labels: {{ include "anihpj.labels" . | nindent 4 }}
spec:
  replicas: {{ .Values.replicaCount }}
  selector:
    matchLabels: {{ include "anihpj.selectorLabels" . | nindent 6 }}
  template:
    metadata:
      labels: {{ include "anihpj.selectorLabels" . | nindent 8 }}
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: {{ .Chart.Name }}
        image: "{{ .Values.image.repository }}:{{ .Values.image.tag }}"
        imagePullPolicy: {{ .Values.image.pullPolicy }}
        ports:
        - containerPort: {{ .Values.service.port }}
        envFrom:
        - configMapRef:
            name: {{ include "anihpj.fullname" . }}-config
        - secretRef:
            name: {{ include "anihpj.fullname" . }}-secret
        resources: {{ toYaml .Values.resources | nindent 10 }}

8.2 Service Template — Ports, Type, Selectors

templates/service.yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: {{ include "anihpj.fullname" . }}
  labels: {{ include "anihpj.labels" . | nindent 4 }}
spec:
  type: {{ .Values.service.type }}
  ports:
  - port: {{ .Values.service.port }}
    targetPort: http
    protocol: TCP
  selector: {{ include "anihpj.selectorLabels" . | nindent 4 }}

8.3 ConfigMap Template — appSettings from values.yaml

templates/configmap.yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: ConfigMap
metadata:
  name: {{ include "anihpj.fullname" . }}-config
data:
  DEBUG: {{ .Values.config.debug | quote }}
  ALLOWED_HOSTS: {{ .Values.config.allowedHosts | quote }}
  DB_HOST: {{ .Release.Name }}-postgresql
  REDIS_HOST: {{ .Release.Name }}-redis-master

8.4 Secret Template — Database Credentials

templates/secret.yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: Secret
metadata:
  name: {{ include "anihpj.fullname" . }}-secret
type: Opaque
data:
  SECRET_KEY: {{ .Values.config.secretKey | b64enc | quote }}
  DB_PASSWORD: {{ .Values.postgresql.auth.password | b64enc | quote }}

8.5 Ingress Template — Optional with Conditional

templates/ingress.yaml
{{ if .Values.ingress.enabled }}
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: Ingress
metadata:
  name: {{ include "anihpj.fullname" . }}
spec:
  rules:
  {{ range .Values.ingress.hosts }}
  - host: {{ .host | quote }}
    http:
      paths:
      {{ range .paths }}
      - path: {{ .path }}
        pathType: {{ .pathType }}
        backend:
          service:
            name: {{ include "anihpj.fullname" $ }}
            port:
              number: {{ $.Values.service.port }}
      {{ end }}
  {{ end }}
{{ end }}

8.6 HPA Template — Auto-Scaling Configuration

templates/hpa.yaml
{{ if .Values.autoscaling.enabled }}
apiVersion: autoscaling/v2
kind: HorizontalPodAutoscaler
metadata:
  name: {{ include "anihpj.fullname" . }}
spec:
  scaleTargetRef:
    apiVersion: apps/v1
    kind: Deployment
    name: {{ include "anihpj.fullname" . }}
  minReplicas: {{ .Values.autoscaling.minReplicas }}
  maxReplicas: {{ .Values.autoscaling.maxReplicas }}
  metrics:
  - type: Resource
    resource:
      name: cpu
      target:
        type: Utilization
        averageUtilization: {{ .Values.autoscaling.targetCPUUtilizationPercentage }}
{{ end }}

8.7 NOTES.txt — User-Facing Output on Install

templates/NOTES.txt
🎉 Thank you for installing {{ .Chart.Name }}!

Your Django job board application has been deployed.

📋 Release: {{ .Release.Name }}
📦 Namespace: {{ .Release.Namespace }}
🔄 Replicas: {{ .Values.replicaCount }}

{{ if .Values.ingress.enabled }}
🌐 Access your application at:
{{ range .Values.ingress.hosts }}
  http://{{ .host }}
{{ end }}
{{ else }}
🔧 To access the application, run:
  kubectl port-forward svc/{{ include "anihpj.fullname" . }} 8000:{{ .Values.service.port }}
{{ end }}

📊 Check status: helm status {{ .Release.Name }}
📝 Run migrations: kubectl exec deploy/{{ include "anihpj.fullname" . }} -- python manage.py migrate

8.8 _helpers.tpl — Reusable Template Helpers

templates/_helpers.tpl
{{/* Expand the name of the chart */}}
{{- define "anihpj.name" -}}
{{- default .Chart.Name .Values.nameOverride | trunc 63 | trimSuffix "-" }}
{{- end }}

{{/* Fully qualified app name */}}
{{- define "anihpj.fullname" -}}
{{- printf "%s-%s" .Release.Name .Chart.Name | trunc 63 | trimSuffix "-" }}
{{- end }}

{{/* Common labels */}}
{{- define "anihpj.labels" -}}
app.kubernetes.io/name: {{ include "anihpj.name" . }}
helm.sh/chart: {{ .Chart.Name }}-{{ .Chart.Version }}
app.kubernetes.io/instance: {{ .Release.Name }}
app.kubernetes.io/managed-by: {{ .Release.Service }}
{{- end }}

{{/* Selector labels */}}
{{- define "anihpj.selectorLabels" -}}
app.kubernetes.io/name: {{ include "anihpj.name" . }}
app.kubernetes.io/instance: {{ .Release.Name }}
{{- end }}
Q1
How do you make a resource conditional (e.g., only create Ingress if enabled)?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

{{ if .Values.ingress.enabled }}...Ingress YAML...{{ end }}. Wrap the entire resource in an if block.

Explanation
  {{ if .Values.ingress.enabled }}
  apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
  kind: Ingress
  # ... full ingress spec ...
  {{ end }}
  # If ingress.enabled = false → entire resource is SKIPPED
  # No empty YAML, no error — just nothing rendered
Explanation

Helm uses if for conditionals: {{ if .Values.ingress.enabled }}...{{ end }}. Wrap entire resources. Use eq, ne, lt, gt, and, or, not for comparisons. Resource-level conditionals are standard practice.

⏱️ 15 minutes
🏋️ Hands-On Drill: Build & Deploy the anihpj Chart

Scenario: Your team needs a Helm chart for the anihpj Django app. Build it from scratch and deploy to a dev namespace.

  1. Scaffold: helm create anihpj-chart then delete default templates
  2. Chart.yaml: Set name, appVersion "2.1.0", type: application
  3. values.yaml: Set image.repository=anihpj/anihpj, image.tag=v2.1, service.port=8000, ingress.enabled=true
  4. deployment.yaml: Use template from Section 8.1 with ConfigMap/Secret envFrom
  5. service.yaml: ClusterIP port 8000 with selector labels from _helpers.tpl
  6. configmap.yaml: DEBUG=false, ALLOWED_HOSTS=*, DB_HOST from release name
  7. secret.yaml: SECRET_KEY and DB_PASSWORD with b64enc
  8. Validate: helm lint ./anihpj-chart
  9. Dry-run: helm install test ./anihpj-chart --dry-run --debug
  10. Deploy: helm install anihpj-dev ./anihpj-chart -n dev --create-namespace
✅ Solution & Verification 
# Verify deployment
kubectl get all -n dev
helm list -n dev
helm status anihpj-dev -n dev

# Test template rendering
helm template anihpj-dev ./anihpj-chart -f values-dev.yaml --debug

# Check computed values
helm get values anihpj-dev -n dev --all

# If broken, check the manifest
helm get manifest anihpj-dev -n dev | grep -A5 "kind:"
Q2
How do you reference a ConfigMap in a Deployment's envFrom?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

envFrom: - configMapRef: name: {{ include "mychart.fullname" . }}-config. Use include to get the computed name.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q3
What's the purpose of _helpers.tpl?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Defines reusable named templates (labels, names, selectors). Files starting with _ are NOT deployed as K8s resources. Use {{ include "helperName" . }} to invoke.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q4
How do you pass values from a Secret's data field safely?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

{{ .Values.secretValue | b64enc | quote }}. Secret data must be base64-encoded. Use b64enc to encode at render time.

Explanation

Never store plaintext secrets in values.yaml. Use placeholders, inject at deploy time (--set or CI/CD), encode with b64enc in templates. Production: Sealed Secrets, External Secrets Operator, or Vault.

Q5
How do you render resource limits/requests from values.yaml?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

resources: {{ toYaml .Values.resources | nindent 10 }}. toYaml serializes, nindent adds indentation + newline.

Explanation

Never store plaintext secrets in values.yaml. Use placeholders, inject at deploy time (--set or CI/CD), encode with b64enc in templates. Production: Sealed Secrets, External Secrets Operator, or Vault.

Q6
Where does NOTES.txt output appear?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

In the terminal after install/upgrade. It's not a K8s resource — just user-facing text. Use it for access URLs, next steps, and status commands.

Explanation

values.yaml provides defaults accessed via .Values. Best practices: comment keys, flat nesting, sensible defaults, never store secrets. Override with --set (highest) or -f files.

Q7
How do labels defined in _helpers.tpl get applied to all resources?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Each resource template includes them: labels: {{ include "mychart.labels" . | nindent 4 }}. The named template returns a YAML block that gets indented into each resource.

Explanation

Files starting with _ are NOT deployed. They contain reusable named templates via {{ define "name" }}. Always invoke with {{ include "name" . }} — never template (no pipeline support).

Q8
What does nindent do vs indent?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

indent N adds N spaces before each line. nindent N does the same but also adds a newline first — crucial when placing YAML blocks on a new line after a key.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q9
How do you reference the parent scope ($) inside a nested range?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Use $ which always refers to the root context: {{ $.Values.service.port }} works even inside nested range blocks.

Explanation

range iterates lists or maps. For lists: {{ range .Values.hosts }}{{ . }}{{ end }}. For maps: {{ range $k, $v := .Values.env }}. Use $ to access root scope inside range blocks.

Q10
What's the complete workflow for validating template changes before deploying?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm lint ./chart → helm template ./chart --debug → helm install test ./chart --dry-run → helm install prod ./chart. Lint catches syntax errors, template/debug shows rendered output, dry-run simulates the install.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

🐳 anihpj Dockerfile — The Container Behind the Chart

Helm deploys Kubernetes resources, but the actual application runs inside a container. Here's the Dockerfile that builds the anihpj (Django + jobpost) image referenced in our chart's values.yaml:

Dockerfile
# ─── anihpj Dockerfile ───
# Django Job Board Application with jobpost module
# Models: Skills, User, Author, Location, JobPost

FROM python:3.11-slim

# Set environment variables
ENV PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1 \
    PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1 \
    APP_HOME=/app

WORKDIR $APP_HOME

# Install system dependencies
RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends \
    gcc libpq-dev && \
    apt-get clean && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*

# Install Python dependencies
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt

# Copy application code
COPY . .

# Collect static files
RUN python manage.py collectstatic --noinput

# Expose Django port
EXPOSE 8000

# Run with Gunicorn (production WSGI server)
CMD ["gunicorn", "anihpj.wsgi:application", "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000", "--workers", "3"]
Build → Push → Deploy Pipeline
  ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────────────┐
  │ Docker   │     │ Container    │     │ Helm deploys     │
  │ Build    │────▶│ Registry     │────▶│ to Kubernetes    │
  │          │     │ (Docker Hub, │     │                  │
  │ docker   │     │  ACR, ECR,   │     │ helm install     │
  │ build -t │     │  GCR, etc.)  │     │ anihpj ./chart   │
  │ anihpj   │     │              │     │ --set image.tag  │
  │          │     │ docker push  │     │ =v2.1            │
  └──────────┘     └──────────────┘     └──────────────────┘

  🔑 The Docker image tag in values.yaml must match the tag you pushed!
     values.yaml: image.tag: "v2.1"  ← must match docker push tag

🧪 Template Testing Workflow — Validate Before Deploying

1
Lint

helm lint ./anihpj-chart — Check Chart.yaml syntax, template rendering, and best practices. Catches YAML errors before they reach the cluster.

2
Template (local render)

helm template anihpj-test ./anihpj-chart -f values-dev.yaml --debug — Render templates locally without cluster access. Verify the output YAML looks correct.

3
Dry-Run (cluster simulation)

helm install anihpj-test ./anihpj-chart --dry-run --debug — Simulates the install against the live cluster. Validates API versions and resource types without creating anything.

4
Deploy to Dev

helm install anihpj-dev ./anihpj-chart -f values-dev.yaml -n dev --create-namespace — Deploy to a dev namespace first. Verify pods are running and healthy.

5
Run Tests

helm test anihpj-dev -n dev — Execute test pods to verify connectivity, DB access, and API endpoints respond correctly.

6
Promote to Production

helm upgrade --install anihpj-prod ./anihpj-chart -f values-prod.yaml -n production --atomic --wait --timeout 10m

💡 Never Skip Steps 1-3

On the certification exam, always lint + template + dry-run before installing. The exam tests your ability to catch errors BEFORE deploying. A failed install wastes precious time.

Chapter 9: Chart Dependencies & Subcharts

Real applications rarely live in isolation. anihpj needs PostgreSQL for data and Redis for caching. Instead of bundling everything into one massive chart, Helm lets you declare dependencies — other charts your application needs. This chapter covers the complete dependency system.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🔗 Dependencies 🎯 20% Exam Weight

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Declare dependencies in Chart.yaml for PostgreSQL, Redis, and more
  • ✅ Manage dependencies with helm dependency update and helm dependency build
  • ✅ Compare Subcharts vs Umbrella Charts patterns
  • ✅ Share configuration across subcharts with Global Values
  • ✅ Import values from child charts into parent templates
  • ✅ Control dependency versions with SemVer constraints
🎓 Certification Domains
Advanced Features 20%

9.1 Chart.yaml Dependencies — Adding PostgreSQL, Redis, Nginx

Chart.yaml with Dependencies
apiVersion: v2
name: anihpj
version: 0.1.0
appVersion: "2.1.0"
dependencies:
  - name: postgresql
    version: "12.x.x"                              # SemVer range
    repository: https://charts.bitnami.com/bitnami
    condition: postgresql.enabled                   # Ctrl via values
    tags:
      - database
  - name: redis
    version: "~18.0.0"                             # ~ = patch-level flexibility
    repository: https://charts.bitnami.com/bitnami
    condition: redis.enabled
    tags:
      - cache
  - name: ingress-nginx
    version: "^4.0.0"                              # ^ = compatible releases
    repository: https://kubernetes.github.io/ingress-nginx
    condition: ingress-nginx.enabled

9.2 helm dependency update/build — Managing Dependencies

Dependency Commands
# Download dependencies (update Chart.lock + download to charts/)
helm dependency update ./anihpj-chart

# Build from existing Chart.lock (no network — faster)
helm dependency build ./anihpj-chart

# List current dependencies
helm dependency list ./anihpj-chart

# Update specific dependency
helm dependency update ./anihpj-chart
⚠️ Always Commit Chart.lock

Chart.lock pins exact dependency versions. Without it, CI/CD builds may download different versions. helm dependency build reads from the lock file — use it in CI for reproducible builds.

9.3 Subcharts vs Umbrella Charts — Pattern Comparison

📦 Subcharts (Recommended)
  • Each dependency is a separate chart
  • Parent chart declares deps in Chart.yaml
  • Clean separation of concerns
  • Dependencies can be updated independently
  • Standard for most Helm charts
☂️ Umbrella Charts
  • One chart that wraps multiple sub-apps
  • All templates in one chart
  • Simpler for small projects
  • Harder to version components separately
  • Consider if you have 2-3 tightly coupled services

9.4 Global Values — Sharing Config Across Subcharts

Use the global: key in values.yaml to share configuration with all subcharts. Every subchart can access .Values.global.

Global Values Example
# Parent values.yaml
global:
  imageRegistry: myregistry.com
  storageClass: fast-ssd
  postgresql:
    auth:
      postgresPassword: shared-secret

# Subcharts access these via:
# {{ .Values.global.imageRegistry }}
# {{ .Values.global.postgresql.auth.postgresPassword }}

9.5 Importing Values from Child Charts

Use the import-values directive in Chart.yaml to bring values from a child chart into the parent's .Values scope.

import-values Example
dependencies:
  - name: postgresql
    version: "12.x.x"
    repository: https://charts.bitnami.com/bitnami
    import-values:
      - child: service.ports.postgresql   # From subchart
        parent: dbPort                    # Accessible as .Values.dbPort
      - child: auth
        parent: dbAuth                    # Entire auth section imported

9.6 Dependency Version Constraints

ConstraintMeaningMatches
"12.5.0"Exact versionOnly 12.5.0
"~12.5.0"Patch-level (~)12.5.0 ≤ x < 12.6.0
"^12.5.0"Compatible (^)12.5.0 ≤ x < 13.0.0
"12.x.x"WildcardAny 12.x.x
">=12.0.0 <13.0.0"Range12.0.0 to 12.x.x
Q1
Where do you declare chart dependencies?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

In Chart.yaml under the dependencies: key. Each dependency specifies name, version (SemVer constraint), and repository.

Explanation

Dependencies are resolved by helm dependency update, which downloads the chart .tgz files into the charts/ directory and records exact versions in Chart.lock.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q2
What's the difference between helm dependency update and helm dependency build?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

update re-resolves versions and regenerates Chart.lock. build uses existing Chart.lock without network access — faster for CI/CD.

Explanation

Dependencies in Chart.yaml are resolved by helm dependency update, which downloads charts to charts/ and creates Chart.lock. Always commit Chart.lock for reproducible builds.

Q3
How do you share configuration with all subcharts?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Use the global: key in the parent's values.yaml. All subcharts access it via {{ .Values.global.xxx }}.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q4
What version constraint allows any 12.x.x release?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

"12.x.x" — the wildcard constraint. "~12.5.0" allows patches (12.5.0-12.5.x). "^12.5.0" allows compatible releases (12.5.0-12.x.x).

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q5
How do you conditionally enable/disable a dependency?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Use condition: postgresql.enabled in the dependency declaration. Set postgresql.enabled: false in values.yaml to skip that dependency.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q6
Where are dependency charts stored after download?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

In the charts/ directory as .tgz archives. Chart.lock records their exact versions and digests.

Explanation

Helm uses if for conditionals: {{ if .Values.ingress.enabled }}...{{ end }}. Wrap entire resources. Use eq, ne, lt, gt, and, or, not for comparisons. Resource-level conditionals are standard practice.

Q7
What is an umbrella chart?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

An umbrella chart wraps multiple related sub-apps as dependencies. It's a meta-chart that deploys a complete stack (e.g., anihpj + postgres + redis + ingress) via one helm install.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q8
How do you import a specific value from a child chart?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Use import-values in the dependency declaration: { child: service.port, parent: pgPort } makes .Values.pgPort available in the parent.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q9
What files does helm dependency update create/modify?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

It downloads .tgz files into charts/ and creates/updates Chart.lock with exact versions and SHA digests.

Explanation

Dependencies in Chart.yaml are resolved by helm dependency update, which downloads charts to charts/ and creates Chart.lock. Always commit Chart.lock for reproducible builds.

Q10
Should charts/ and Chart.lock be committed to Git?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Chart.lock: YES — always commit. charts/: It depends. Commit for offline builds; .gitignore for clean repos (CI runs helm dependency build). Both approaches are valid.

Explanation

Chart.lock pins exact dependency versions and digests. Always commit to Git for reproducible builds. Rebuild with helm dependency build (no network, faster, reads lock file).

📋Chart.yaml depsDeclare dependencies
⬇️helm dep updateDownload & lock
🌐global valuesShare across subcharts
📥import-valuesPull from child charts
🔒Chart.lockReproducible builds
🏷️SemVerVersion constraints

🛠️ Dependency Troubleshooting & Best Practices

🔧
"dependency not found"

Run helm dependency update to download missing charts. Check the repository URL is correct and accessible. Verify the chart name matches exactly.

🔧
Version conflict

Your SemVer constraint is too loose or too tight. Use helm dependency list to see resolved versions. Pin exact versions in Chart.lock for reproducible builds.

🔧
Conditional dependency not disabling

Check the condition: key in Chart.yaml matches a value in your values.yaml. The value must be exactly false (not "false" as a string).

🔧
Using tags for bulk control

Use tags: in dependencies + tags.database: false in values.yaml to disable all database-tagged dependencies at once. More flexible than individual conditions.

Chapter 10: Hooks & Lifecycle Management

Hooks give you control over when things happen during a Helm operation. Need to run a database migration before upgrading? Back up data before deleting? Hooks let you inject Jobs and other resources at specific points in the release lifecycle.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🪝 Hooks 🎯 20% Exam Weight

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Understand what Helm Hooks are and when to use them
  • ✅ Master all 8 hook types (pre/post-install, upgrade, delete, rollback, test)
  • ✅ Control hook execution with policies and weights
  • ✅ Create Job-based hooks for database migrations and backups
  • ✅ Build a real DB migration hook for anihpj
Helm Install Lifecycle with Hooks
  helm install myapp ./chart
       │
       ▼
  ┌─────────────────┐
  │ pre-install     │  ← Hook: Job/ConfigMap (weight: -5)
  │ (run DB backup) │
  └────────┬────────┘
           ▼
  ┌─────────────────┐
  │ Render & Apply  │  ← All non-hook resources
  │ K8s Manifests   │    (Deployment, Service, ConfigMap...)
  └────────┬────────┘
           ▼
  ┌─────────────────┐
  │ post-install    │  ← Hook: Job (weight: 5)
  │ (run DB migrate)│
  └────────┬────────┘
           ▼
  ┌─────────────────┐
  │ test            │  ← helm test (test pods)
  │ (verify app)    │
  └─────────────────┘

  HOOK TYPES: pre-install, post-install, pre-delete,
              post-delete, pre-upgrade, post-upgrade,
              pre-rollback, post-rollback, test

  WEIGHTS: Lower numbers run FIRST (-5 before 0 before 5)
  DELETION: hook-succeeded, hook-failed, before-hook-creation

10.1 What are Helm Hooks? — Pre/Post Operations

A Hook is a Kubernetes resource (usually a Job) annotated with "helm.sh/hook": <hook-type>. Instead of being deployed as part of the normal release, hooks run at specific lifecycle events — before install, after upgrade, before delete, etc.

10.2 Hook Types — All 8 Variants

📥
pre-install

Runs BEFORE resources are created. Use for: init scripts, DB schema setup.

✅
post-install

Runs AFTER all resources are created. Use for: data seeding, notifications.

🔄
pre-upgrade

Runs BEFORE upgrade applies. Use for: DB backups, pre-migration checks.

📤
post-upgrade

Runs AFTER upgrade completes. Use for: DB migrations, cache warming.

🗑️
pre-delete

Runs BEFORE resources are deleted. Use for: graceful shutdown, data export.

🧹
post-delete

Runs AFTER deletion. Use for: cleanup external resources.

⏪
pre-rollback

Runs BEFORE rollback. Use for: compatibility checks.

🔙
post-rollback

Runs AFTER rollback. Use for: notification, validation.

10.3 Hook Execution Policy — Controlling Deletion & Failure Behavior

PolicyBehavior
hook-succeededDelete hook resource AFTER it succeeds (default for Jobs)
hook-failedDelete hook resource if it FAILS
before-hook-creationDelete previous hook BEFORE creating new one (default)

10.4 Hook Weights — Controlling Execution Order

Multiple hooks of the same type run in weight order (lowest first). Default weight is 0. Negative weights run before positives.

Hook Weight Example
# Backup runs FIRST (weight: -5), then Migration (weight: 5)
annotations:
  "helm.sh/hook": pre-upgrade
  "helm.sh/hook-weight": "-5"    # Backup Job
---
annotations:
  "helm.sh/hook": pre-upgrade
  "helm.sh/hook-weight": "5"     # Migration Job

10.5 Job-Based Hooks — Running Batch Operations

Hook Job Template
apiVersion: batch/v1
kind: Job
metadata:
  name: {{ .Release.Name }}-db-migrate
  annotations:
    "helm.sh/hook": pre-upgrade
    "helm.sh/hook-weight": "5"
    "helm.sh/hook-delete-policy": hook-succeeded
spec:
  template:
    spec:
      restartPolicy: Never
      containers:
      - name: migrate
        image: "{{ .Values.image.repository }}:{{ .Values.image.tag }}"
        command: ["python", "manage.py", "migrate"]

10.6 anihpj DB Migration Hook — Pre-Upgrade Migration Job

Here's a complete, real-world hook for running Django database migrations before every upgrade of anihpj:

templates/db-migrate-hook.yaml
apiVersion: batch/v1
kind: Job
metadata:
  name: {{ include "anihpj.fullname" . }}-db-migrate
  labels: {{ include "anihpj.labels" . | nindent 4 }}
  annotations:
    "helm.sh/hook": pre-upgrade
    "helm.sh/hook-weight": "5"
    "helm.sh/hook-delete-policy": hook-succeeded,before-hook-creation
spec:
  template:
    spec:
      restartPolicy: Never
      containers:
      - name: migrate
        image: "{{ .Values.image.repository }}:{{ .Values.image.tag }}"
        command: ["python", "manage.py", "migrate", "--noinput"]
        envFrom:
        - configMapRef:
            name: {{ include "anihpj.fullname" . }}-config
        - secretRef:
            name: {{ include "anihpj.fullname" . }}-secret
Q1
What annotation marks a resource as a Helm hook?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

"helm.sh/hook": <hook-type> (e.g., pre-install, post-upgrade). Jobs are the most common hook resource type.

Explanation

Hooks are regular K8s resources with special annotations. Helm recognizes these annotations and manages the resource differently — running it at lifecycle events instead of deploying it as part of the normal release.

Explanation

Hooks run at lifecycle events via "helm.sh/hook" annotation. Types: pre/post-install/upgrade/delete/rollback, test. Synchronous — block the release. Use weights for ordering and delete policies for cleanup.

Q2
Name four hook types and when they execute.
Answer & Explanation
Answer

pre-install (before resources created), post-install (after), pre-upgrade (before upgrade), post-upgrade (after). Also: pre-delete, post-delete, pre-rollback, post-rollback, test.

Explanation

Hooks run at lifecycle events via "helm.sh/hook" annotation. Types: pre/post-install/upgrade/delete/rollback, test. Synchronous — block the release. Use weights for ordering and delete policies for cleanup.

Q3
How do you ensure one hook runs before another of the same type?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Use "helm.sh/hook-weight": "<number>". Lower numbers run first. Default is 0. Negative weights run before positives.

Explanation

Hooks run at lifecycle events via "helm.sh/hook" annotation. Types: pre/post-install/upgrade/delete/rollback, test. Synchronous — block the release. Use weights for ordering and delete policies for cleanup.

Q4
What does hook-delete-policy: hook-succeeded do?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Deletes the hook resource (e.g., Job pod) after it completes successfully. Keeps the cluster clean — no lingering completed Job pods.

Explanation

Hooks run at lifecycle events via "helm.sh/hook" annotation. Types: pre/post-install/upgrade/delete/rollback, test. Synchronous — block the release. Use weights for ordering and delete policies for cleanup.

Q5
What resource type is most commonly used for hooks?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Job — it runs a task to completion and exits. ConfigMaps and Secrets can also be hooks but they don't "execute" — they're just created at hook time.

Explanation

Hooks run at lifecycle events via "helm.sh/hook" annotation. Types: pre/post-install/upgrade/delete/rollback, test. Synchronous — block the release. Use weights for ordering and delete policies for cleanup.

Q6
When would you use a pre-upgrade hook?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

For database migrations, backups before schema changes, compatibility checks, or any task that must complete BEFORE the new version goes live.

Explanation

Hooks run at lifecycle events via "helm.sh/hook" annotation. Types: pre/post-install/upgrade/delete/rollback, test. Synchronous — block the release. Use weights for ordering and delete policies for cleanup.

Q7
How are test hooks different from other hooks?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Test hooks run on demand via helm test <release>, not automatically during install/upgrade. They validate the deployment is working correctly.

Explanation

Hooks run at lifecycle events via "helm.sh/hook" annotation. Types: pre/post-install/upgrade/delete/rollback, test. Synchronous — block the release. Use weights for ordering and delete policies for cleanup.

Q8
What annotation controls when a hook resource is deleted?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

"helm.sh/hook-delete-policy". Values: hook-succeeded, hook-failed, before-hook-creation. Multiple policies can be comma-separated.

Explanation

Hooks run at lifecycle events via "helm.sh/hook" annotation. Types: pre/post-install/upgrade/delete/rollback, test. Synchronous — block the release. Use weights for ordering and delete policies for cleanup.

Q9
Why use Jobs instead of Pods for hooks?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Jobs run to completion and track success/failure. Pods just run indefinitely. Jobs are designed for batch operations — exactly what hooks do.

Explanation

Hooks run at lifecycle events via "helm.sh/hook" annotation. Types: pre/post-install/upgrade/delete/rollback, test. Synchronous — block the release. Use weights for ordering and delete policies for cleanup.

Q10
What happens if a hook fails?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

The release operation (install/upgrade/delete) fails and the release enters a failed state. The hook Job pod may remain for debugging. Use --atomic for automatic rollback on failure.

Explanation

Hooks run at lifecycle events via "helm.sh/hook" annotation. Types: pre/post-install/upgrade/delete/rollback, test. Synchronous — block the release. Use weights for ordering and delete policies for cleanup.

🪝HooksLifecycle operations
⚖️WeightsExecution ordering
📋PoliciesDeletion control
🏗️JobsBatch operations
🔄MigrationsDB schema updates
🧪TestOn-demand validation

🪝 Common Hook Patterns & When to Use Each

Hook Decision Flowchart — Which Hook Do I Need?
  Need to run something BEFORE resources are created?
  ──YES──▶ pre-install (init scripts, schema setup)

  Need to run something AFTER resources are created?
  ──YES──▶ post-install (data seeding, notifications)

  Need to run BEFORE an upgrade takes effect?
  ──YES──▶ pre-upgrade (DB backup, compatibility check)

  Need to run AFTER an upgrade completes?
  ──YES──▶ post-upgrade (DB migration, cache warming, smoke tests)

  Need to clean up BEFORE resources are deleted?
  ──YES──▶ pre-delete (data export, deregister from load balancer)

  Need to validate the deployment works?
  ──YES──▶ test (connectivity check, API endpoint test)

  🔑 KEY: Hooks run SYNCHRONOUSLY — they block the release until complete!
     Use --timeout to prevent hung hooks from blocking forever.
⚠️ Hook Timeout Warning

If a hook hangs (e.g., a migration Job can't connect to the DB), it blocks the entire release operation. Always set --timeout on install/upgrade commands. The default timeout is 5 minutes — long enough for most operations, but adjust for large migrations.

Chapter 11: Testing & Validation — Ship with Confidence

Helm provides multiple layers of validation: linting, template rendering, dry-run simulation, schema validation, and test hooks. Together, they form a safety net that catches errors before they reach production. This chapter covers every validation technique.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🧪 Testing 🎯 20% Exam Weight

  • ✅ Write test pod definitions for connectivity and smoke testing
  • ✅ Catch errors early with helm lint
  • ✅ Preview rendered YAML with helm template
  • ✅ Simulate operations with --dry-run
  • ✅ Validate values with JSON Schema
  • ✅ Integrate testing into CI/CD pipelines
🎓 Certification Domains
Advanced Features 20% Testing & Validation PRACTICAL

11.1 Chart Tests — helm test

helm test
# Run all tests for a release
helm test anihpj-dev -n dev

# Run tests and show logs
helm test anihpj-dev --logs

# Run with timeout
helm test anihpj-dev --timeout 5m

11.2 Test Pods — Writing Test Cases

templates/tests/test-connection.yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: "{{ include "anihpj.fullname" . }}-test-connection"
  annotations:
    "helm.sh/hook": test
spec:
  containers:
  - name: wget
    image: busybox
    command: ['wget']
    args: ['{{ include "anihpj.fullname" . }}:{{ .Values.service.port }}']
  restartPolicy: Never

11.3 helm lint — Catching Template Errors Early

Linting
# Lint a chart
helm lint ./anihpj-chart

# Lint with strict mode (warnings become errors)
helm lint ./anihpj-chart --strict

# Lint with specific values
helm lint ./anihpj-chart -f values-prod.yaml

11.4 helm template — Rendering Without Installing

Template Rendering
# Render templates locally (no cluster needed)
helm template anihpj ./anihpj-chart -f values-prod.yaml

# Render with debug output
helm template anihpj ./anihpj-chart --debug

# Render and validate with kubeval
helm template ./chart | kubeval --strict

11.5 --dry-run — Simulating Install/Upgrade

Dry-Run Simulation
# Simulate install (validates against live cluster)
helm install anihpj-test ./chart --dry-run --debug

# Simulate upgrade
helm upgrade anihpj ./chart --dry-run --debug

# Dry-run is NOT a template render — it talks to the API server
# It validates: API versions, resource types, webhooks

11.6 Schema Validation — values.schema.json Deep Dive

values.schema.json
{
  "$schema": "https://json-schema.org/draft-07/schema#",
  "type": "object",
  "required": ["replicaCount", "image"],
  "properties": {
    "replicaCount": {
      "type": "integer",
      "minimum": 1,
      "maximum": 50
    },
    "image": {
      "type": "object",
      "required": ["repository", "tag"],
      "properties": {
        "repository": { "type": "string", "minLength": 1 },
        "tag": { "type": "string", "pattern": "^v?\\d+\\.\\d+" },
        "pullPolicy": {
          "type": "string",
          "enum": ["Always", "IfNotPresent", "Never"]
        }
      }
    }
  }
}

11.7 CI/CD with helm test — Integrating into Pipelines

1
Lint

helm lint ./chart --strict — Fail fast on syntax errors.

2
Template

helm template ./chart -f values-ci.yaml — Render locally.

3
Dry-Run

helm install test ./chart --dry-run — Validate against cluster.

4
Install

helm upgrade --install test ./chart -n ci --atomic — Deploy to CI namespace.

5
Test

helm test test -n ci --logs — Run test hooks.

6
Cleanup

helm uninstall test -n ci — Remove CI deployment.

Q1
What command runs chart tests for a deployed release?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm test <release> -n <namespace>. This executes all test hook resources (usually test pods). Use --logs to see output.

Explanation

Test hooks are annotated with "helm.sh/hook": test. They run on demand, not automatically. This separates validation from deployment — you deploy first, then test. 

# Test hook pod template:
metadata:
  annotations:
    "helm.sh/hook": test           # Only runs with helm test
    "helm.sh/hook-delete-policy": before-hook-creation
spec:
  containers:
  - name: test
    command: ["curl", "http://myapp-service/smoke"]
  restartPolicy: Never

# Test command: helm test my-release -n prod --logs

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q2
What's the difference between helm lint and helm template?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm lint checks Chart.yaml syntax and template rendering without producing output. helm template renders templates to YAML locally. Lint = validate, Template = render.

Explanation

helm lint catches syntax errors, missing fields, and deprecated APIs. Run it FIRST — a 1-second lint saves 15 minutes of debugging. Use --strict to treat warnings as errors.

Q3
How does --dry-run differ from helm template?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm template renders locally (offline). --dry-run connects to the K8s API server and validates API versions, resource types, and admission webhooks — but doesn't create anything.

Explanation

helm template renders YAML locally without a cluster. Unlike --dry-run, it skips API validation. Use --debug for detailed output. Ideal for CI/CD and offline development.

Q4
Where are test pod definitions typically placed?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

In templates/tests/. Common test files: test-connection.yaml (HTTP check), test-db.yaml (database connectivity).

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q5
What JSON Schema keyword ensures a value is within a range?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

"minimum" and "maximum" for numbers. "minLength" and "maxLength" for strings. "enum" for restricted choices.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q6
What makes a Pod a test pod?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

The annotation "helm.sh/hook": test. This tells Helm to run this pod only when helm test is executed, not during normal install/upgrade.

Explanation

values.schema.json validates user values at install/upgrade time. Key keywords: type, required, enum, minimum/maximum, pattern. Catches invalid config before reaching the cluster.

Q7
How do you validate values against values.schema.json?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Helm automatically validates values against values.schema.json at install/upgrade time. You can also run helm lint which triggers schema validation.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q8
What CI/CD step should come first: lint, template, or dry-run?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Lint (fastest, catches syntax errors) → Template (render locally) → Dry-run (validate against cluster) → Install (deploy) → Test (verify).

Explanation

--dry-run simulates without creating resources. Validates API versions and webhooks against the cluster. Always use before deploying — catches 80% of errors in 2 seconds.

Q9
Can you run helm template without a Kubernetes cluster?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Yes. helm template is purely local — it renders templates to YAML without any cluster connection. Great for CI/CD and offline development.

Explanation

--dry-run simulates without creating resources. Validates API versions and webhooks against the cluster. Always use before deploying — catches 80% of errors in 2 seconds.

Q10
What flag shows test pod logs?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm test <release> --logs. This streams test pod output so you can see what passed or failed and why.

Explanation

helm template renders YAML locally without a cluster. Unlike --dry-run, it skips API validation. Use --debug for detailed output. Ideal for CI/CD and offline development.

🧪dry-runCluster simulation
📐schemaJSON validation
🏃helm testRun test hooks

📊 Validation Methods — Which One When?

MethodSpeedNeeds Cluster?CatchesExam Priority
helm lint⚡ Instant❌ NoSyntax errors, missing required fields, deprecated APIs🔴 ALWAYS FIRST
helm template⚡ Fast❌ NoTemplate rendering errors, missing values, nil pointers🟡 SECOND
--dry-run🐢 Moderate✅ YesAPI version mismatches, webhook rejections, resource conflicts🟢 THIRD
helm test🐢 Slow✅ YesApplication-level issues (connectivity, API responses, DB access)🔵 AFTER DEPLOY
Schema validation⚡ Instant❌ NoInvalid value types, out-of-range values, missing required fields🟡 AUTO
💡 The Validation Pyramid

Run validations in this order: Lint → Template → Dry-Run → Install → Test. Each layer catches different issues. Lint catches 80% of problems before they waste cluster resources. Never skip lint — it takes 1 second and prevents 15 minutes of debugging.

Chapter 12: Chart Repositories & Distribution

Charts need a home. Whether you're consuming community charts or distributing your own, repositories are the distribution mechanism for Helm. This chapter covers everything from public registries to private OCI repositories, chart signing, and version management strategies.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 📦 Distribution 🎯 15% Exam Weight

  • ✅ Sign charts and verify provenance with GPG keys
  • ✅ Configure private repositories with authentication
  • ✅ Manage chart versions and rollout strategies

12.1 Public Repositories — Bitnami, Artifact Hub, Stable

12.2 Creating Your Own Chart Repository

🏛️
ChartMuseum

Lightweight, open-source Helm chart repository server. Supports S3, GCS, Azure, and local storage. Simple HTTP API for pushing charts.

🏗️
Harbor

Enterprise-grade container + Helm chart registry. Built-in RBAC, vulnerability scanning, replication, and OCI support.

☁️
OCI Registries

Use any OCI-compliant registry (Docker Hub, ECR, ACR, GCR) to store Helm charts. No separate chart server needed!

12.3 OCI-Based Charts — helm push to Container Registry

Helm 3.8+ supports OCI registries natively. Store charts alongside container images in the same registry — simpler infrastructure.

OCI Chart Operations
# Login to OCI registry
helm registry login registry.example.com -u myuser -p mypass

# Package and push
helm package ./anihpj-chart
helm push anihpj-0.1.0.tgz oci://registry.example.com/charts

# Pull from OCI
helm pull oci://registry.example.com/charts/anihpj --version 0.1.0

# Install directly from OCI
helm install anihpj oci://registry.example.com/charts/anihpj --version 0.1.0

# List tags
helm show chart oci://registry.example.com/charts/anihpj
💡 OCI = Simpler Infrastructure

With OCI support, you don't need a separate chart repository server. Your existing container registry (Docker Hub, ECR, ACR, GCR) now stores both images AND charts — one less service to manage.

12.4 Chart Signing & Provenance — helm package --sign

Signing Charts
# Generate a GPG key (one-time)
gpg --full-generate-key

# Package and sign
helm package ./anihpj-chart --sign --key 'devops@anihpj.com' --keyring ~/.gnupg/secring.gpg

# Verify a signed chart
helm verify anihpj-0.1.0.tgz
# Output: Signed by: DevOps Team

12.5 Private Repositories — Authentication & Access Control

Private Repository Auth
# Add private repo with basic auth
helm repo add myprivate https://charts.mycompany.com --username admin --password secret

# Add repo with username prompt (safer)
helm repo add myprivate https://charts.mycompany.com --username admin
# Helm will prompt for the password

# OCI registry login
helm registry login myregistry.azurecr.io -u myuser -p mytoken

12.6 Chart Version Management & Rollout Strategies

📌
Semantic Versioning

Follow MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH. Bump MAJOR for breaking changes, MINOR for new features, PATCH for fixes.

🔄
Canary Releases

Deploy new chart version to a subset of users first. Monitor metrics, then promote. helm install canary ./chart --set canary.enabled=true

🔙
Rollback Ready

Every release is versioned. helm rollback restores any previous version in seconds. Keep --history-max high enough.

Q10
How should you manage chart versions for production rollouts?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Use SemVer (MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH). Bump MAJOR for breaking changes. Use canary releases to test new versions. Keep --history-max high for rollback safety.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

🌐Public ReposBitnami, Artifact Hub
🏛️Private ReposChartMuseum, Harbor
📦OCIContainer registries
🔏SigningGPG provenance
🔐AuthPrivate repo access
📌VersioningSemVer + rollback

🔀 Choosing Your Repository Strategy

Repository Decision Tree
  Need to share charts publicly?
  ──YES──▶ Artifact Hub (list your repo there)
  │
  NO — Internal/private charts?
  │
  ├── Already have a container registry (Docker Hub, ECR, ACR, GCR)?
  │   ──YES──▶ Use OCI! Store charts alongside images. Simplest option.
  │
  ├── Need enterprise features (RBAC, scanning, replication)?
  │   ──YES──▶ Harbor — full-featured registry for containers + charts.
  │
  └── Need something lightweight and simple?
      ──YES──▶ ChartMuseum — single binary, S3/GCS/Azure backends.

  🔑 For most teams: OCI is the future. Harbor for enterprises.
     ChartMuseum if you need a quick, standalone chart server.

Chapter 13: Security Best Practices

Security in Helm spans RBAC permissions, secrets handling, value protection, provenance verification, network policies, and vulnerability scanning. A single misconfigured chart can expose your entire cluster. This chapter builds your security mindset.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🔒 Security 🎯 15% Exam Weight

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Configure RBAC for Helm operations with ServiceAccounts and ClusterRoles
  • ✅ Manage secrets with Sealed Secrets, External Secrets Operator, and HashiCorp Vault
  • ✅ Never commit secrets to values.yaml — use secure alternatives
  • ✅ Verify chart authenticity with provenance checks
  • ✅ Apply Network Policies and SecurityContexts for defense in depth
  • ✅ Scan container images in charts for CVEs
🎓 Certification Domains
Production 15% Security CRITICAL

13.1 RBAC for Helm — ServiceAccounts & ClusterRoles

Helm RBAC Example
apiVersion: v1
kind: ServiceAccount
metadata:
  name: helm-deployer
  namespace: production
---
apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: Role
metadata:
  name: helm-deployer
  namespace: production
rules:
- apiGroups: [""]
  resources: ["secrets", "configmaps", "services", "pods"]
  verbs: ["get", "list", "create", "update", "delete"]
- apiGroups: ["apps"]
  resources: ["deployments"]
  verbs: ["get", "list", "create", "update", "delete"]
---
apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: RoleBinding
metadata:
  name: helm-deployer
  namespace: production
subjects:
- kind: ServiceAccount
  name: helm-deployer
roleRef:
  kind: Role
  name: helm-deployer
  apiGroup: rbac.authorization.k8s.io

13.2 Secrets Management — Sealed Secrets, External Secrets, Vault

🔐
Sealed Secrets

Encrypt secrets into a SealedSecret CRD that can be safely committed to Git. Only the cluster controller can decrypt them.

🔗
External Secrets

Sync secrets from external providers (AWS Secrets Manager, GCP Secret Manager, Azure Key Vault) into Kubernetes Secrets automatically.

🏦
HashiCorp Vault

Enterprise-grade secret management. Inject secrets into pods via sidecar or CSI driver. Integrate with Helm via vault CLI or API.

13.3 Values Security — Never Commit Secrets to values.yaml

Secure Values Workflow
  ❌ WRONG: Secrets in values.yaml          ✅ RIGHT: Secrets via external tools
  ─────────────────────────────             ────────────────────────────────
  values.yaml:                              values.yaml:
    dbPassword: "MySecret123"  ← NO!          dbPassword: ""  ← placeholder
    apiKey: "sk-abc123"       ← NO!           apiKey: ""     ← placeholder
                                            helm install ./chart \
  Committed to Git = exposed!                 --set dbPassword=$(vault read -field=pw secret/db) \
                                              --set apiKey=$(aws secretsmanager get-secret-value ...)

13.4 Chart Provenance Verification — helm verify

Provenance Verification
# Verify chart signature and provenance
helm verify anihpj-0.1.0.tgz

# Install only if verified
helm verify anihpj-0.1.0.tgz && helm install anihpj ./anihpj-0.1.0.tgz

# Check provenance file
tar -xzf anihpj-0.1.0.tgz anihpj/Chart.yaml
cat anihpj-0.1.0.tgz.prov

13.5 Network Policies & SecurityContext

Your chart should include security hardening as a best practice. Add a securityContext to pods and optionally include NetworkPolicy templates.

Security Best Practices in Templates
spec:
  securityContext:
    runAsNonRoot: true
    runAsUser: 1000
    fsGroup: 2000
  containers:
  - name: {{ .Chart.Name }}
    securityContext:
      allowPrivilegeEscalation: false
      readOnlyRootFilesystem: true
      capabilities:
        drop: ["ALL"]

13.6 CVE Scanning for Chart Images

Scanning Images in Charts
# Scan with Trivy
trivy image anihpj/anihpj:v2.1

# Scan in CI pipeline
helm template ./chart | grep "image:" | awk '{print $2}' | tr -d '"' | while read img; do
  trivy image --severity HIGH,CRITICAL "$img"
done

# Use Harbor's built-in vulnerability scanner
# Harbor automatically scans images pushed to its registry
Q1
What Kubernetes object should you create to give Helm limited deployment permissions?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

A ServiceAccount with a Role (or ClusterRole) and RoleBinding. This limits what Helm can create/delete in the cluster.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q2
Why should you never store secrets in values.yaml?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

values.yaml is plain text and typically committed to Git. Secrets in Git = exposed credentials. Use Sealed Secrets, External Secrets, or Vault instead.

Explanation

values.yaml provides defaults accessed via .Values. Best practices: comment keys, flat nesting, sensible defaults, never store secrets. Override with --set (highest) or -f files.

Q3
What tool can encrypt Kubernetes Secrets for safe Git storage?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Sealed Secrets (Bitnami). Encrypts secrets into SealedSecret CRDs. Only the cluster controller can decrypt. Safe to commit to Git.

Explanation

values.yaml provides defaults accessed via .Values. Best practices: comment keys, flat nesting, sensible defaults, never store secrets. Override with --set (highest) or -f files.

Q4
How do you verify a chart hasn't been tampered with?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm verify chart.tgz. This checks the GPG signature and provenance file. Requires the signer's public key in your keyring.

Explanation

Never store plaintext secrets in values.yaml. Use placeholders, inject at deploy time (--set or CI/CD), encode with b64enc in templates. Production: Sealed Secrets, External Secrets Operator, or Vault.

Q5
What securityContext settings make a pod more secure?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

runAsNonRoot: true, readOnlyRootFilesystem: true, allowPrivilegeEscalation: false, capabilities.drop: ["ALL"].

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q6
What tool scans container images for CVEs?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Trivy (Aqua Security) — open-source, fast, comprehensive. Also: Clair, Snyk, and Harbor's built-in scanner.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q7
How does External Secrets Operator work?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

It syncs secrets from external providers (AWS/GCP/Azure/Vault) into Kubernetes Secrets. Define an ExternalSecret CRD, and the operator keeps K8s Secrets in sync.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q8
What is the principle of least privilege in Helm RBAC?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Give the ServiceAccount only the permissions needed for the chart to work. Don't use cluster-admin. Use namespaced Roles, not ClusterRoles, when possible.

Explanation

Never store plaintext secrets in values.yaml. Use placeholders, inject at deploy time (--set or CI/CD), encode with b64enc in templates. Production: Sealed Secrets, External Secrets Operator, or Vault.

Q9
What file proves a chart was signed by a specific identity?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

The provenance file (.tgz.prov). Generated by helm package --sign. Contains the chart's hash and the signer's digital signature.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q10
How should you handle secrets in CI/CD pipelines that use Helm?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Inject secrets at deploy time via CI/CD secret variables: helm upgrade --install app ./chart --set dbPassword=$CI_DB_PASSWORD. Never store secrets in the chart repo.

Explanation

Never store plaintext secrets in values.yaml. Use placeholders, inject at deploy time (--set or CI/CD), encode with b64enc in templates. Production: Sealed Secrets, External Secrets Operator, or Vault.

🛡️RBACLeast privilege access
🔐SecretsNever in values.yaml
✅VerifyProvenance checks
🔒SecurityContextPod hardening
🔍ScanningCVE detection
🌐NetPolNetwork isolation

✅ Helm Security Checklist — Production Readiness

1️⃣
Use dedicated ServiceAccounts

Never use default SA for Helm. Create a dedicated SA with minimal RBAC permissions. Scope to specific namespaces.

2️⃣
Externalize secrets

Use Sealed Secrets, External Secrets Operator, or Vault. Never commit plaintext secrets to Git. Override at deploy time via CI/CD.

3️⃣
Verify chart provenance

Run helm verify before installing any chart from external sources. Only install signed charts in production.

4️⃣
Harden pod security

Set runAsNonRoot: true, drop ALL capabilities, use readOnlyRootFilesystem. Add these to your chart's default values.

5️⃣
Scan images regularly

Integrate Trivy or Harbor scanning in CI/CD. Block deployments with CRITICAL CVEs. Update base images frequently.

6️⃣
Apply Network Policies

Restrict pod-to-pod communication. Only allow necessary ingress/egress. Default-deny with specific allows.

7️⃣
Limit release history

Set --history-max to a reasonable number. Old revisions contain sensitive values. Clean up unused releases.

8️⃣
Use private repositories

Host charts in private OCI registries or Harbor. Authenticate with helm registry login. Never expose internal charts publicly.

  ┌────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐

              ┌────────────────────────────────────┘

  │ (validate)   │   │ (verify)  │   │ (STAGING)     │
  └──────────────┘   └───────────┘   └──────┬───────┘

              ┌──────────────────────────────┘
              ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐
