                Argo CD (GitOps), Tekton Pipelines
Helm vs Alternatives — When to Use What
  TOOL          BEST FOR...            WHEN TO USE
  ────────────  ─────────────────────  ─────────────────────
  Helm          Packaging reusable     Sharing charts across
                K8s applications       teams/orgs
  ────────────  ─────────────────────  ─────────────────────
  kubectl       One-off deployments    Quick tests, no reuse
  ────────────  ─────────────────────  ─────────────────────
  Kustomize     Environment-specific   Multiple envs from
                overlays (no templates) same base (no params)
  ────────────  ─────────────────────  ─────────────────────
  Operators     Complex stateful apps  Databases, monitoring
                with custom logic      with day-2 operations
  ────────────  ─────────────────────  ─────────────────────
  Argo CD       GitOps continuous      Git as single source
                delivery               of truth (can use Helm)
  ────────────  ─────────────────────  ─────────────────────
  Helmfile      Declarative helm       Managing 50+ releases
                release orchestration  declaratively

14.1 GitOps with Helm — Argo CD + Helm Integration

Argo CD watches a Git repository for chart changes and automatically syncs them to your cluster. Git is the single source of truth.

Argo CD Application (using Helm)
apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
kind: Application
metadata:
  name: anihpj-prod
spec:
  project: default
  source:
    repoURL: https://github.com/anihpj/charts
    path: anihpj-chart
    targetRevision: main
    helm:
      valueFiles:
      - values-prod.yaml
  destination:
    server: https://kubernetes.default.svc
    namespace: production
  syncPolicy:
    automated:
      prune: true
      selfHeal: true

14.2 Helm in GitHub Actions / GitLab CI

GitHub Actions Workflow
name: Deploy with Helm
on:
  push:
    branches: [main]
jobs:
  deploy:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
    - uses: actions/checkout@v3
    - name: Helm Lint
      run: helm lint ./chart
    - name: Helm Template
      run: helm template test ./chart -f values-prod.yaml
    - name: Deploy to Production
      run: |
        helm upgrade --install anihpj-prod ./chart \
          -f values-prod.yaml \
          -n production --atomic --wait --timeout 10m

14.3 Automated Chart Testing in CI

1
Lint

helm lint ./chart --strict — syntax and best practice validation.

2
Template

helm template test ./chart -f ci-values.yaml — render YAML locally.

3
Install

helm install test ./chart -n ci --create-namespace --atomic

4
Test

helm test test -n ci --logs — run test hooks.

5
Cleanup

helm uninstall test -n ci

14.4 Multi-Environment Deployments with Helm

Multi-Environment Deploy
# Same chart, different values per environment
helm upgrade --install anihpj-dev  ./chart -f values-dev.yaml  -n dev  --atomic --wait
helm upgrade --install anihpj-stg  ./chart -f values-staging.yaml -n staging --atomic --wait
helm upgrade --install anihpj-prod ./chart -f values-prod.yaml -n production --atomic --wait

14.5 Helmfile — Declarative Helm Releases

Helmfile lets you declare all your releases in a single YAML file. Instead of running helm install for each release, you run helmfile apply once.

helmfile.yaml
repositories:
- name: bitnami
  url: https://charts.bitnami.com/bitnami
releases:
- name: anihpj-prod
  namespace: production
  chart: ./anihpj-chart
  values:
  - values-prod.yaml
- name: postgresql
  namespace: production
  chart: bitnami/postgresql
  version: 12.5.0
- name: redis
  namespace: production
  chart: bitnami/redis
  version: 18.0.0

14.6 Blue-Green & Canary Deployments with Helm

🔵🟢
Blue-Green

Deploy new version (green) alongside old (blue). Switch traffic via Service selector. Instant rollback: switch selector back. helm install anihpj-green ./chart --set color=green

🐤
Canary

Gradually shift traffic to new version. Start with 10%, monitor, increase. helm upgrade anihpj-canary ./chart --set canary.weight=10

Q1
What is GitOps and how does Helm fit into it?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

GitOps uses Git as the single source of truth. Helm charts are stored in Git. Argo CD or Flux watches the repo and syncs changes to the cluster automatically.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q2
What CI step should run before helm install in a pipeline?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm lint (syntax check), then helm template (render YAML), then helm install --dry-run (validate against cluster). All three before actual install.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q3
What is Helmfile?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

A declarative tool for managing multiple Helm releases. Define all releases in helmfile.yaml and apply them with one command: helmfile apply.

Explanation

helm install creates a new release tracked in Kubernetes Secrets. Key flags: --atomic (auto-rollback), --wait (wait for readiness), --dry-run (validate first). Always dry-run before deploying on the exam.

Q4
How do you deploy the same chart to dev, staging, and prod?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Use different values files: helm install app-dev ./chart -f values-dev.yaml -n dev, helm install app-prod ./chart -f values-prod.yaml -n production.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q5
How does Argo CD deploy a Helm chart?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Argo CD reads an Application CRD that points to a Git repo + chart path. It runs helm template and applies the rendered YAML. Git changes trigger automatic sync.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q6
What is the idempotent install/upgrade command for CI/CD?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm upgrade --install <release> ./chart -f values.yaml -n <ns> --atomic --wait --timeout 10m. Works whether the release exists or not.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q7
What's the difference between Blue-Green and Canary deployments?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Blue-Green: two complete environments, switch traffic all at once. Canary: gradually shift a percentage of traffic. Blue-Green = instant rollback. Canary = gradual validation.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q8
How do you automate chart testing in CI?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Deploy to a temporary CI namespace, run helm test, then uninstall. Lint → Template → Install → Test → Cleanup. All in a pipeline job.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q9
When should you use Kustomize instead of Helm?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

For environment-specific overlays on existing YAML (no templating needed). Kustomize patches base YAML without parameters. Helm is better for reusable, parameterized charts.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q10
What makes --atomic critical for CI/CD pipelines?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

If the upgrade fails, --atomic automatically rolls back to the previous working state. Prevents broken deployments from staying live in production.

Explanation

--atomic auto-rolls back on failure — your safety net. Combine with --wait --timeout. Without it, failed releases stay broken. Essential for production and exam deployments.

🏋️ Hands-On Drill: Build a Complete CI/CD Pipeline with Helm

Scenario: Set up a CI/CD pipeline that lints, tests, and deploys the anihpj chart to staging, then promotes to production.

  1. Lint: helm lint ./anihpj-chart --strict
  2. Template: helm template test ./anihpj-chart -f values-staging.yaml --debug
  3. Deploy to Staging: helm upgrade --install anihpj-stg ./anihpj-chart -f values-staging.yaml -n staging --create-namespace --atomic --wait
  4. Test: helm test anihpj-stg -n staging --logs
  5. Verify: kubectl get all -n staging and curl the service endpoint
  6. Promote to Production: helm upgrade --install anihpj-prod ./anihpj-chart -f values-prod.yaml -n production --atomic --wait --timeout 10m
  7. Verify Production: helm status anihpj-prod -n production
✅ Complete Pipeline Script 
#!/bin/bash
set -e
CHART="./anihpj-chart"
echo "🔍 Linting..."
helm lint $CHART --strict
echo "📄 Rendering templates..."
helm template test $CHART -f values-staging.yaml --debug > /dev/null
echo "🚀 Deploying to staging..."
helm upgrade --install anihpj-stg $CHART -f values-staging.yaml -n staging --create-namespace --atomic --wait --timeout 5m
echo "🧪 Running tests..."
helm test anihpj-stg -n staging --logs
echo "✅ Staging verified. Promoting to production..."
helm upgrade --install anihpj-prod $CHART -f values-prod.yaml -n production --atomic --wait --timeout 10m
echo "🎉 Production deployed! Status:"
helm status anihpj-prod -n production

🏭 CI/CD Pipeline Best Practices for Helm

🔒
Never commit secrets to chart repo

Inject secrets at deploy time via CI/CD variables. helm upgrade --install app ./chart --set dbPassword=$CI_SECRET. Values files in Git should contain placeholders, never real secrets.

📦
Version charts automatically

Use CI to auto-increment chart version on merge. Tools like helm-bump or semantic-release can automate SemVer bumps.

🧪
Test in isolated namespaces

Each CI run deploys to a unique namespace: helm install pr-123 ./chart -n ci-pr-123 --create-namespace. Clean up after tests pass. No cross-contamination.

📊
Monitor rollout health

After deploy, wait for rollout: kubectl rollout status deploy/app -n production --timeout=5m. Alert if rollout doesn't complete.

🔙
Auto-rollback on failure

Always use --atomic flag. If deployment fails, Helm auto-rolls back. Add health checks to your templates so failures are detected quickly.

📝
Generate change logs

Use helm diff plugin in CI to show what changed. Post the diff as a PR comment for review before merge. helm diff upgrade app ./chart -f values-prod.yaml

CI/CD Decision Flow for Helm Deployments
  Git Push → PR Opened?
  ──YES──▶ helm lint + helm template (validate) → helm diff (show changes)
  │         → Review diff → Merge → Deploy pipeline
  │
  ──NO (merge to main)──▶
  │
  ▼
  helm lint --strict
  │  FAIL → ❌ Stop pipeline, notify
  ▼
  helm template -f values-prod.yaml
  │  FAIL → ❌ Template error, fix
  ▼
  helm install test ./chart -n ci --dry-run
  │  FAIL → ❌ API validation error
  ▼
  helm upgrade --install prod ./chart -f values-prod.yaml --atomic --wait --timeout 10m
  │  FAIL → Auto-rollback (--atomic), alert team
  ▼
  helm test prod --logs
  │  FAIL → ⚠️ App issue, investigate
  ▼
  🎉 Deployed successfully!

15.3 Terminal Setup & Aliases

Exam Terminal Setup
# Essential Helm aliases
alias h=helm
alias hi='helm install'
alias hu='helm upgrade --install'
alias hls='helm list'
alias hrb='helm rollback'
alias hun='helm uninstall'
alias hgv='helm get values'
alias hgm='helm get manifest'

# Enable autocompletion
source <(helm completion bash)
source <(kubectl completion bash)
alias k=kubectl
complete -o default -F __start_kubectl k

# Vim for YAML
echo -e "set tabstop=2\nset shiftwidth=2\nset expandtab\nset number" > ~/.vimrc

15.4 Time Management Strategy — 120 min, ~60 Qs

3-Pass Time Strategy
  PASS 1 (0-35 min): Quick Wins — Answer everything you can do in under 3 minutes
  ─────────────────
  • Multiple choice questions
  • Simple command-line tasks (helm list, helm install, helm search)
  • Flag the rest for Pass 2

  PASS 2 (35-90 min): Medium Tasks — The bulk of your score
  ─────────────────
  • Template writing/editing
  • Chart structure questions
  • Value configuration tasks

  PASS 3 (90-120 min): Hard Tasks + Review
  ─────────────────
  • Complex multi-step scenarios
  • Review flagged questions
  • Verify answers if time permits

  🔑 NEVER spend more than 5 minutes on a single question.
     Flag it, move on, come back in Pass 3.

15.5 Imperative Commands — Speed Shortcuts

Fast Commands for the Exam
# Chart management
helm create mychart              # Scaffold chart (ALWAYS use this!)
helm lint ./chart                # Validate before deploying
helm package ./chart             # Create .tgz

# Quick installs
helm install test ./chart --dry-run --debug  # Simulate first
helm install app ./chart -n ns --create-namespace --atomic --wait

# Quick values
helm show values bitnami/nginx   # See all configurable values
helm get values myrelease --all  # See what's actually deployed

# History & rollback
helm history app                  # Check revisions
helm rollback app                 # Quick rollback to previous

What is the first thing you should do when the exam terminal opens?

Explanation

The first 5 minutes of setup can save 10+ minutes during the exam. Aliases save ~2s per command. Autocompletion prevents flag typos. Vim YAML settings prevent indentation errors. Reading all questions first lets you apply the 3-pass strategy effectively.

Q4
What command should you use for EVERY deployment on the exam?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm upgrade --install <name> ./chart -f values.yaml -n <ns> --create-namespace --atomic --wait --timeout 5m. Idempotent (install or upgrade), atomic (auto-rollback), wait (verify readiness). This single command pattern handles 90% of exam deploy tasks.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

The exam asks you to "deploy a chart named webapp to the prod namespace." You run the install but forget --namespace. What happens?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

0 points for that question. The exam scores exact requirements. Wrong namespace = wrong answer, even if the chart deploys perfectly. Always verify with kubectl config view --minify | grep namespace before deploying.

Explanation

On the exam, namespace accuracy is critical. Wrong namespace = 0 points. Always read questions carefully for namespace requirements. Use -n <ns> explicitly. Verify with kubectl config view --minify.

Q8
Why should you ALWAYS run --dry-run before helm install on the exam?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

--dry-run --debug validates API versions, catches template errors, and shows exactly what would be deployed — without creating anything. A failed install wastes time on cleanup. Dry-run catches 80% of errors in 2 seconds.

Explanation

helm install creates a new release tracked in Kubernetes Secrets. Key flags: --atomic (auto-rollback), --wait (wait for readiness), --dry-run (validate first). Always dry-run before deploying on the exam.

Q9
How should you handle secrets (passwords, API keys) during the exam?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Use --set to inject at deploy time: helm install app ./chart --set db.password=exam123. Never hardcode secrets in values.yaml. For Secret templates, use {{ .Values.secret | b64enc }} to encode at render time.

Explanation

Never store plaintext secrets in values.yaml. Use placeholders, inject at deploy time (--set or CI/CD), encode with b64enc in templates. Production: Sealed Secrets, External Secrets Operator, or Vault.

Q10
With 2 minutes left, what should you verify before submitting?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

1. helm list -A — verify all expected releases are deployed. 2. Check namespace requirements on your 3 hardest questions. 3. Ensure no release is in "failed" state. 4. Submit — don't second-guess yourself in the final seconds.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

15.8 Top 10 Mistakes That Cost Points

1️⃣
Not using --dry-run first

Always simulate before deploying. helm install --dry-run catches API errors without polluting the cluster.

2️⃣
Forgetting --namespace

Wrong namespace = 0 points. Read the question carefully for namespace requirements.

3️⃣
Not using helm create

Writing chart structure from scratch wastes time. Always scaffold with helm create.

4️⃣
Skipping helm lint

Deploying without linting is gambling. helm lint takes 1 second and catches syntax errors.

5️⃣
Nil pointer panics in templates

Accessing undefined values crashes templates. Always use default: {{ .Values.x | default "safe" }}.

6️⃣
Wrong value precedence

--set beats -f files. Multiple -f: last wins. Know the precedence chain cold.

7️⃣
Not using helm show values

Need to know what values a chart accepts? helm show values chart prints them all with comments.

8️⃣
Forgetting --atomic on upgrades

Failed upgrade without --atomic leaves release in failed state. Always use --atomic for safety.

9️⃣
Confusing version vs appVersion

version = chart version (templates). appVersion = application version (image). They're different.

🔟
Spending too long on one question

5+ minutes stuck? Flag and move on. Points per minute is the real metric.

⏱️120 min~60 questions
🎯~70%Passing score

  2. Add labels with {{ .Release.Name }} and {{ .Chart.Name }}

  4. Create a Secret with base64-encoded values using b64enc
  5. Template render: helm template test ./chart --debug
✅ Template Snippet
apiVersion: v1
kind: ConfigMap
data:
  debug: {{ .Values.debug | default false | quote }}
  logLevel: {{ .Values.logLevel | default "info" | quote }}

Q1
You created a chart with helm create myapp but the deployment uses nginx. How do you change it to deploy your custom app?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Edit values.yaml: change image.repository to your app and image.tag to your version. Edit templates/deployment.yaml: change container port, add env vars, update health check paths. The default nginx chart is a template — customize it for your app.

Explanation

helm create scaffolds a complete chart with Chart.yaml, values.yaml, templates/, and helpers. On the exam, always use this — never start from an empty directory. Customize the generated files. Saves 5-10 minutes per chart task.

Q2
After helm install, pods show CrashLoopBackOff. What's your debugging sequence?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

1. kubectl logs deploy/<name> -n <ns> — see application errors. 2. kubectl describe pod <name> — check events for image pull errors, OOM kills. 3. helm get values <release> --all — verify configuration. 4. Check if dependencies (DB, Redis) are running and reachable.

Explanation

helm install creates a new release tracked in Kubernetes Secrets. Key flags: --atomic (auto-rollback), --wait (wait for readiness), --dry-run (validate first). Always dry-run before deploying on the exam.

Q3
You need to add PostgreSQL and Redis as dependencies. What are the exact steps?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

1. Add to Chart.yaml: dependencies: [{name: postgresql, version: "12.x.x", repository: https://charts.bitnami.com/bitnami}, {name: redis, version: "~18.0.0", repository: https://charts.bitnami.com/bitnami}]. 2. Run helm dependency update. 3. Reference in templates via {{ .Release.Name }}-postgresql for service discovery.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q4
How do you create a database migration hook that runs before every upgrade?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Create a Job template with annotations: "helm.sh/hook": pre-upgrade, "helm.sh/hook-weight": "5", "helm.sh/hook-delete-policy": hook-succeeded. The Job runs python manage.py migrate --noinput using the same image. Deploy with helm upgrade --install — the hook runs automatically.

Explanation

Hooks run at lifecycle events via "helm.sh/hook" annotation. Types: pre/post-install/upgrade/delete/rollback, test. Synchronous — block the release. Use weights for ordering and delete policies for cleanup.

Q5
Your chart has a ConfigMap template. How do you verify it renders correctly before deploying?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

1. helm lint ./chart --strict — syntax check. 2. helm template test ./chart -f values.yaml --debug — local render. 3. Check output: grep for ConfigMap, verify values are substituted correctly. 4. helm install test ./chart --dry-run --debug — cluster validation.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q6
What command packages a chart for distribution and where does the output go?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm package ./chart creates a chartname-version.tgz file in the current directory. Push to OCI: helm push chart-0.1.0.tgz oci://registry.example.com/charts. The .tgz contains all chart files except those in .helmignore.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q7
You deployed an app but forgot to enable ingress. The chart has an ingress template controlled by ingress.enabled. How do you fix this?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm upgrade <release> ./chart --set ingress.enabled=true -n <ns> --atomic --wait. The conditional template {{ if .Values.ingress.enabled }} will now render the Ingress resource. No need to reinstall — upgrade adds the missing resource.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q8
During Lab 5's simulation, you upgrade to replicaCount=5 but the pods don't scale. What's likely wrong?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Check: 1. helm get values <release> --all | grep replicaCount — did the value actually change? 2. kubectl get deploy <name> -o yaml | grep replicas — is the deployment updated? 3. Resource quota might be limiting pod count. 4. HPA might be overriding replicas if autoscaling is enabled.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q9
How do you test that a deployed chart is actually working correctly?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

1. helm test <release> -n <ns> --logs — runs test hook pods. 2. helm status <release> --show-resources — check all resources healthy. 3. kubectl port-forward svc/<name> 8080:<port> then curl localhost:8080/health/. 4. Check pod logs for startup errors.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q10
After completing all Lab 5 tasks, what's the cleanup sequence?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

1. helm list -n exam — verify the release. 2. helm uninstall exam-app -n exam — remove the release. 3. kubectl delete ns exam — cleanup namespace. 4. helm list -A — confirm no leftover releases. Always cleanup in labs — it's good practice and may be required by the exam.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

16.5 Lab 5: Full Helm Certification Simulation (60 min) — 10 Tasks

⏱️ 60 minutes
🎯 Full Exam Simulation — 10 Tasks
  1. Create a chart: Scaffold a chart named "exam-app" with helm create. Set version 1.0.0.
  2. Configure values: Set replicaCount: 3, image: nginx:1.25, service.port: 8080.
  3. Add ConfigMap: Template a ConfigMap with app.env=production and app.debug=false.

  6. Install release: Deploy to "exam" namespace with --atomic --wait.
  7. Upgrade release: Change replicaCount to 5 and upgrade.
  8. Rollback: Rollback to revision 1 and verify replicaCount is 3 again.
  9. Add dependency: Add Redis as a dependency, update, and verify.
  10. Test & cleanup: Run helm test, then uninstall the release.

17.1 Helm Fundamentals (20 Questions)

Q1
What is the primary purpose of Helm?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Package manager for Kubernetes. Packages YAML manifests into reusable, versioned, configurable Charts.

Explanation

Just as apt manages .deb packages, Helm manages Kubernetes resource bundles. One helm install deploys an entire application stack.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q2
What are the three core Helm concepts?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Chart (package), Release (running instance), Repository (storage).

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q3
What was removed in Helm 3 and why?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Tiller — the server-side component. Removed for security: Tiller ran with cluster-admin and anyone with network access could deploy anything.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q4
Where does Helm 3 store release information?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

In Kubernetes Secrets (default) within the release namespace. Named sh.helm.release.v1.<name>.v<rev>.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q5
What command lists all releases across all namespaces?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm list --all-namespaces or helm list -A.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

17.2 Chart Structure & Templates (20 Questions)

Q1
What command scaffolds a new chart?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm create <chart-name>. Creates Chart.yaml, values.yaml, templates/, and helpers.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q2
What are the five Built-in Objects available in templates?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

.Values, .Release, .Chart, .Files, .Capabilities (plus .Template).

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q3
How do you conditionally include a resource?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

{{ if .Values.ingress.enabled }}...{{ end }}. The entire resource block is wrapped in the conditional.

Explanation

The five built-in objects are: .Values (config), .Release (release metadata), .Chart (chart metadata), .Files (static file access), .Capabilities (cluster info). Plus .Template for template info.

Q4
What's the difference between template and include?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

template writes inline (no pipeline). include returns a string (supports | indent, | quote). Always prefer include.

Explanation

Helm uses if for conditionals: {{ if .Values.ingress.enabled }}...{{ end }}. Wrap entire resources. Use eq, ne, lt, gt, and, or, not for comparisons. Resource-level conditionals are standard practice.

Q5
How do you embed a file from the chart into a ConfigMap?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

{{ .Files.Get "config/nginx.conf" | indent 4 }}. Files outside templates/ are accessible via .Files.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

17.3 Values & Configuration (15 Questions)

Q1
What is the value precedence order?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

--set (highest) → -f files (last wins) → parent chart values → values.yaml → subchart defaults (lowest).

Explanation

Value precedence (highest to lowest): --set → -f files (last wins) → parent chart values → values.yaml → subchart defaults. --set always beats everything else.

Q2
How do you override a value during install?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm install app ./chart --set image.tag=v3.0. For nested: --set service.port=8080.

Explanation

Value precedence (highest to lowest): --set → -f files (last wins) → parent chart values → values.yaml → subchart defaults. --set always beats everything else.

Q3
What file provides JSON Schema validation for values?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

values.schema.json. Helm auto-validates user values against this schema at install/upgrade time.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Declarative management of multiple Helm releases. Define all releases in one YAML file, apply with helmfile apply.

Explanation

Chapter 18: Helm Operations at Scale — Running Helm in Production

Running one or two releases is easy. Running 50+ releases across multiple clusters, managing release storage, monitoring for drift, and recovering from disasters — that's operations at scale. This chapter covers performance optimization, storage backends, SDK usage, monitoring, disaster recovery, and multi-cluster Helm strategies.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~2 hours 🏭 Operations 📈 Scale

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Optimize Helm performance for large charts and many releases
  • ✅ Understand release storage backends — Secrets vs ConfigMaps vs SQL
  • ✅ Use the Helm SDK for programmatic chart management
  • ✅ Monitor Helm releases for drift and health
  • ✅ Build disaster recovery plans — backup and restore release state
  • ✅ Manage Helm across multiple Kubernetes clusters

18.1 Helm Performance — Large Charts & Many Releases

As your Helm usage grows, performance becomes critical. A chart with 100+ templates, or managing 200+ releases, requires optimization strategies.

📊
Chart Size Optimization

Problem: Charts with 100+ templates take seconds to render. Solutions: Use library charts for shared templates, split monolithic charts into subcharts, use .helmignore to exclude unnecessary files from packaging, keep templates focused — one resource per template file.

🚀
Release Count Optimization

Problem: helm list slows down with 200+ releases. Solutions: Use --namespace scoping, increase --history-max carefully (each revision stores a full Secret), clean up old failed releases, consider using --keep-history sparingly.

⚡
Template Caching

Helm caches downloaded charts in ~/.cache/helm/. For CI/CD, warm the cache in your pipeline image or use helm dependency build with a pre-populated charts/ directory to skip downloads.

🔧
API Rate Limiting

Large-scale installs can hit K8s API rate limits. Use --wait judiciously (it polls the API), batch releases with Helmfile's --concurrency, and consider increasing K8s API server QPS if you control the cluster.

Performance Commands
# See how many releases and revisions exist
helm list -A | wc -l
kubectl get secrets -A -l owner=helm | wc -l

# Clean up old failed releases
helm list -A --failed | awk 'NR>1{print $1}' | while read r; do
  helm uninstall "$r" -n $(helm list -A | grep "$r" | awk '{print $2}')
done

# Limit release history
helm install myapp ./chart --history-max 5
helm upgrade myapp ./chart --history-max 5

18.2 Release Storage Backends — Secrets vs ConfigMaps vs SQL

Helm 3 stores release history as Kubernetes Secrets by default. But for enterprise-scale deployments, alternative backends offer better performance and manageability.

BackendDefault inProsCons
SecretsHelm 3Encrypted at rest (some K8s distros), native RBAC, no extra infra1MB size limit per Secret, large manifests may exceed it
ConfigMapsHelm 2 (legacy)Simple, easy to inspectPlain text, less secure, same 1MB limit. Not recommended.
SQL (PostgreSQL)Helm 3.3+ via SDKNo size limit, fast queries, backup-friendly, proper indexingExtra infrastructure, requires Helm SDK integration
📖 SQL Storage Driver

Introduced in Helm 3.3+, the SQL storage driver stores releases in PostgreSQL. Benefits: no 1MB Secret limit, efficient release listing with SQL queries, easier backup/recovery. Requires helm.sh/storage-driver: sql configuration. Primarily used via the Helm SDK for programmatic access — not yet a first-class CLI feature.

Storage Backend Decision Flow
  WHICH STORAGE BACKEND SHOULD YOU USE?

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  START: How many releases do you manage?                        │
  │                                                                  │
  │  < 50 releases? ──▶ Secrets (Default)                          │
  │  ✅ Simple, no extra infra, encrypted at rest.                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  50-200 releases? ──▶ Secrets + strict history-max              │
  │  ✅ Still viable. Set --history-max 5. Clean up failed releases. │
  │                                                                  │
  │  200+ releases? ──▶ Consider SQL Driver                          │
  │  ✅ No 1MB limit. Efficient queries. Better backup.              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Manifests > 1MB? ──▶ MUST use SQL Driver                      │
  │  ⚠️ Secrets have a hard 1MB limit. You CANNOT store large       │
  │     manifests in Secrets. Split chart OR use SQL.                │
  │                                                                  │
  │  🔑 For 99% of teams: Secrets are sufficient.                   │
  │     SQL Driver is for Helm-at-scale (platform teams, operators). │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

18.3 Helm SDK — Programmatic Chart Management

The Helm Go SDK lets you embed Helm operations directly into your Go applications — install, upgrade, rollback charts programmatically. Used by tools like Argo CD, Flux, and custom operators.

Helm SDK — Go Example (Conceptual)
import (
    "helm.sh/helm/v3/pkg/action"
    "helm.sh/helm/v3/pkg/chart/loader"
    "helm.sh/helm/v3/pkg/cli"
)

func installChart(name, namespace, chartPath string) error {
    settings := cli.New()
    actionConfig := new(action.Configuration)
    actionConfig.Init(settings.RESTClientGetter(), namespace, "secrets", nil)

    chart, _ := loader.Load(chartPath)
    install := action.NewInstall(actionConfig)
    install.ReleaseName = name
    install.Namespace = namespace
    install.Atomic = true
    install.Wait = true

    _, err := install.Run(chart, map[string]interface{}{})
    return err
}
🔧
Who Uses the SDK?

Argo CD — renders Helm charts for GitOps, Flux CD — Helm controller, Terraform Helm Provider — manages releases via Terraform, Custom Operators — Kubernetes operators that deploy apps via Helm.

💡
Why Use the SDK?

When you need programmatic control: dynamic value injection, custom release naming logic, integration with internal systems, or embedding Helm in a larger platform. Not for day-to-day CLI usage — for building tools that USE Helm.

💡 SDK vs CLI — When to Use Which

CLI = 99% of daily work. SDK = building tools (operators, GitOps controllers, deployment platforms). For the certification, understand the SDK conceptually — what it enables, not its Go API. The exam tests CLI proficiency, not Go programming.

18.4 Monitoring Helm Releases

Once deployed, you need to know if releases are healthy, if drift has occurred, and if any releases are in a failed state.

Helm Release Monitoring Strategy
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  HELM RELEASE MONITORING — THREE LAYERS                            │
  │                                                                     │
  │  LAYER 1: Release Health (Helm-level)                              │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────                              │
  │  helm list -A --failed          ← Find broken releases            │
  │  helm list -A --pending          ← Find stuck operations           │
  │  helm status <release> -n <ns>  ← Check individual health        │
  │                                                                     │
  │  LAYER 2: Resource Health (Kubernetes-level)                       │
  │  ──────────────────────────────────────────────                     │
  │  kubectl get pods -l app.kubernetes.io/instance=<release>          │
  │  kubectl rollout status deploy/<name> -n <ns>                     │
  │                                                                     │
  │  LAYER 3: Application Health (App-level)                           │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────                          │
  │  helm test <release> -n <ns>     ← Run test hooks                 │
  │  Prometheus metrics / Grafana dashboards                            │
  │  Liveness/readiness probe monitoring                                │
  │                                                                     │
  │  🚨 DRIFT DETECTION:                                               │
  │  helm get manifest <release> | kubectl diff -f -                   │
  │  (or use helm-diff plugin: helm diff upgrade <release> ./chart)    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Health Check Commands
# Find all failed releases across the cluster
helm list -A --failed

# Check status with resource details
helm status anihpj-prod -n production --show-resources

# Detect drift — compare live state to Helm's last known state
helm get manifest anihpj-prod -n production | kubectl diff -f -

# Better drift detection with helm-diff plugin
helm plugin install https://github.com/databus/helm-diff
helm diff upgrade anihpj-prod ./anihpj-chart -f values-prod.yaml

18.5 Disaster Recovery — Backup & Restore Release State

Helm stores release state in Secrets. Backing them up is backing up your Helm release history. Without them, Helm doesn't know what it deployed.

1
Identify Release Secrets

kubectl get secrets -A -l owner=helm — These Secrets contain all release metadata, rendered manifests, and values. Each revision is a separate Secret named sh.helm.release.v1.<name>.v<rev>.

2
Backup Secrets

kubectl get secrets -A -l owner=helm -o yaml > helm-releases-backup.yaml. This saves ALL release history. For production, use Velero or etcd snapshots for comprehensive cluster backup that includes Helm Secrets.

3
Restore Secrets

kubectl apply -f helm-releases-backup.yaml — Re-creates the Secrets. After restore, Helm sees releases as "deployed" and can manage them normally. helm list should show all restored releases.

4
Verify Restoration

After restore: helm list -A, helm status <release>, helm get manifest <release>. If resources were also lost, run helm upgrade --install <release> ./chart -f values.yaml to recreate them.

⚠️ Backup Also: Chart Sources + Values Files + Repositories

Release Secrets contain rendered manifests — not chart source code. For complete DR: (1) Back up your chart Git repos, (2) Back up values files, (3) Back up release Secrets, (4) Document repository URLs and credentials. With all four, you can fully reconstruct any deployment from scratch.

DR Recovery Timeline — RPO & RTO for Helm Releases
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  HELM DR — RECOVERY POINT OBJECTIVE (RPO) & RECOVERY TIME (RTO)  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  SCENARIO                    RPO           RTO        TOOL       │
  │  ────────────────────────    ────────      ───────    ─────────  │
  │  Accidental uninstall        Last backup   ~5 min     helm install│
  │  (helm uninstall)            (if kept)                from backup │
  │                                                                    │
  │  Namespace deletion          Last etcd     ~10 min    kubectl     │
  │  (kubectl delete ns)         snapshot                 apply       │
  │                                                                    │
  │  Cluster-wide disaster       Last etcd     ~30 min    Velero or   │
  │  (control plane failure)     snapshot                 etcd restore│
  │                                                                    │
  │  Complete region loss        Last offsite ~2 hours   Velero with │
  │  (cloud region down)         backup                   S3 backup   │
  │                                                                    │
  │  🔑 GOLDEN RULE: Test your restore procedure quarterly.           │
  │     A backup you haven't tested is not a backup — it's a prayer.  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

18.6 Helm in Multi-Cluster Environments

Managing Helm across dev, staging, prod, and DR clusters requires a consistent strategy for kubeconfig management, release naming, and configuration.

Multi-Cluster Helm Strategy
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  MULTI-CLUSTER HELM MANAGEMENT                                     │
  │                                                                     │
  │  ┌─────────────┐   ┌─────────────┐   ┌─────────────┐             │
  │  │ DEV CLUSTER │   │ STG CLUSTER │   │ PROD CLUSTER│             │
  │  │ (kubeconfig │   │ (kubeconfig │   │ (kubeconfig │             │
  │  │  dev.yaml)  │   │  stg.yaml)  │   │  prod.yaml) │             │
  │  └──────┬──────┘   └──────┬──────┘   └──────┬──────┘             │
  │         │                 │                 │                     │
  │         └─────────────────┼─────────────────┘                     │
  │                           │                                       │
  │                    ┌──────▼──────┐                                │
  │                    │  Helm CLI   │                                │
  │                    │ (switch via │                                │
  │                    │  KUBECONFIG │                                │
  │                    │  env var)   │                                │
  │                    └─────────────┘                                │
  │                                                                     │
  │  DEPLOY PATTERNS:                                                  │
  │  KUBECONFIG=dev.yaml  helm install dev  ./chart -f values-dev.yaml │
  │  KUBECONFIG=stg.yaml  helm install stg  ./chart -f values-stg.yaml │
  │  KUBECONFIG=prod.yaml helm install prod ./chart -f values-prod.yaml│
  │                                                                     │
  │  BETTER: Use kubectl contexts:                                     │
  │  kubectl config use-context dev-cluster                            │
  │  kubectl config use-context prod-cluster                           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🔀
Kubeconfig Switching

Use KUBECONFIG env var or kubectl config use-context to switch clusters. Helm uses the same kubeconfig as kubectl. Always verify: kubectl cluster-info BEFORE helm install.

📋
Helmfile for Multi-Cluster

Helmfile supports environments: with different kubeconfigs per environment. One helmfile.yaml can manage all clusters: helmfile -e prod apply.

🔄
Argo CD Multi-Cluster

Argo CD can deploy Helm charts to external clusters. Add external clusters to Argo CD, then specify destination.server in Application CRD. Argo handles kubeconfig and auth.

🏷️
Consistent Naming

Use a naming convention: <app>-<env> (e.g., anihpj-dev, anihpj-prod). This makes helm list -A readable across clusters. Combine with consistent namespace naming.

🎓 Multi-Cluster on the Exam

The exam typically uses a single cluster. However, you may be asked to switch contexts: kubectl config use-context <context-name>. Helm automatically follows the current kubectl context. Always verify: kubectl config current-context before deploying.

anihpj Multi-Cluster Production Architecture
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ANIHPJ MULTI-CLUSTER PRODUCTION SETUP                                    │
  │                                                                           │
  │  ┌─────────────────┐   ┌─────────────────┐   ┌─────────────────┐        │
  │  │ DEV (minikube)  │   │ STAGING (AKS)   │   │ PROD (3×AKS)    │        │
  │  │                 │   │                 │   │                 │        │
  │  │ helm install    │   │ helm install    │   │ helm install    │        │
  │  │ anihpj-dev \    │   │ anihpj-stg \    │   │ anihpj-prod \   │        │
  │  │ ./chart \       │   │ ./chart \       │   │ ./chart \       │        │
  │  │ -f values-dev\  │   │ -f values-stg\  │   │ -f values-prod\ │        │
  │  │ .yaml \         │   │ .yaml \         │   │ .yaml \         │        │
  │  │ -n dev          │   │ -n staging      │   │ -n production   │        │
  │  └────────┬────────┘   └────────┬────────┘   └────────┬────────┘        │
  │           │                     │                     │                  │
  │           └─────────────────────┼─────────────────────┘                  │
  │                                 │                                        │
  │                    ┌────────────▼────────────┐                           │
  │                    │  COMMON CHART REPO      │                           │
  │                    │  (OCI: registry.example │                           │
  │                    │   .com/charts/anihpj)   │                           │
  │                    └─────────────────────────┘                           │
  │                                                                           │
  │  DEPLOYMENT PATTERN:                                                      │
  │  ──────────────────                                                       │
  │  kubectl config use-context dev-cluster                                  │
  │  helm upgrade --install anihpj-dev oci://registry.example.com/charts/    │
  │    anihpj --version 1.2.0 -f values-dev.yaml -n dev --atomic --wait     │
  │                                                                           │
  │  kubectl config use-context prod-cluster-east                            │
  │  helm upgrade --install anihpj-prod oci://registry.example.com/charts/   │
  │    anihpj --version 1.2.0 -f values-prod-east.yaml -n production         │
  │    --atomic --wait --timeout 10m                                          │
  │                                                                           │
  │  🔑 SAME chart, SAME version, DIFFERENT values per cluster.              │
  │     This is the gold standard for multi-cluster Helm at scale.            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Q1
What is the default storage backend for Helm 3 release history?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Kubernetes Secrets (in the release namespace). Helm 2 used ConfigMaps. Secrets are the default because they offer better security (encrypted at rest on many K8s distributions).

Explanation

Each release revision creates one Secret: sh.helm.release.v1.<name>.v<rev>. These Secrets contain the chart, values (including secrets!), and rendered manifests. The 1MB Secret size limit can be a problem for very large manifests — consider the SQL driver for those cases. 

# View release secrets:
kubectl get secrets -l owner=helm -n prod
# NAME                               TYPE     DATA
# sh.helm.release.v1.anihpj.v1     helm.sh/release.v1   1
# sh.helm.release.v1.anihpj.v2     helm.sh/release.v1   1

# Inspect revision:
kubectl get secret sh.helm.release.v1.anihpj.v2 -n prod -o jsonpath='{.data.release}' | base64 -d | gzip -d | jq .
# Output: {"name":"anihpj","chart":{...},"config":{...},"manifest":"---\napiVersion: apps/v1..."}

Explanation

default is the #1 safety function: {{ .Values.x | default "safe" }}. Prevents nil pointer panics — the most common Helm error. Every nullable value reference needs this.

Q2
How do you detect if someone manually modified resources deployed by Helm?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Use helm diff upgrade (helm-diff plugin) or helm get manifest <release> | kubectl diff -f -. This compares Helm's stored manifest with the live state in Kubernetes.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q3
What command backs up all Helm release Secrets?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

kubectl get secrets -A -l owner=helm -o yaml > helm-backup.yaml. The label owner=helm identifies all Helm release Secrets. Restore with kubectl apply -f helm-backup.yaml.

Explanation

Never store plaintext secrets in values.yaml. Use placeholders, inject at deploy time (--set or CI/CD), encode with b64enc in templates. Production: Sealed Secrets, External Secrets Operator, or Vault.

Q4
How do you manage Helm across multiple Kubernetes clusters?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Switch kubeconfig contexts: kubectl config use-context <cluster> or set KUBECONFIG=/path/to/config. Helm always uses the current kubectl context. For automation, use Helmfile with environment definitions or Argo CD for GitOps multi-cluster.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q5
What is the Helm SDK used for?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Programmatic Helm chart management from Go applications. Used by Argo CD, Flux, Terraform Helm Provider, and custom operators. The SDK provides Go APIs for install, upgrade, rollback, and uninstall operations.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q6
What happens if a release Secret exceeds 1MB?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

The install/upgrade fails. Kubernetes Secrets have a 1MB hard limit. Solution: split monolithic charts into smaller subcharts, use the SQL storage driver, or reduce rendered manifest size by removing unnecessary resources.

Explanation

Never store plaintext secrets in values.yaml. Use placeholders, inject at deploy time (--set or CI/CD), encode with b64enc in templates. Production: Sealed Secrets, External Secrets Operator, or Vault.

Q7
How do you find all failed Helm releases across the cluster?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm list --all-namespaces --failed or helm list -A --failed. Also: --pending for stuck operations, --uninstalled with --all for uninstalled releases.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q8
What performance issues arise with 200+ Helm releases?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm list becomes slow (queries all release Secrets). Solutions: namespace scoping, reducing history-max, cleaning up failed releases, using the SQL storage driver for better indexing.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q9
What is the SQL storage driver and when should you use it?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Introduced in Helm 3.3+, stores releases in PostgreSQL instead of Secrets. Use when: (1) manifests exceed 1MB Secret limit, (2) you need efficient listing/queries at scale, (3) you want easier backup/recovery with SQL tools. Requires Helm SDK — not a CLI feature yet.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q10
What's the DR strategy for Helm releases?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

4-part backup: (1) Chart source code (Git), (2) Values files (Git), (3) Release Secrets (kubectl get secrets -A -l owner=helm), (4) Repository config (helm repo list). For comprehensive DR, use Velero or etcd snapshots to back up the entire cluster state.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

⏱️ 25 minutes
🏋️ Hands-On Drill: Scale Operations — Backup, Restore & Multi-Cluster Deploy

Scenario: You're the platform engineer for anihpj. You need to: (1) back up all releases, (2) simulate a disaster recovery, (3) deploy to a second cluster. Complete all tasks.

  1. Backup: Export ALL release secrets: kubectl get secrets -A -l owner=helm -o yaml > anihpj-helm-backup-$(date +%Y%m%d).yaml
  2. Audit: Count releases and revisions: helm list -A | wc -l and kubectl get secrets -A -l owner=helm | wc -l
  3. Simulate DR: helm uninstall anihpj-dev --keep-history then restore: kubectl apply -f anihpj-helm-backup.yaml
  4. Verify: helm list -A — should show anihpj-dev as "uninstalled" with history intact
  5. Find failed releases: helm list -A --failed — clean up any stale failures
  6. Switch context: kubectl config use-context staging-cluster then deploy: helm upgrade --install anihpj-stg ./chart -f values-staging.yaml -n staging --atomic --wait
  7. Verify multi-cluster: kubectl config get-contexts then check helm list -A on each cluster
✅ Complete Solution Script 
#!/bin/bash
set -e
echo "🔍 Auditing current state..."
helm list -A
echo "Releases: $(helm list -A | wc -l)"
echo "Secrets: $(kubectl get secrets -A -l owner=helm | wc -l)"

echo "💾 Backing up all releases..."
kubectl get secrets -A -l owner=helm -o yaml > helm-full-backup.yaml
echo "Backup size: $(du -h helm-full-backup.yaml | cut -f1)"

echo "🧹 Checking for failed releases..."
FAILED=$(helm list -A --failed -o json | jq -r '.[].name')
if [ -n "$FAILED" ]; then
  echo "Found failed releases: $FAILED"
  echo "Consider: helm uninstall  --keep-history"
fi

echo "🌐 Multi-cluster status:"
kubectl config get-contexts
echo "Current: $(kubectl config current-context)"
echo "✅ Operations audit complete!"
⚡PerformanceLarge charts & many releases
💾StorageSecrets / SQL backends
🔧SDKProgrammatic Helm
📊MonitoringHealth & drift detection
🔄DRBackup & restore
🌐Multi-ClusterKubeconfig strategy

Chapter 19: Advanced Chart Patterns — Reusable, Scalable Helm

Beyond basic charts, Helm offers advanced patterns for maximum reusability. Library charts share template code across charts. Starter charts jump-start new projects. Feature toggles conditionally include resources. Post-renderers integrate Kustomize natively. These patterns separate intermediate users from Helm experts.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~2 hours 📐 Patterns 🏗️ Architecture

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Create reusable Library Charts for shared template code
  • ✅ Build Starter Charts for rapid project scaffolding
  • ✅ Design environment-specific chart patterns
  • ✅ Use Feature Toggles for conditional resource inclusion
  • ✅ Integrate Kustomize with Helm via Post-Renderers
  • ✅ Generate chart boilerplate automatically

19.1 Library Charts — Reusable Chart Components

A Library Chart (type: library) is NOT deployable — it only contains named templates that other charts import. Think of it as a "template-only" chart for shared helper functions and resource patterns.

Library Charts — How They Work
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LIBRARY CHART PATTERN                                             │
  │                                                                     │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐                                          │
  │  │ my-library/          │  ← type: library (NOT deployable)       │
  │  │ Chart.yaml           │                                          │
  │  │ templates/           │                                          │
  │  │   _labels.tpl        │  ← Shared label helpers                  │
  │  │   _deployment.tpl    │  ← Standard deployment template          │
  │  │   _service.tpl       │  ← Standard service template             │
  │  └────────┬─────────────┘                                          │
  │           │                                                        │
  │           │ imported by                                            │
  │           ▼                                                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────────┐             │
  │  │ myapp-chart/        │   │ otherapp-chart/      │             │
  │  │ Chart.yaml          │   │ Chart.yaml           │             │
  │  │ dependencies:       │   │ dependencies:        │             │
  │  │ - name: my-library  │   │ - name: my-library   │             │
  │  │   version: 1.0.0    │   │   version: 1.0.0     │             │
  │  │   repository: ...   │   │   repository: ...    │             │
  │  └──────────────────────┘   └──────────────────────┘             │
  │                                                                     │
  │  Both charts use {{ include "my-library.labels" . }}              │
  │  DRY principle: define template logic ONCE, use everywhere.        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Library Chart Example
# my-library/Chart.yaml
apiVersion: v2
name: my-library
type: library         # ← KEY: type must be "library"
version: 1.0.0

# my-library/templates/_standard-labels.tpl
{{- define "my-library.labels" -}}
app.kubernetes.io/name: {{ .Chart.Name }}
app.kubernetes.io/version: {{ .Chart.AppVersion }}
helm.sh/chart: {{ .Chart.Name }}-{{ .Chart.Version }}
app.kubernetes.io/managed-by: {{ .Release.Service }}
{{- end }}

# Usage in any chart that depends on my-library:
labels: {{ include "my-library.labels" . | nindent 4 }}

19.2 Starter Charts — helm create --starter

Starter charts are templates for helm create. Instead of the default nginx chart, you can scaffold from your organization's standardized chart template.

Starter Chart Workflow
# Create a starter chart
mkdir -p ~/.helm/starters/myorg-starter/templates
cat > ~/.helm/starters/myorg-starter/Chart.yaml <<EOF
apiVersion: v2
name: "{{ .Name }}"
version: 0.1.0
type: application
EOF

# Use the starter
helm create myapp --starter=myorg-starter

# The new chart inherits files from the starter template
# Great for enforcing organizational standards!

19.3 Chart Boilerplate Generation

Beyond starters, tools like helmify, chart-builder, and custom scripts auto-generate charts from existing Kubernetes manifests or templates.

Boilerplate Tools
# helmify: Convert existing K8s YAML to Helm chart
kubectl get deploy,svc,cm,secret myapp -o yaml | helmify myapp-chart

# Generate from existing manifests
cat deploy.yaml svc.yaml | helmify generated-chart

19.4 Environment-Specific Patterns

Proven patterns for managing dev/staging/prod differences cleanly — without duplicating charts.

Three Environment Patterns
  PATTERN 1: Multiple Values Files (Simplest — Recommended)
  ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  chart/
    values.yaml          ← Common defaults
    values-dev.yaml      ← Dev overrides (replicaCount: 1)
    values-staging.yaml  ← Staging overrides (replicaCount: 2)
    values-prod.yaml     ← Prod overrides (replicaCount: 5)
  Deploy: helm install app ./chart -f values-<env>.yaml -n <env>

  PATTERN 2: Environment Key in values.yaml
  ─────────────────────────────────────────
  environment: "dev"    # Switch in values.yaml
  Templates use: {{ if eq .Values.environment "prod" }}...

  PATTERN 3: Separate Charts per Environment
  ──────────────────────────────────────────
  Only when environments are RADICALLY different.
  Not recommended — duplicating chart logic is error-prone.

19.5 Feature Toggles & Conditional Resources

Use boolean values to toggle entire resources or features on/off without changing templates.

Feature Toggle Example
# values.yaml
features:
  ingress: true
  autoscaling: true
  monitoring: false       # Toggle the entire ServiceMonitor
  serviceMesh: false     # Toggle Istio sidecar injection

# templates/servicemonitor.yaml
{{ if .Values.features.monitoring }}
apiVersion: monitoring.coreos.com/v1
kind: ServiceMonitor
# ... full monitor spec ...
{{ end }}

# Install with toggles:
helm install app ./chart --set features.monitoring=true

19.6 Post-Renderers — Kustomize + Helm Integration

Post-renderers let you modify Helm's output before it's applied to Kubernetes. The most common use case: running Kustomize on Helm-rendered manifests.

Post-Renderer Pipeline
  ┌──────────┐     ┌────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────┐
  │  Helm    │────▶│  Rendered  │────▶│  Post-       │────▶│  K8s API │
  │  Template│     │  YAML      │     │  Renderer    │     │  Server  │
  │  Engine  │     │            │     │  (Kustomize) │     │          │
  └──────────┘     └────────────┘     └──────────────┘     └──────────┘
                                          │
                                          │ Applies patches,
                                          │ namespace overrides,
                                          │ common labels, etc.

  USAGE:
  helm install myapp ./chart   --post-renderer ./kustomize-wrapper.sh

  # kustomize-wrapper.sh:
  #!/bin/bash
  cat <&0 > /tmp/helm-output.yaml
  kustomize build --enable-helm | kubectl apply -f -
🔀
When to Use Post-Renderers

When you need Kustomize-style patches on Helm output (common labels, namespace overrides, strategic merges) without modifying the chart. Also: injecting sidecars, adding annotations that the chart doesn't support, enforcing organizational policies.

⚠️
Post-Renderer Caveats

Post-renderers break Helm's "single source of truth" model. Changes made by the renderer are invisible to Helm — helm get manifest won't show them. Use sparingly and document clearly. Prefer modifying values.yaml or templates over post-rendering.

Advanced Patterns Decision Guide — Which Pattern When?
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ADVANCED HELM PATTERNS — WHEN TO USE EACH                                │
  │                                                                           │
  │  Pattern              Use When...                    Exam Relevance        │
  │  ───────────────────  ─────────────────────────────  ──────────────────   │
  │  Library Charts       Multiple charts share the      🟢 High — conceptual │
  │                       same template logic (labels,                        │
  │                       deployment specs)                                   │
  │                                                                           │
  │  Starter Charts       Your org has a standard        🟡 Medium — practical │
  │                       chart scaffold everyone                              │
  │                       should use                                          │
  │                                                                           │
  │  Feature Toggles      One chart needs to work        🟢 High — common     │
  │                       in multiple configurations                          │
  │                       (monitoring on/off, etc.)                           │
  │                                                                           │
  │  Post-Renderers       You MUST patch Helm output     🟡 Medium — edge case │
  │                       without modifying the chart                         │
  │                       (org policies, sidecars)                            │
  │                                                                           │
  │  Environment Files    Different config per env       🟢 High — fundamental │
  │                       (dev/staging/prod)                                  │
  │                                                                           │
  │  🔑 MASTER RULE: Start with environment files. Add feature toggles.      │
  │     Use library charts when you hit copy-paste. Post-renderers last.      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Q1
What is a Library Chart and how is it different from an Application Chart?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

A Library Chart (type: library) contains only named templates — it is NOT deployable. Application Charts (type: application) are deployable. Library charts provide reusable template snippets imported by other charts via dependencies.

Explanation

Think of library charts as "template-only modules." They define {{ define "..." }} blocks that consuming charts invoke with {{ include "..." . }}. This enables DRY (Don't Repeat Yourself) for common template patterns like standard labels, deployment specs, or security contexts across multiple charts.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q2
How do you create a chart from a starter template?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

helm create myapp --starter=<starter-name>. Starter charts live in $HELM_HOME/starters/. They're templates for helm create — scaffolding your organization's standard chart structure.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q3
What is a Post-Renderer in Helm?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

A script or binary that modifies Helm's rendered YAML before it's applied to Kubernetes. Used with --post-renderer flag. Common use: running Kustomize on Helm output to add patches, labels, or namespace overrides.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q4
How do you conditionally enable/disable a resource with feature toggles?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Wrap the resource in an if block: {{ if .Values.features.monitoring }}...resource YAML...{{ end }}. Set features.monitoring: true/false in values.yaml to toggle. One boolean controls the entire resource.

Explanation

Helm uses if for conditionals: {{ if .Values.ingress.enabled }}...{{ end }}. Wrap entire resources. Use eq, ne, lt, gt, and, or, not for comparisons. Resource-level conditionals are standard practice.

Q5
What's the recommended pattern for managing dev/staging/prod environments with Helm?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Multiple values files: One chart with values-dev.yaml, values-staging.yaml, values-prod.yaml. Deploy: helm install app ./chart -f values-<env>.yaml -n <env>. This keeps a single source of truth for templates.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q6
What does helmify do?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Converts existing Kubernetes YAML manifests into a Helm chart. Takes plain YAML files as input and generates Chart.yaml, templates/, and values.yaml with parameterized fields extracted from the manifests.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q7
Why would you use a Library Chart instead of duplicating template code?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Library charts enforce DRY (Don't Repeat Yourself). Define shared templates once (labels, deployments, security contexts) and import them across all charts. Fix a bug in the library chart → all consuming charts are updated.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q8
What is a downside of Post-Renderers?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Post-renderer changes are invisible to Helm. helm get manifest shows the pre-rendered output, not what was actually applied. This creates a discrepancy between Helm's stored state and live state — making debugging harder.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q9
How do you generate boilerplate charts from existing K8s resources?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Use helmify: kubectl get deploy,svc,cm myapp -o yaml | helmify myapp-chart. Or write a custom script that extracts values into values.yaml and replaces them with template expressions. Manual approach: helm create + customize.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.

Q10
What's the difference between Starter Charts and Library Charts?
Answer & Explanation
Answer

Starter Charts: Templates for helm create — used at chart creation time to scaffold new charts. Library Charts: Runtime template dependencies — imported at render time by other charts. Starter = scaffolding tool, Library = shared code module.

Explanation

This is a key Helm certification concept. The performance-based exam tests practical application — understanding why is as important as knowing how. Review the related chapter section for deeper context, diagrams, and hands-on practice drills.
